Abstract: Scaling humanoid motion foundation models is constrained by the scarcity of diverse, robot-executable data and by the difficulty of jointly learning from heterogeneous multimodal inputs while supporting real-time closed-loop control. We present Movens-5M, constructed from raw Internet videos through clip generation, quality filtering, semantic annotation, motion recovery, and physical refinement with humanoid retargeting and execution verification. It contains 15 million human-motion sequences and 5 million physically executable humanoid-motion sequences spanning over 10,000 hours, aligned with text, RGB video, speech, and music. Building on this data, we introduce Movens-Zero, a scalable understanding-generation-execution architecture that supports joint learning across modalities and real-time closed-loop execution. Task-mixed flow-matching training jointly optimizes a single action expert across seven generation tasks, enabling direct motion generation from text, speech, music, and video. Multimodal Real-Time Chunking connects overlapping chunks under continuously updated instructions and robot states for continuous closed-loop execution. We further introduce Movens-Intent, a four-track benchmark with 8,192 expert-verified condition-motion pairs across text, speech, music, and video. Experiments show consistent gains across all four modalities when scaling execution-verified data from 1% to 100% and action-expert capacity from 64M to 911M parameters, while demonstrating that data quality and cross-modal supervision improve generation and execution.

### Paper [49]
Title: Online Learning of Heterogeneous Locomotion Dynamics under Reinforcement Learning Policies for Precise Loco-Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41892
Authors: Tianlin Zhang, Weipeng Xia, Lingwei Zhang, Yiyang Shao, Zhongyu Li, Yun-Hui Liu
Abstract: How to reuse a pretrained locomotion policy for precise loco-manipulation is still an open problem. We propose an online method to learn the closed-loop dynamics under a frozen locomotion policy. The learned model predicts floating-base motion and improves loco-manipulation accuracy. However, unobserved latent conditions of the policy introduce heterogeneous responses, meaning that the same observed state will lead to different motions. Direct learning without accounting for this heterogeneity introduces bias and fluctuations into dynamics estimates, degrading floating-base prediction and end-effector tracking. To address this problem, a contaminated Gaussian model is proposed to approximate the effects of latent conditions. The model uses two Gaussian components to approximate predictable and unpredictable responses under the same observed state. Then, the contaminated Gaussian model is combined with a Gaussian process to construct an online learner using random Fourier features (RFFs). The learner estimates each response's probability of belonging to the predictable component and uses it to weight online updates, capturing predictable responses while reducing bias and fluctuations caused by heterogeneous observations. With consistent and unbiased dynamics estimates, model predictive control (MPC) predicts floating-base motion accurately and enables precise loco-manipulation. A finite-sample error bound is established to ensure uniform convergence in online learning. Simulation and hardware experiments demonstrate consistent end-effector tracking across different manipulator configurations and tasks, with errors of 0.01 m.

### Paper [50]
Title: Adaptive Coverage Algorithms for Mobile Ad Hoc Networks in Dynamic Worlds
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41936
Authors: Hugo Gandhi, Edwin Meriaux, Antonio Lor\'ia
Abstract: This paper extends an existing framework for the coordination of mobile agents in unknown worlds, by considering the possibility of dynamic instability. In the original problem, known as the Partially Observable Cooperative Guard Art Gallery Problem (POCGAGP) (arXiv:2512.09495), the multi-agent system must establish a communication network which completely covers an initially unknown constrained space. However, the POCGAGP fails to account for the unpredictability often seen in real-world deployment. To address this, the dynamic case (DPOCGAGP) introduces two settings which can influence the mobile network during deployment: Agent Loss and World Change, which represent dynamic changes to the world. Within this study, we present advancements to two state-of-the-art algorithms, the Centralized Agent-Directed Exploration for Network Coverage (CADENCE), and its decentralized analogue, DADENCE, to provide solutions in these cases. We then compare their performance against another state-of-the-art benchmark algorithm, and show that the adapted algorithms outperform across our benchmark test set, which comprises over 4500 test cases of varied size and structure.

### Paper [51]
Title: Language-Conditioned Behavioral Cloning for Autonomous Endoscope Guidance
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41978
Authors: Ariel Rodriguez, Lorenzo Mazza, Ortrun Hellig, Alejandra Toscano, Rayan Younis, Stefanie Speidel
Abstract: Automated endoscope guidance reduces the need for continuous manual camera control during minimally invasive surgery. However, the desired camera motion depends on the surgeon's immediate intentions and cannot always be inferred from the visual operative scene alone. We propose to automate the task using language-conditioned behavioral cloning; we define 6 clinically relevant guidance behaviors and collect 235 subtask demonstrations. In preliminary phantom experiments, our policy achieves 100% success on focus, overview, and stop, 80% on search-tip and follow-tip behaviors, and 50% on horizon leveling. These results demonstrate the feasibility of learning heterogeneous surgeon-requested camera behaviors with a single visuomotor policy, while the observed failure modes motivate future work on adding temporal context for more demanding guidance tasks.

### Paper [52]
Title: Design and Validation of a Robotic Knee Exoskeleton with Musculoskeletal Simulation-Derived Biological Torque Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41981
Authors: Rajiv V. Joshi, Jimin An, Ilseung Park, Dawit Lee, Inseung Kang
Abstract: Data-driven controllers have enabled lower-limb robotic exoskeletons to adapt assistance to the user across many tasks. A leading example is biological torque control, which estimates the user's own joint moment from onboard sensors and assists in proportion to that estimate. However, its estimators are trained on data recorded with the target device, so deploying the controller on new hardware requires collecting new data. Existing open-source efforts release hardware, software, or controllers separately, so this data collection must still be repeated on the assembled device. Here, we present an open-source knee exoskeleton, built from off-the-shelf components and widely available fabrication tools, with a biological torque controller derived entirely from simulation. A muscle-driven imitation policy reproduces squatting motions from an open biomechanics dataset, and the resulting knee moments are distilled into an estimator that uses only onboard sensors, so no training data are recorded on the device. In seven able-bodied participants, assistance reduced peak biological knee extension moment by 21.5% and peak vastus medialis activation by 26.7% during squatting, relative to squatting without the exoskeleton (p<0.05). Our results demonstrate that a biological torque controller derived from simulation can transfer to physical hardware without device-specific data collection. Because the hardware and training pipeline are released together, research groups can train and compare knee exoskeleton controllers on shared hardware without additional data collection overhead.

### Paper [53]
Title: Suspended-Fabric Robotic Manipulation Surfaces: Modelling, Design Space, and Multimodular Extensio
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41982
Authors: Pratik Ingle, J{\o}rn Lambertsen, Kasper St{\o}y, Andres Fai\~na
Abstract: Robotic Manipulation Surfaces (RMS) reposition objects by deforming the surface on which they rest. A recent class of RMS suspends a continuous fabric sheet from a sparse grid of vertical linear actuators lowering the actuator density of such surfaces but how to design such surfaces so that objects can be controlled everywhere on it, and how to extend it beyond a single module so that objects can pass between modules and achieve parallel, remain open. This paper presents a separable catenary model of the suspended sheet that is fast to compute and provides both the surface shape for given actuator heights and the actuator heights for a desired local slope. The closed form inverse lets a simple PID controller position objects on the sheet, and the model relates fabric length, module span and actuator stroke to the reachable workspace, yielding a minimum fabric length bound and design charts for these surfaces. The approach is then extended to a multimodular platform in which neighbouring modules share actuators. On a $2 \times 2$ prototype, we demonstrate that centimetre-scale objects are transferred between modules and positioned within 0.03 m over a $1 m^2$ workspace with only nine actuators, enabling multi-object manipulation and opening the way to parallel manipulation.

### Paper [54]
Title: Nexus-VLA: Bridging Human and Robot Experiences via a Shared Hierarchical Semantic Latent Space
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42021
Authors: Biaoliang Guan, Tingyu Yuan, Wen Ye, Ziming Ji, Xu Yang, Yan Huang, Jihua Zhu, Chaoyang Zhao
Abstract: Large-scale egocentric human videos provide rich and scalable manipulation experience for robot policy learning, yet substantial embodiment gaps in visual appearance, kinematic structure, and action spaces hinder effective knowledge transfer. Existing methods often decouple cross-embodiment knowledge alignment from robot policy learning, making it difficult for human manipulation knowledge to directly and fully contribute to robot action learning. To address this issue, we propose Nexus-VLA, a hybrid policy learning framework that enables cross-embodiment knowledge transfer through end-to-end human-robot joint training, allowing human manipulation experience to directly contribute to robot policy learning. We first introduce a hierarchical cross-embodiment semantic alignment paradigm that progressively establishes shared manipulation semantics from task intent to fine-grained interactions, enabling more complete use of the manipulation knowledge contained in human videos. Building on this hierarchy, we design a shared manipulation latent representation that jointly encodes multi-level shared knowledge into an internal representation directly used for continuous action generation, improving policy learning efficiency while effectively leveraging human knowledge. Across RoboCasa-GR1, OOD settings, and real-world robot experiments, Nexus-VLA achieves an 80.5% task success rate on RoboCasa-GR1 and demonstrates strong advantages in human-data utilization, learning efficiency, and cross-scenario generalization.

### Paper [55]
Title: Proof-Carrying Evolution: Black-Box Search, White-Box Flight
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42043
Authors: Amit Jain, Sergio Sanchez-Hurtado, Victor Rodriguez-Fernandez, Richard Linares
Abstract: We study whether a language model can help design controllers whose behavior can be formally checked. In proof-carrying evolution (PCE), a search proposes controllers together with their certificates. Their mathematical forms are fixed; a proposer chooses their numerical parameters together. Certificate checks guide selection, and only a certified candidate can be returned. The deployed artifact is a control law and its certificate; the language model is used only during design. We compare four language models with classical search and synthesis on docking under a speed limit, pursuit of a maneuvering target, and attitude control on SO(3), using paired runs and shared proposal allowances. Certificates are checked in exact rational arithmetic during search on the linear problems. On SO(3), the search uses floating-point checks, followed by exact re-verification of every reported champion. The best run of each method also receives Lean 4 proofs of its stated control properties. On the linear problems, the strongest models beat the standard classical baselines on every run, but a classical optimizer that chooses certificates for the same objective closes most of that lead. Among the cited pursuit champions, the ranking does not survive a change from Euler to zero-order-hold dynamics. On SO(3), no model detectably outperforms CMA-ES when mixed with classical mutation. When models write every proposal, one tested model improves on CMA-ES and the other performs worse than its mixed variant. All search medians remain below the hand-tuned law. In a docking control with the objective and mutation operator held fixed, retaining certified candidates yields new certified designs in every run; removing this rule yields none.

### Paper [56]
Title: A Hybrid Path Planning Framework for Assistive Wheelchair Navigation in Dynamic Pedestrian Environments
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42049
Authors: Damla Leblebicio\u{g}lu, Rachel Barume, Ta\c{s}k{\i}n Pad{\i}r
Abstract: Autonomous wheelchairs can improve the quality of life for individuals with mobility impairments by providing greater independence. However, reliable navigation especially in human environments is a major challenge due to factors such as dynamic obstacles, unpredictable pedestrian behavior, and the users' need to complete multiple stops along the way before reaching their final destination. This paper presents a hybrid path planning framework for multi-goal navigation in dynamic environments. The framework integrates the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) for global task ordering with the Potential Field (PF) method and heuristic local planners such as Rapidly-exploring Random Tree (RRT) and A-star (A*) to achieve collision-free navigation. Within this hybrid framework, TSP determines the visiting order of intermediate stations, PF transfers global path information, and local planners avoid obstacles while guiding the robot to the stations. The local planners generate feasible paths that allow the robot to follow the global trajectory and reroute to avoid static and dynamic obstacles. For this purpose, two algorithms have been developed: (i) TSP-PF-RRT and (ii) TSP-PF-A*. The performance of the proposed algorithms are evaluated using Monte Carlo simulations in crowded and extra-crowded scenarios that contain heterogeneous dynamic and static obstacles. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed hybrid framework enables efficient multi-goal navigation while maintaining safe operation in dynamic environments with humans, with TSP-PF-RRT achieving fewer or no collisions and shorter travel distances.

### Paper [57]
Title: REA: A Routed Embodied Agent Runtime for Asynchronous Brain-Cerebellum Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42053
Authors: Pengfei Zhou, Yixing Ma, Zhengfeiyang Zhang, Haoyuan Chen, Zhenglin Wan, Hexin Wang, Wangbo Zhao, Yang You
Abstract: Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. Conversely, fast policies like VLAs and whole-body controllers offer more efficient reflexes, but struggle to generalize. While pairing a deliberative brain (foundation models) with a reactive cerebellum (fast policies) seems natural for embodied systems, two obstacles remain: methods are built and scored on incompatible stacks, and their asynchronous coordination is difficult. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. In a separate 100-episode RoboTwin 2.0 study, REA achieves 84% success with VLA policies and 86% with VLA+code, compared with 80% for the strongest single VLA baseline MEDex. These results demonstrate learned and programmatic policies operating within the same runtime. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.

### Paper [58]
Title: ROOF: Learning Humanoid Fall Recovery under Overhead Constraints
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42097
Authors: Yidan Lu, Jiayuan Yu, Zongrui Cai, Jingwen Sun, Jinrui Han, Jing Wang, Ping Luo, Peng Lu
Abstract: A fallen humanoid beneath an overhead obstacle faces a coupled recovery problem. It must create clearance while organizing support for a stable rise, since attempting to stand before making room can further increase obstruction. We introduce a reinforcement learning method with contact-supported geometric escape guidance, which rewards incremental reduction of robot-obstacle overlap without prescribing an escape path. To achieve this, a learned motion prior, together with posture-dependent support and joint-stall costs, is proposed to shape a single policy that learns when to clear the obstacle, how to establish support, and when to complete the rise, all using proprioception alone. In simulation, it recovers in 88.5% of trials beneath plates that can only be lifted, compared with 18.9% without geometric guidance, and in 98.6% of trials beneath free plates with randomized size, mass and placement. On a Unitree G1, the deployed policy recovered in 95 of 150 attempts across seven real-world obstacle scenes, including furniture and multi-object arrangements absent from training. Project page: https://lu-yidan.github.io/ROOF/

### Paper [59]
Title: RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141
Authors: Shengguang Wu, Suning Huang, Cherie Ho, Ria Doshi, Hao Zhu, Serena Yeung-Levy
Abstract: Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.

### Paper [60]
Title: Hierarchical World Models with Implicit Dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42146
Authors: Gaoyue Zhou, Yvonne Wu, Zichen Jeff Cui, Nicolas Ballas, Mido Assran, Lerrel Pinto, Yann LeCun
Abstract: Planning with world models enables test-time adaptation for embodied agents, offering superior generalization over direct policy learning. However, long-horizon tasks remain hindered by cumulative errors and search complexity. Existing hierarchical methods often use rigid macro-actions or latent skills, leading to brittle representations and limited fine-grained control. We introduce ID-WM (Implicit Dynamics World Models), a hierarchical world model that decouples state-space reachability from local control. Our framework utilizes a high-level model to sample feasible transitions across the state manifold and a low-level world model as an inverse dynamics solver to ground these transitions into precise actions. This allows the planner to identify reachable subgoals without being constrained by low-level execution details. By prioritizing local physical feasibility over global behavioral patterns, ID-WM synthesizes novel long-horizon plans from short segments, transcending the specific behavioral modes seen during training. We demonstrate its effectiveness across three navigation and manipulation suites, achieving a 38.5% improvement over state-of-the-art policy and diffusion-based planners. Crucially, by leveraging this hierarchical structure to mitigate compounding errors, our approach yields a 135% performance gain over single-level world model MPC, particularly in complex, unseen configurations for long-horizon tasks. Videos can be viewed at https://id-wm.github.io

### Paper [61]
Title: How a group that cannot communicate knows itself by its enemy
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40835
Authors: Gregory Falco
Abstract: Teams that cannot communicate seem stuck with what each member sees. We show that their opponent can tell them the rest. Silent hunters, jammed robots and traders who never meet all face an adversary that reacts to the group as a whole; every member can read that reaction, a tell, and learn what no member knows alone. One tell can do the work of a message from every member. The adversary would rather hide, but hiding interferes with its own task, and a team that commits in advance to how it will respond decides how much hiding is worth. A committed team never loses when its opponent cares more about its own goals, and does as well as a fully informed team exactly when the opponent's stake outweighs any gain from lying. Below that point the team learns more by trusting less: in a solved Gaussian model it obtains the truth by promising not to use all of it and keeps the cost the opponent would have paid to conceal; an exact test decides when this holds in finite games. How a team spreads its trust matters too. A lone uninformed member with a full-support prior needs a private coin, but with additive squared-error losses two interchangeable members who respond differently replace it, and a small discount on trust insures against misjudging the enemy. Information, on this view, is not only gathered or aggregated; it is produced by how a team commits to respond.

### Paper [62]
Title: AquaProbe: Efficient Adaptation of Frozen Vision Models for Underwater Images
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40900
Authors: Md Shihabul Islam Shovo, Nishi Kanta Paul, Trung Dong, Xiaomin Lin
Abstract: Reliable visual perception is essential for safe autonomous underwater exploration. Yet vision models pretrained on terrestrial images become less reliable underwater, where turbidity, backscatter, and wavelength-dependent attenuation alter scene appearance. Existing adaptation methods either spread adapters equally across the encoder depth or select layers using the divergence between two fixed domains. In this paper, we introduce AquaProbe for measuring how the location of the domain gap changes with water type and imaging range. AquaProbe separates newly introduced divergence from that accumulated through a frozen vision encoder using paired clean and physically rendered images and allocates a fixed LoRA budget according to the measured profile. The segmentation mean Intersection-over-Union (mIoU) for the proportional LoRA rank allocation, based on the measured profile, is 70.1; this is a better result than uniform LoRA rank allocation by 1.9 mIoU points while having the same parameter budget of 0.59 million adapter parameters. The three-way router, conditioned on estimated condition clusters, achieves 70.6 mIoU on the standard SUIM split and 63.4 AUDRC under held-out degradation.

### Paper [63]
Title: Robot Navigation and Canine Welfare: A Pilot Study Surfacing Challenges for Cross-Species Legibility and Proxy Validity in Animal-Machine Interaction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40921
Authors: Haoxiang Zhang, Nancy Smith
Abstract: Autonomous delivery robots increasingly share residential spaces with companion animals, yet navigation behavior is rarely designed with animal welfare in mind. This paper presents a pilot study evaluating a curve-out maneuver, a laterally arcing pass-by trajectory triggered at the dog's alert distance, proposed as a candidate welfare-informed navigation intervention grounded in canine social communication research and legible motion theory. Following a research through design approach, we developed and evaluated a first design iteration of the artifact in a naturalistic home environment with a companion dog. Contrary to our hypothesis, the curve-out produced a more acute stress response than the straight-line baseline. We argue that discontinuous execution limitation, the robot stopping and reorienting multiple times to approximate the arc, undermined legibility, which in itself became a stressor. The pilot surfaces the legibility of robot behavior to non-humans' eyes and proxy stimulus validity as structural challenges for AMI and repositions the design problem for future work.

### Paper [64]
Title: CuPIQP: A Batched and Differentiable QP Solver on GPUs for Robot Learning and Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41021
Authors: Fenglong Song, Roland Schwan, Yuwen Chen, Yuning Jiang, Colin N. Jones
Abstract: Quadratic programs (QPs) are fundamental building blocks of many robotic tasks such as inverse kinematics, whole-body control, and model predictive control. Extending these methods to large-scale robot learning and control requires solving many QPs concurrently and, for end-to-end learning, differentiating through their solutions. Efficient batched optimization is therefore essential to bringing established model-based methods into GPU-based robotics pipelines. We present \textsc{cuPIQP}, a batched and differentiable QP solver on GPUs based on a proximal interior-point method, with unified support for dense, sparse, and multistage problems. The solver exploits parallelism across independent QPs and their internal structure while preserving per-problem numerical updates and termination decisions. Benchmarks with up to 16384 QPs per batch demonstrate solve-time speedups of up to $18.8\times$ over \textsc{Moreau} and $4.26\times$ over eight-core \textsc{PIQP}. In an end-to-end learning task, \textsc{cuPIQP} accelerates training by $4.6\times$ over \textsc{QPAX} and $7.8\times$ over \textsc{Moreau}. By reducing the computational cost, \textsc{cuPIQP} provides efficient, reusable infrastructure for integrating model-based optimization into large-scale robot learning and control.

### Paper [65]
Title: Consensus Within, Competition Between: Decentralized LLM Teams for Perimeter Defense
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41044
Authors: Khawaja Abaid Ullah, Ananya Acharya, Mohammad Javad Khojasteh
Abstract: The use of large language models (LLMs) to coordinate multi-agent teams raises a fundamental architectural question: whether task allocation should be centralized in a single LLM controller or distributed across autonomous LLM agents, particularly when communication is unreliable. In a deliberately simplified perimeter-defense simulation against LLM-controlled intruders, we compared a central commander, an auction-based consensus team and independent agents under five network conditions. Breach rates were 15% for the consensus team, 32% for the commander and 29% for independent agents, and of the different network conditions, only limited radio range changed a team's breach rate, raising the commander's. In consensus team, giving a low-cost LLM more authority over the bid computation raised breaches by 38 percentage points. With birds and civilian drones in airspace, we added a photograph for each to the input text state. Only the near-frontier model treated them apart from intruders. Intruders disguised as existing taxidermy bird drones were mistook as birds by both low-cost and near-frontier LLMs, going unpursued in 59-61% of near-frontier decisions (1-16% before). However, a one-sentence forewarning in the prompt lowered it to 0-25%. We find that LLM team with a dedicated external conflict resolution protocol results in fewest breaches. Code: https://github.com/the-kas-lab/consensus-within-competition-between.

### Paper [66]
Title: MotorSense: A Video-EMG Dataset of Motoric Representations for Action Understanding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41127
Authors: Eadom Dessalene, Michael Maynord, Amir-Hossein Shahidzadeh, Botao He, Yasamin Tabatabaee, Ruoshi Liu, Ruohan Gao, Paul Pu Liang, Yiannis Aloimonos
Abstract: Computer vision models learn hand action only from its visual consequences, without access to the underlying embodied motor signals. We address this absence with MotorSense, the first large-scale paired egocentric video and surface electromyography (EMG) dataset. MotorSense pairs bimanual EMG bands with egocentric video and yields synchronized video, bimanual EMG and IMU, and audio across 100 hours of unscripted manipulation. EMG offers a powerful scalable alternative to manual annotation: it captures finger movement, hand configuration, and the forces exerted between hand and environment as a dense signal free of occlusion and labeler bias, whereas manual annotation is costly, subjective, and unreliable under occlusion. We use MotorSense to learn sensorimotor alignment, producing an organization of action that is shared across the visual and motoric spaces, through cross-modal pre-training between video and EMG. The resultant vision models are then transferred to downstream tasks that benefit from sensorimotor alignment: in this case, action recognition, 3D hand reconstruction, and 3D contact reconstruction. We further demonstrate a generative application in which EMG motor signals are used to condition a video diffusion model, yielding videos of fine-grained hand manipulation. Across four action-centric computer vision tasks and eight popular video datasets, we demonstrate that sensorimotor alignment provides a stronger foundation for visual representation learning than conventional self-supervised learning methods over video, outperforming every baseline across all tasks and datasets evaluated.

### Paper [67]
Title: SACQ: Structure-Aware Codebook Quantization for Ultra-Low-Bit Vision-Language-Action Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41408
Authors: Haesung Lee, Insu Han
Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models enable capable robot behavior, but their large memory footprint limits deployment on resource-constrained robotic platforms. Weight quantization is a natural remedy, yet post-training quantization (PTQ) recipes inherited from large language models degrade when applied to VLAs at ultra-low precision. Existing low-bit PTQ methods often fail to explicitly account for the structure of downstream operations and allocate precision without considering its effect on the policy actions. We introduce SACQ, an ultra- low-bit weight-only vector-codebook quantization framework for VLAs. SACQ combines (1) structure-aware codebook fitting with role-specific quadratic metrics that reflect input statistics and downstream sensitivity; (2) action-aware mixed-bit allocation based on the increase in final-action error as individual layers are compressed; and (3) two-stage post-quantization refinement that reassigns codeword indices with second-order error compensation and calibrates codebooks and row-group scales against the decoded action. Using GR00T N1.7, SACQ achieves 93.85% average success across four LIBERO suites at 1.5 bits per language/action weight while reducing model memory by 4.83x. With INT8 activation quantization and low-precision attention, it retains 93.55% success while running 1.51x faster than BF16. On SimplER, SACQ further achieves 63.76% and 52.27% success under Visual Matching and Variant Aggregation at 2 bits per language/action weight, compared to 58.90% and 53.45% for BF16, respectively.

### Paper [68]
Title: FOCUS: First-Order Control Using Optimal Transport Supervision for Imitation from Observations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41540
Authors: Jo\~ao A. C\^andido Ramos, Lionel Blond\'e, Hugues Vinzant, Jacopo Castellini, Alexandros Kalousis
Abstract: Imitation learning from observations must recover behavior from state-only demonstrations without known temporal correspondence. Differentiable simulators provide pathwise policy gradients, but differentiating through long, contact-rich trajectories can make optimization unstable. We introduce FOCUS, which combines local optimal-transport alignment with short-horizon differentiable actor-critic learning. FOCUS matches agent rollout windows to multiple sampled expert segments using a soft best-of-$K$ objective that emphasizes locally compatible matches. The resulting transport-based costs provide differentiable imitation rewards, while a learned critic bootstraps value beyond the rollout horizon. FOCUS requires neither temporally aligned trajectories nor expert-aligned initialization or teacher forcing. Across contact-rich locomotion tasks in DFlex, including a musculoskeletal humanoid controlled by 152 muscle activations, FOCUS improves sample efficiency and training stability over adversarial imitation-from-observation methods and prior differentiable imitation baselines.

### Paper [69]
Title: DrawSplat: Atlanta World-Guided Implicit Gaussian SLAM for Indoor Reconstruction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41602
Authors: Fan Zhu, Zhisong Xu, Ziyu Chen, Zhenjun Zhao, Takayuki Okatani
Abstract: RGB-D SLAM requires accurate online tracking, high-quality rendering, and geometrically consistent reconstruction. Existing Gaussian SLAM methods mainly optimize Gaussian primitives with photometric, depth, or local frame-to-model constraints, which are often insufficient in indoor scenes with weak textures, large planar structures, noisy depth, and long trajectories. The resulting maps may suffer from pose drift, surface thickness artifacts, noisy normals, and blurred structural boundaries. We propose DrawSplat, an Atlanta World-guided implicit Gaussian SLAM framework for indoor reconstruction. DrawSplat follows a drawing-like process: explicit point-line geometry first sketches the structural layout, and implicit neural Gaussians then fill in high-fidelity appearance and continuous geometric details. It estimates camera poses, sparse geometry, and gravity direction through point-line joint tracking, infers structural planes from tracked lines using the shared-gravity component of the Atlanta World prior, and converts them into differentiable regularization terms on Gaussian centers and normals. To improve compactness and efficiency, DrawSplat further combines an anchor-based implicit neural Gaussian representation with adaptive structural sampling, which allocates Gaussian capacity to structurally informative regions. Experiments on multiple datasets show that DrawSplat achieves robust tracking, high-fidelity rendering, and strong geometric reconstruction among Gaussian-based SLAM systems, while maintaining a practical balance among quality, efficiency, and model size.

### Paper [70]
Title: PGGT-Loc: Primitive-Grounded Geometry Transformer for Feed-Forward Camera Localization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41833
Authors: Hanqiao Ye, Yuzhou Liu, Mengqi Rong, Jian Liu, Yangdong Liu, Shuhan Shen
Abstract: Camera localization methods are mostly multi-stage pipelines relying on dedicated and complex maps that are costly to construct, store, and maintain over time. Alternatively, well-structured maps that organize scenes with compact yet expressive primitives are often readily available, providing rich structural and semantic cues that humans can interpret. However, their potential for direct camera localization remains largely unexplored due to the significant modality gap between map primitives and visual observations. This motivates a new concept for localization, which estimates camera poses by grounding query observations to map primitives. In this paper, we instantiate the concept in the context of planar primitives with PGGT, the Primitive-Grounded Geometry Transformer network. Given a single query image and a set of planar map primitives, the network is trained to predict multiple localization targets for feed-forward 6-DoF camera localization that generalizes to unseen environments. With this straightforward and scalable architecture, our method pushes the state-of-the-art localization performance on both dense surface maps and minimal layout models. The code and models are available at https://github.com/3dv-casia/PGGT-Loc

### Paper [71]
Title: Sensorium: An Open-Source Multimodal Data Collection and Curation Library for Embodied Surgical AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41946
Authors: Lorenzo Mazza, Ariel Rodriguez, Stefanie Speidel
Abstract: Embodied surgical AI requires reliable multimodal data, yet surgical data collection remains costly, difficult to repeat, and strongly dependent on the hardware setup. We present Sensorium, an open-source Python library for recording, processing, annotating, and curating multimodal surgical data from configurable robot and sensor streams using the Robot Operating System (ROS) and ROS2 middleware. The library combines operator-facing data acquisition, configurable processing, semantic annotation, and episodic dataset generation. We evaluate Sensorium across 1516 recordings of phantom, ex vivo, and in vivo porcine experiments involving multiple surgical tasks and sensing configurations, and report preliminary usability feedback.

### Paper [72]
Title: CausalWAM: Learning the Causes of Actions in World Action Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42096
Authors: Juntao Cheng, Jinzhou Tang, Fan Feng, Henrik I. Christensen, Yujia Zheng
Abstract: World action models (WAMs) learn latent variables from observations and use these to both predict the evolution of the scene and to generate actions. The prediction has to be of the whole scene, whereas only a portion of the scene causes actions. WAMs fitted to demonstrations do not, in general, guarantee that they will generate actions solely from the latent causes of the demonstrated actions (the action-relevant latent variables), as the model could just as well learn to imitate in this way whilst entangling the action-relevant latent variables with the nuisance variables. We therefore separate out what causes action from what prediction depends on. We prove that under a sparsity constraint the latent variables used to generate actions are an invertible function of the action-relevant latent variables alone and the true structure is identified up to a permutation of the latent variables. This requires neither sufficient temporal dependence nor any assumptions on how the unobserved regimes evolve and holds even when the action-relevant latent variables are statistically dependent on the rest. We therefore propose CWAM (Causal WAM), which incorporates the constraint into a pretrained WAM by adding a sparse routing path to its action head, with the world modeling objective remaining unchanged. Experiments on both synthetic data and real-world robots validate the theory as well as showcase the improved efficiency in closed-loop success.

### Paper [73]
Title: EquiContact: A Hierarchical SE(3) Vision-to-Force Equivariant Framework for Spatially Generalizable Contact-rich Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10961
Authors: Joohwan Seo, Arvind Kruthiventy, Soomi Lee, Megan Teng, Seoyeon Choi, Xiang Zhang, Jongeun Choi, Roberto Horowitz
Abstract: This paper presents a framework for learning vision-based robotic policies for contact-rich manipulation tasks, which generalize spatially across task configurations.
  We propose EquiContact, a novel hierarchical framework composed of a high-level vision planner, such as a Diffusion Equivariant Descriptor Field (Diff-EDF) and a low-level compliant visuomotor policy, such as a Geometric Compliant Action Chunking Transformer (G-CompACT). G-CompACT, operating using localized observations from wrist-mounted RGB images, end-effector pose feedback in the form of a geometrically consistent error vector (GCEV) and force-torque readings, produces actions defined on the end-effector frame.
  We show that the entire EquiContact pipeline is $\SE$-equivariant under equivariant reference-frame estimation and left-invariant visual features, from perception to force control. Once the robot learns to execute a contact-rich task, such as inserting a peg-in-hole (PiH), it can execute similar tasks at different locations and orientations without retraining of the low-level policy. We also describe three key components for attaining spatially generalizable contact-rich policies: geometrically consistent compliance, the use of localized policies, and induced equivariance.
  Real-world peg-in-hole (PiH) experiments demonstrate near-perfect success rates under spatially out-of-distribution configurations, validating the proposed framework and principles, while additional screwing and surface-wiping experiments evaluate the transfer of the localized policy design beyond insertion. The experimental videos and more details can be found on the project website: https://equicontact.github.io/EquiContact-website/

### Paper [74]
Title: Reflection-Based Relative Localization for Cooperative UAV Teams Using Active Markers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17166
Authors: Tim Lakemann, Daniel Bonilla Licea, Viktor Walter, Martin Saska
Abstract: Reflections of active markers in the environment are a common source of ambiguity in onboard visual relative localization. This work presents a novel approach that exploits these typically unwanted reflections for onboard relative localization in heterogeneous multi-UAV teams operating over specular, near-planar surfaces such as calm water and flat indoor floors. Using only a camera, active markers, and a single-beam rangefinder, the method requires no prior knowledge of robot size, marker configuration, or surface reflectance properties, and propagates the uncertainty introduced by surface irregularities, including those of dynamic water surfaces. We further characterize, analytically and experimentally, how surface tilt biases the estimate, and identify the slope range over which the level-surface assumption holds. Indoor multi-UAV and outdoor experiments validate the localization accuracy, demonstrating reliable operation across varying lighting conditions and greater effective range than state-of-the-art vision-based active-marker methods, with higher accuracy beyond 30 m. The project website with the video is available at: https://timlakemann.github.io/reflection-based-relative-localization/.

### Paper [75]
Title: WildOS: Open-Vocabulary Object Search in the Wild
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19308
Authors: Hardik Shah, Manthan Patel, Erica Tevere, Deegan Atha, Marcel Kaufmann, Shehryar Khattak, Marco Hutter, Jonas Frey, Patrick Spieler
Abstract: Autonomous navigation in complex, unstructured outdoor environments requires robots to operate over long ranges without prior maps and limited depth sensing. In such settings, relying solely on geometric frontiers for exploration is often insufficient. In such settings, the ability to reason semantically about where to go and what is safe to traverse is crucial for robust, efficient exploration. This work presents WildOS, a unified system for long-range, open-vocabulary object search that combines safe geometric exploration with semantic visual reasoning. WildOS builds a sparse navigation graph to maintain spatial memory, while utilizing a foundation-model-based vision module, ExploRFM, to score frontier nodes of the graph. ExploRFM simultaneously predicts traversability, visual frontiers, and object similarity in image space, enabling real-time, onboard semantic navigation tasks. The resulting vision-scored graph enables the robot to explore semantically meaningful directions while ensuring geometric safety. Furthermore, we introduce a particle-filter-based method for coarse localization of the open-vocabulary target query, that estimates candidate goal positions beyond the robot's immediate depth horizon, enabling effective planning toward distant goals. Extensive closed-loop field experiments across diverse off-road and urban terrains demonstrate that WildOS enables robust navigation, significantly outperforming purely geometric and purely vision-based baselines in both efficiency and autonomy. Our results highlight the potential of vision foundation models to drive open-world robotic behaviors that are both semantically informed and geometrically grounded. Project Page: https://leggedrobotics.github.io/wildos/

### Paper [76]
Title: CHOP: Counterfactual Human Preference Labels Improve Obstacle Avoidance in Visuomotor Navigation Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02004
Authors: Gershom Seneviratne, Jianyu An, Vaibhav Shende, Sahire Ellahy, Yaxita Amin, Kondapi Manasanjani, Samarth Chopra, Jonathan Deepak Kannan, Dinesh Manocha
Abstract: Visuomotor navigation policies have shown strong perception-action coupling for embodied agents, yet they often struggle with safe navigation and dynamic obstacle avoidance in complex real-world environments. We introduce CHOP, a novel approach that leverages Counterfactual Human Preference Labels to align visuomotor navigation policies towards human intuition of safety and obstacle avoidance in navigation. In CHOP, for each visual observation, the robot's executed trajectory is included among a set of counterfactual navigation trajectories: alternative trajectories the robot could have followed under identical conditions. Human annotators provide pairwise preference labels over these trajectories based on anticipated outcomes such as collision risk and path efficiency. These aggregated preferences are then used to fine-tune visuomotor navigation policies, aligning their behavior with human preferences in navigation. Experiments on the SCAND dataset show that visuomotor navigation policies fine-tuned with CHOP reduce near-collision events by 49.7%, decrease deviation from human-preferred trajectories by 45.0%, and increase average obstacle clearance by 19.8% on average across multiple state-of-the-art models, compared to their pretrained baselines. These improvements transfer to real-world deployments on a Ghost Robotics Vision60 quadruped, where CHOP-aligned policies improve average goal success rates by 24.4%, increase minimum obstacle clearance by 6.8%, reduce collision and intervention events by 45.7%, and improve normalized path completion by 38.6% on average across navigation scenarios, compared to their pretrained baselines. Our results highlight the value of counterfactual preference supervision in bridging the gap between large-scale visuomotor policies and human-aligned, safety-aware embodied navigation.

### Paper [77]
Title: $\pi$-StepNFT: Wider Space Needs Finer Steps in Online RL for Flow-based VLAs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02083
Authors: Siting Wang, Xiaofeng Wang, Zheng Zhu, Minnan Pei, Xinyu Cui, Cheng Deng, Jian Zhao, Guan Huang, Haifeng Zhang, Jun Wang
Abstract: Flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for embodied control, but multi-step denoising makes policy-gradient online reinforcement learning (RL) difficult because the final-action likelihood induced by the sampler is not directly tractable. We identify an exploration--supervision mismatch within the denoising trajectory, where stochastic differential equation (SDE) sampling explores a wider local stochastic transition space while supervision derived from the final denoised output does not directly use the adjacent transitions observed during sampling. We propose $\pi$-StepNFT (Step-wise Negative-aware Fine-Tuning), a flow-native online RL framework that couples wider stochastic exploration with finer step-wise supervision without computing the final-action likelihood. $\pi$-StepNFT treats observed SDE transitions as supervision units, using variance-normalized transition errors to rank mirrored positive and negative velocity branches and map rollout feedback to local velocity updates. On LIBERO, starting from shared few-shot SFT initializations, $\pi$-StepNFT raises average success from 57.6\% to 92.0\% for $\pi_0$ and from 77.1\% to 94.0\% for $\pi_{0.5}$. On ManiSkill, it achieves the highest reported mean success among the evaluated RL baselines on five test-only visual perturbations. Our project homepage is available at https://wangst0181.github.io/pi-StepNFT/.

### Paper [78]
Title: FlowTouch: View-Invariant Visuo-Tactile Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08255
Authors: Seongjin Bien, Carlo Kneissl, Tobias J\"ulg, Frank Fundel, Thomas Ressler-Antal, Florian Walter, Bj\"orn Ommer, Gitta Kutyniok, Wolfram Burgard
Abstract: Tactile sensation is essential for contact-rich manipulation tasks. It provides direct feedback on object geometry, surface properties, and interaction forces, enhancing perception and enabling fine-grained control. An inherent limitation of tactile sensors is that readings are available only when an object is touched. This precludes their use during planning and the initial execution phase of a task. Predicting tactile information from visual information can bridge this gap. A common approach is to learn a direct mapping from camera images to the output of vision-based tactile sensors. However, the resulting model will depend strongly on the specific setup and on how well the camera can capture the area where an object is touched. In this work, we introduce FlowTouch, a novel model for view-invariant visuo-tactile prediction. Our key idea is to use an object's local 3D mesh to encode rich information for predicting tactile patterns while abstracting away from scene-dependent details. FlowTouch integrates scene reconstruction and Flow Matching-based models for image generation. Our results show that FlowTouch is able to bridge the sim-to-real gap and generalize to new sensor instances. We further show that the resulting tactile images can be used for downstream grasp stability prediction. Our code, datasets and videos are available at https://flowtouch.github.io/

### Paper [79]
Title: Specification-Aware Distribution Shaping for Robotics Foundation Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17969
Authors: Sad{\i}k Bera Y\"uksel, Derya Aksaray
Abstract: Robotics foundation models have demonstrated strong capabilities in executing natural language instructions across diverse tasks and environments. However, they remain largely data-driven and lack formal guarantees on constraint satisfaction during deployment. In practice, robots often need to comply with operational constraints involving rich spatio-temporal requirements such as time-bounded goal visits, sequential objectives, and persistent safety conditions. In this work, we propose a specification-aware action distribution optimization framework that enforces a broad class of Signal Temporal Logic (STL) constraints during execution of a pretrained robotics foundation model without modifying its parameters. At each decision step, the method computes a minimally modified action distribution that satisfies a hard STL feasibility constraint by reasoning over the remaining horizon using forward dynamics propagation. We validate the proposed framework in simulation using state-of-the-art robotics foundation models across multiple environments and complex specifications.

### Paper [80]
Title: Semantically Adaptive Control Barrier Functions with Vision-Language Models for Real-Time Robot Navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21142
Authors: Jeffrey Chen, Rohan Chandra
Abstract: Robots operating in dynamic, unstructured environments must maintain safety without becoming unnecessarily conservative. While control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a principled mechanism for collision avoidance, their behavior is typically determined from geometric state alone and therefore cannot distinguish between semantically different situations with similar physical configurations. We present AlphaAdj, a vision-to-control navigation framework that uses egocentric RGB observations and a vision-language model (VLM) to adapt CBF conservativeness according to visual semantic context. The VLM produces a scalar contextual caution signal that dynamically modulates the CBF parameter alpha, while a geometry- and speed-dependent cap bounds admissible aggressiveness. To support real-time operation despite VLM latency, inference runs asynchronously with the control loop, and the semantic signal is extracted directly from token-level output probabilities rather than full autoregressive text generation. We evaluate AlphaAdj across matched-geometry, dynamic-human, and cluttered navigation scenarios, as well as on a physical Clearpath Jackal. When obstacle geometry is held fixed, AlphaAdj allocates 0.405 m more clearance to a human than to a benign object, while geometry-only and non-visual adaptive CBF baselines produce no semantic differentiation. This additional behavioral reserve improves robustness to unexpected human motion, including cluttered conditions in which all evaluated geometric baselines collide while AlphaAdj remains collision-free. The system maintains a 0.0167 s control period with 0.158 s mean VLM round-trip latency, demonstrating that semantic visual reasoning can complement geometric safety control without entering the critical control path.

### Paper [81]
Title: TacO: Benchmarking Tactile Sensors for Object Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21976
Authors: Anya Zorin, Zilin Si, Sachin Bhadang, Myungsun Park, Junsung Park, Alexiy Buynitsky, Taejun Park, Sohee John Yoon, Yong-Lae Park, Oliver Kroemer, Zeynep Temel, Michael T. Tolley, Sha Yi, Xiaolong Wang
Abstract: Vision-based learning from demonstrations has achieved remarkable success in enabling robots to perform manipulation tasks and high-level semantic reasoning, yet it remains insufficient for complex, contact-rich manipulation. While there is broad agreement that tactile sensing improves manipulation, there is no empirical guidance on which tactile sensors are best suited for which manipulation tasks. In this paper, we provide a systematic, task-driven evaluation of tactile sensors for robot manipulation and propose a framework for selecting and evaluating sensors based on manipulation policy performance. Separate manipulation policies are trained for tactile sensors of four distinct modalities: visual, acoustic, magnetic, and resistive, across three tasks: pick-and-place with unknown mass, object reorientation, and plug insertion. For each task, we analyze how sensor properties, including spatial resolution, shear sensing, tactile representation, and inherent material friction, affect task performance. Rather than tactile sensing being universally beneficial in the same way, our results show that the usefulness of tactile information depends strongly on sensor modality, material properties, and the specific manipulation tasks. The tactile sensors, code, data, and hardware setup will be publicly available on the project website.

### Paper [82]
Title: NDPP-Grasp: Non-Differentiable Physical Plausibility Constraint-Guided Task-Oriented Dexterous Grasp Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02432
Authors: Qiuchi Xiang, Haoxuan Qu, Hossein Rahmani, Jun Liu
Abstract: Task-oriented dexterous grasp generation aims to produce dexterous grasp poses that are both physically plausible and functionally suitable for specified manipulation tasks. Existing diffusion-based methods often address these two requirements in a decoupled manner: they first train a grasp diffusion model for task alignment and then rely on post-generation refinement to improve physical plausibility. However, this after-the-fact correction strategy applies physical plausibility guidance only once the grasp has already been generated, leaving the generation trajectory itself unguided by physical constraints and potentially leading to suboptimal grasps. To address this problem, we propose a novel framework that directly injects physical plausibility guidance into the denoising process of a task-aligned grasp diffusion model in a practical and effective manner, even when physical plausibility constraints are non-differentiable. This allows physical plausibility to shape grasp generation throughout denoising while preserving task alignment. Extensive experiments demonstrate the efficacy of our framework.

### Paper [83]
Title: Rapid co-design of Buoyancy-assisted robots for Challenging Locomotion using Gaussian Evolutionary Specialists
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07424
Authors: Ankit Sinha, Nitish Sontakke, Dennis Hong, Yusuke Tanaka, Sehoon Ha
Abstract: Designing high-performance legged robots requires jointly optimizing morphology and control. Model-free reinforcement learning (RL) enables learning complex controllers during co-design, but repeatedly training policies across candidate morphologies makes design optimization computationally expensive. Existing approaches can amortize policy learning across morphologies, but a single shared policy tends to converge toward one control strategy regardless of morphology, which we refer to as behavioral diversity collapse. We introduce Gaussian Evolutionary Specialists (GES), an RL-based co-design framework that maintains a population of specialist policies whose design-space territories evolve according to their relative control performance. We evaluate GES on the Buoyancy-Assisted Light Legged Unit (BALLU) robot for obstacle traversal and ramp walking over design spaces of up to four dimensions. GES achieves higher best design performance than Monolithic and Mixture-of-Experts baselines, with gains of 5.0-9.0 cm in obstacle height and 10.0-12.7 points in ramp grade. For the same 200 design-optimization evaluations, GES reduces wall-clock co-design time by 67.4% compared with retraining a policy for every candidate. Finally, teleoperated hardware experiments evaluate the optimized BALLU morphology, which traverses obstacles of up to 24 cm, compared with 8 cm on the original design.

### Paper [84]
Title: VEGA: Learning Navigation VLAs from In-the-Wild Egocentric Video with Geometric Trajectory Supervision
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18426
Authors: Gershom Seneviratne, Yohan Abeysinghe, Jianyu An, Vaibhav Shende, Rahul Kumar, Dinesh Manocha
Abstract: We introduce VEGA, an approach for training navigation VisionLanguage-Action (VLA) models from unlabeled egocentric navigation videos. Internet-scale egocentric videos provide a scalable source of navigation-relevant visual observations, capturing cluttered scenes, close-range obstacles, and natural human motion through real-world spaces. However, these videos are not directly usable for policy learning because they do not provide obstacle-aware trajectories conditioned on explicit navigation goals in the robot's coordinate frame. VEGA addresses this gap by reconstructing local scene geometry from monocular video, sampling navigation goals (represented as text, image, or spatial waypoints) and generating obstacle-aware trajectories using the constructed geometry. The resulting trajectory distribution is then used to train a flow-matching VLA navigation policy. By using geometry exclusively during training, VEGA distills obstacle-aware planning directly into a vision-based policy. Furthermore, we introduce VEGA-Bench, a benchmark containing 250k scenes and approximately 5 million navigation goals paired with scene geometry, designed to evaluate goal progress, collision avoidance, and obstacle clearance of VLAs. Our evaluation shows that VEGA achieves competitive goal progress while reducing collisions by 33.0% and improving obstacle clearance by 17.9% over the strongest baseline on VEGABench, while improving success by at least 150.0%, reducing collisions by at least 66.7%, and improving obstacle clearance by at least 60.0% in real-world trials. Ultimately, we demonstrate that video-derived geometric supervision provides a scalable and effective signal for training obstacle-aware navigation VLAs. The code and benchmark will be released at the time of publication.

### Paper [85]
Title: Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613
Authors: Bryce Grant, Aryeh Rothenberg, Logan Senning, Zonghe Chua, Zach Patterson, Peng Wang
Abstract: Changes to what a robot can see do not necessarily mean that there is a change in the problem it is trying to solve, and many language-conditioned manipulation systems struggle with this distinction. When something in a scene moves, the robot might need to execute its actions in different places, but its manipulation plan can stay the same. We propose Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints (\spark), which describes a task as a symbolic behavior tree that we only anchor to the current 3D observations when each action is called. By verifying the plan at runtime we can recover from failures locally and anchor the objects to the scene again. We ask for a new plan only if this fails. We isolate this concept by taking a fixed plan and moving objects in the scene while executing. When displacing objects by $10$\,cm we were still able to complete $66.7\%$ of trials with scene-diff verification and layered recovery, and we did not have to change the plan. We test our full system on \libpro with these conditions. There is no practice on the tasks being tested and no access to simulator states during rollouts, and as in \rats and \capx a success check after each attempt decides whether to retry. We use one open-weight 27B planner with 4-bit weights. Under this protocol \spark achieves a mean success of $66.8\%$ across the 6 position and task settings and $66.8\%$ on the held-out language and object settings. We also achieve a $68\%$ success rate across 11 physical task-embodiment settings on 3 different robots. Our work demonstrates that for an important class of failures the robot does not need to ask for a new plan for its task. It can be enough to update the system's view of the world. Code and videos can be found at https://cwru-aism.github.io/spark-page/

### Paper [86]
Title: More than a Manipulator: Planning Propellant-Free Attitude Maneuvers for Free-Floating Spacecraft
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12130
Authors: Harsh G. Bhundiya, Avi Soval, Keenan Albee
Abstract: Spacecraft attitude control is traditionally achieved using momentum exchange devices or propellant-consuming thrusters. Meanwhile, a growing number of missions require robotic manipulators, which are typically treated as disturbance sources to be rejected rather than as actuators for spacecraft reorientation. This work investigates the use of manipulator motions for propellant-free attitude control by formulating a trajectory optimization problem with critical joint and collision avoidance constraints. Using an interior-point solver for the resulting nonlinear program, complex slew and detumble trajectories are computed for a range of spacecraft-manipulator systems, demonstrating rest-to-rest slew maneuvers of up to 90$^\circ$ and detumble maneuvers with a 40% reduction in angular velocity. The achievable control authority is also compared directly with that of reaction wheel arrays via momentum and torque envelopes, demonstrating the potential for manipulators to serve as redundant or even primary attitude control systems. This work provides a framework for using manipulators as multipurpose attitude control actuators, with particularly promising applications in in-space assembly and manufacturing when grasping payloads with high relative mass fractions.

### Paper [87]
Title: Decompose and Reorganize: Planning with Primitives and Visuomotor Policies Learned from Demonstrations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25397
Authors: Yizhou Chen, Hang Xu, Dongjie Yu, Yupu Lu, Tengye Xu, Zeqing Zhang, Wei Zhang, Yi Ren, Ben M. Chen, Jia Pan
Abstract: Successfully automating dexterous, long-horizon bimanual manipulation requires frameworks capable of both high-level reasoning and fine-grained execution. Traditional task and motion planning (TAMP), while excellent at symbolic planning, is often brittle in contact-rich operations. Simultaneously, imitation learning (IL), while effective in manipulation tasks with visual feedback, is limited by its low capability in spatial generalization and multi-stage operation. To reconcile their complementary strengths and limitations, we propose DR-LfD (Decomposed and Reorganized Skills Learned from Demonstrations), a framework that integrates visuomotor policies into a TAMP-gated decision-making system. Based on contact relationships, DR-LfD decomposes human demonstrations into atomic skills, which are reproduced as visuomotor policies or object-centric primitives. The initiation, termination, and constraints of the visuomotor policies are carefully modeled and implemented in a TAMP-compatible form, enabling reorganization of skills learned from different sources. DR-LfD transforms the learning problem from one requiring exponential demonstration data over possible skill sequences to one whose demonstration burden scales with the number of distinct skill types, with limited data for each skill. Real-world and simulation benchmarks demonstrate data-efficient skill composition under unseen setups and physical constraints. On two DexMimicGen tasks, DR-LfD with 100 demonstrations outperforms diffusion-based imitation policies with 1000.

### Paper [88]
Title: UniCross: Unified Cross-Skill Dexterous Manipulation Synthesis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28198
Authors: Hui Zhang, Julian Ferchow, Jie Song, Mirko Meboldt
Abstract: Many dexterous manipulation tasks require the object to remain securely held throughout the interaction. From the perspective of hand-object relational motion, such manipulation comprises four canonical skills: grasping, relocation, in-hand rotation, and in-hand translation. Human hands flexibly compose these skills to accomplish complex tasks. Existing approaches, however, model these skills separately with skill-specific action constraints, objectives, or even dedicated hand morphologies, which breaks the compatibility and continuity required for long-horizon composition. In this work, we present a unified framework that models all four skills in a single formulation instantiated with desired relational targets. The formulation preserves cross-skill compatibility and enables distillation of a single policy without skill identifiers, which achieves strong per-skill performance and generalizes to unseen objects. The compatibility further supports the policy to perform long-horizon skill chaining and execute relational targets unseen during training. The framework also applies across different hand morphologies. Together, these results suggest that distinct dexterous manipulation skills can be modeled as instantiations of a shared relational task formulation. Project page: https://zdchan.github.io/UniCross/

### Paper [89]
Title: Adaptive-WAM: Quality-Guided Early-Exit Planning from Intermediate Video-Diffusion Features
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06008
Authors: Sining Ang, Yuguang Yang, Yan Wang
Abstract: Large video diffusion models provide rich spatiotemporal priors for autonomous driving, but existing world-action models often inherit the cost of iterative future-video generation even though deployment only requires an ego trajectory. We ask a more basic question: how much of a video diffusion model must be executed to make a reliable driving decision? Through a controlled study of video denoising timesteps and Diffusion Transformer (DiT) depth, we find that planning performance is largely insensitive to the tested video-noise levels, whereas strong trajectories can already be decoded from intermediate layers. Based on this observation, we introduce Adaptive-WAM, a quality-aware multi-exit planner built on a Wan2.2-5B backbone. Trajectory diffusion heads are attached to selected DiT blocks, and a lightweight trajectory-quality scorer terminates inference once the best trajectory decoded so far satisfies a quality threshold; otherwise, computation continues from the cached hidden state to a deeper exit. The deployed planner therefore avoids the iterative classifier-free denoising loop and VAE decoding required for future-video synthesis, while dynamically allocating backbone depth according to trajectory quality. On NAVSIM, the adaptive single-trajectory planner achieves 90.8 PDMS; a separate fixed-exit variant reaches 92.6 PDMS with 64 proposals. It further obtains 89.9 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2, yielding the best reported results among the compared front-view video world-model planners. Without target-domain fine-tuning, Adaptive-WAM transfers to nuScenes with 0.88 m average L2 error and a 0.08\% collision rate. On an A100, adaptive routing improves PDMS from 90.62 to 90.79 while averaging 170 ms end-to-end planning latency, approximately 10\% below the 190 ms fixed block-15 planner and 47\% below the 320 ms fixed full-depth planner. Code will be released.

### Paper [90]
Title: Keeping the Franka Emika Panda alive: a ROS 2 stack with a reliable position interface
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19740
Authors: Antonio Langella, Davide Risi, Vincenzo Petrone, Enrico Ferrentino, Pasquale Chiacchio
Abstract: This paper presents an open-source software stack that restores ROS 2 support for the Franka Emika Panda robot while resolving the long-standing unreliability of its external position control interface. We first analyze the root causes of unstable position control and show that the observed vibrations and protective stops arise from the timing of the external control loop and sampling jitter, rather than from limitations of the robot itself. Building on this analysis, we introduce an asynchronous hardware interface that decouples real-time communication from the ROS 2 control loop, a rate-matching mechanism for slower command sources, and a position-domain reference generation strategy that produces reliable, smooth position commands. Experimental validation shows that the proposed architecture reliably tracks velocity references by reducing motion artifacts introduced by the official implementation, and the stack is validated across motion planning, compliance control, position-controlled manipulation, and haptic teleoperation on two independent Panda platforms. By restoring a modern, reliable, and open ROS 2 ecosystem for the Panda, this work lowers the barrier to developing safe, responsive, and reproducible human-robot collaboration applications that integrate planning, perception, interaction, and shared autonomy. Code and videos are available on our website at https://sites.google.com/unisa.it/fer-ros2.

### Paper [91]
Title: Learning and Transferring Closed-Loop Robot Software
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19906
Authors: So Kuroki, Kento Kawaharazuka, Yujin Tang
Abstract: Closed-loop robot policies require observation processing, state management, and situation-dependent branching, making them costly to design and tune manually. Although coding agents increasingly support control-code generation and optimization, it remains unclear whether implementations improved on source tasks also support policy acquisition for new tasks. We study this question by treating complete closed-loop implementations as reusable execution experience. To learn each source policy, a coding agent generates policy code from a few successful demonstrations and iteratively improves it using simulation feedback. The validation-selected implementations are retained in a software archive. To transfer the acquired software to new tasks, the agent generates and improves policies using archived implementations, target demonstrations, and execution feedback. We evaluate policy learning and its relationship to cross-task transfer. For learning, across four source tasks in RoboCasa, iterative optimization increases the success rate from 28.3% with initially generated policy code to 64.2% with validation-selected policy code. We further validate this learning approach on a physical Unitree G1. For transfer, across nine additional target tasks in RoboCasa, success rates are 45.2% without references, 41.5% using initially generated policy code from source tasks, and 57.0% using their validation-selected policy code. These results show that improving source-task code also improves learning on new tasks.

### Paper [92]
Title: Compressed delayed-information projection for underwater navigation under acoustic measurement latency
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27439
Authors: Shuyue Li, Miguel L\'opez-Ben\'itez, Eng Gee Lim, Fei Ma, Qian Dong, Mengze Cao, Limin Yu, Xiaohui Qin
Abstract: Delayed acoustic position measurements describe past states but may reach an underwater vehicle after its navigation estimate has advanced. This paper presents compressed delayed-information projection (CDIP), a causal delayed-update method implemented in a 15-dimensional error-state Kalman filter (ESKF). CDIP retains source-associated inertial measurement unit (IMU) snapshots and maintains current-to-snapshot cross-covariances, projecting delayed corrections without repeated full-from-start filtering. In 154 paired simulation recordings with synthetic horizontal-position fixes delayed by 1.5~s, CDIP reduced mean trajectory-position root-mean-square error (RMSE) from 1.062~m for a reception-time Baseline to 0.456~m (57.1\%). Its mean RMSE approached that of full-from-start Replay (0.451~m), with 99.2\% lower mean core-loop time per processed IMU step for the tested implementations. A six-delay sweep, scheduled permanent-loss outages of 50--300~s, and packet-erasure tests further characterized sensitivity to measurement timing and availability. A separate 256-trial nonlinear synthetic assessment identified departures from the prespecified calibration criterion for CDIP and two fully augmented ESKF references. Together, these results demonstrate an accuracy--computation trade-off for causal underwater navigation under the evaluated delayed-measurement simulation conditions.

### Paper [93]
Title: Denoising Multirobot Trajectories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35651
Authors: Yuhao Zhang, Keisuke Okumura, Ajay Shankar, Amanda Prorok
Abstract: Multi-robot trajectory planning is a fundamental problem in multi-robot coordination but remains computationally challenging due to its nonconvex, multimodal, and high-dimensional nature. This work builds upon D4orm, a dynamics-aware diffusion-denoising framework, and develops a family of planning architectures for diverse operational requirements. Unlike conventional numerical optimization methods, D4orm employs sampling-based optimization to generate solution trajectories through massively parallel sampling, leveraging modern computing architectures such as GPUs. Its diffusion-denoising structure iteratively optimizes deformations to candidate control trajectories, providing an efficient and versatile paradigm for generating kinodynamically feasible and conflict-free trajectories. Using D4orm as the building block for advanced planners, we present a decoupled planner for improved scalability, an online receding-horizon planner with feedback control, and a distributed planner for resource-constrained settings. Evaluations with differential-drive and holonomic robots in 2D and 3D environments demonstrate that D4orm-based approaches find high-quality solutions faster and more reliably than other sampling-based optimization methods, such as MPPI, as well as a learned diffusion-model-based method. We further demonstrate zero-shot deployment on ten real quadrotors with obstacles, large-scale deconfliction with 100 simulated robots, and fully onboard distributed `lifelong' operation with six ground robots. Overall, these results establish diffusion denoising as a scalable and reliable framework for multi-robot coordination. Code and video: https://github.com/proroklab/d4orm

### Paper [94]
Title: Detect and Suppress: A Mechanistic Defense against Adversarial Patches in VLA Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03498
Authors: Yukiya Horiba, Koshiro Aoki, Shunsuke Yasuki, Bum Jun Kim, Taiki Miyanishi
Abstract: Adversarial patches can disrupt Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models by manipulating visual observations, leading to failures in robot control. However, it remains poorly understood which internal mechanisms underlie these failures and how targeted interventions can mitigate them. In this work, we mechanistically analyze VLA representations using a sparse autoencoder (SAE) and identify a feature whose activation strongly correlates with the presence of an adversarial patch. Based on this analysis, we suppress the identified feature at inference time only when a linear probe detects an attack. This intervention improves robustness without the cost of fine-tuning the VLA. We evaluate our method against VLA adversarial patch attacks on LIBERO-10. Conditional intervention improves success rate under intermittent attacks, whereas continuously applying the same intervention substantially degrades policy performance. These results show that attack-related internal representations can provide useful targets for VLA adversarial defense and that controlling when to intervene is important for limiting disruption to nominal policy behavior.

### Paper [95]
Title: I3L: Iterative Interactive Imitation Learning for Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17748
Authors: Wensi Ai, Christopher Luey, Karthik Dharmarajan, Evans Han, Yunfan Jiang, Stef Ren, Jianwen Xie, Jiajun Wu, Li Fei-Fei, Ruohan Zhang
Abstract: Policies trained offline from teleoperated demonstrations can degrade outside their training distribution. This gap is particularly acute in long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation, where long horizons amplify compounding errors and coordinated arm, torso, and base motion makes exhaustive demonstration coverage impractical. We introduce I3L, an integrated hardware and learning system that combines whole-body correction with iterative policy refinement to address this last-mile reliability gap. Its low-cost, open-source JoyLo+ interface uses policy-tracking leader arms and touch-triggered takeover to enable coordinated corrections across the body parts, while iterative deployment and retraining allow operators to target failures of the updated policy.
