We evaluate I3L across three robot embodiments and eight simulated and real-world tasks, including four long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation tasks. Controlled simulation experiments show that four-round HG-DAgger achieves the highest final success on both tasks. Under this protocol, I3L improves autonomous success over the base policies by an average of 68.3 percentage points and achieves at least 90% success on every task, outperforming demonstration-only training by 35.6 percentage points on average with the same number of training trajectories. Averaged across tasks, takeover duration per episode decreases by 72% over four rounds. Together, these results show that I3L helps close the last-mile gap between offline training and reliable deployment and provides practical insights into collecting and using corrections for long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation. Videos, visualizations, and appendix are at https://i-3l.github.io/.

### Paper [96]
Title: TacClaw-Infinity: A Large-Scale Vision-Tactile-Language-Action Dataset for Dexterous Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20320
Authors: Fucai Zhu, Xidong Zhang, Yichi Zhang, Yipan Wang, Fubo Wang, Zibin Wu, Kaiqi Li, Jianhao Jiao, Shihan Lu, Di Hu, Mengdi Xu, Siyu Zhu, Xiaoxiao Long, Yao Yao, Xun Cao, Zhuo Xu, Koichi Hashimoto, Qifeng Chen, Zhe Li, Jianfei Yang, Haofei Lu, Danica Kragic, Shuicheng Yan, Jianghua Duan, Michael Yu Wang, Weihao Yuan
Abstract: Large-scale robot learning increasingly relies on diverse human demonstrations, yet tactile manipulation data remain difficult to scale, and existing multimodal datasets often lack consistent quality control and structured supervision. We present TacClaw-Infinity, a large-scale structured Vision-Tactile-Language-Action (VTLA) dataset for contact-rich manipulation, collected with TacClaw, a tactile-enabled UMI-style handheld interface that records synchronized visual observations, fingertip tactile signals, and end-effector trajectories without requiring a target robot during data collection. TacClaw-Infinity is built through a distributed worldwide collection across homes, laboratories, industrial workplaces, retail stores, and outdoor environments including tasks contributed by academic and industrial collaborators. Together, these efforts yield over 10,000 hours, 1 million episodes, and 1 billion frames spanning more than 1,000 task types. We develop a scalable pipeline for trajectory reconstruction, multimodal quality control, embodiment-aware filtering, and structured annotation, covering contact-centric segmentation, subtask description, and spatial grounding. We validate TacClaw-Infinity at both the data and model levels: At the data level, we assess the collection interface, the action-space compatibility of the curated demonstrations, and the structured annotations, while for the model level, we demonstrate cross-embodiment transfer across three heterogeneous robots without robot-specific demonstrations.

### Paper [97]
Title: Learning from Fleet Experience: Collective Experience Learning Across 96 Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25736
Authors: Chao Yu, Shu'ang Yu, Hao Lin, Hongzhi Zang, Yuxiang Yan, Chenxi Jiang, Zhen Guo, Chuankang Li, Yihao Chen, Xin Xu, Xu Fu, Jiakai Zhou, Liangzhi Shi, Hongyi Ren, Yiwei Liu, Jing Wu, Yuanqing Wang, Chen Wu, Bo Dai, Chunyu Yang, Hongliang Huang, Shuoyu Yue, Weiqi Yin, Yinuo Chen, Ruihua Chen, Chunyang Zhu, Han Xiao, Yanghao Li, Ruipu Wu, Zhikai Cai, Xiu Chen, Xu Tan, Si Liu, Yuchen Yan, Jialin Zhang, Wen Zeng, Yiming Zhou, Zixiao Huang, Xinlei Chen, Guanghui Ren, Quanlu Zhang, Boxun Li, Maoqing Yao, Yu Wang
Abstract: Robot deployment generates experience that can support policy improvement, but continued reliance on expert corrective actions makes learning difficult to scale across growing fleets. We propose learning from fleet experience: using deployed robots' own executions as a source of supervision for further policy improvement. We call this paradigm Collective Experience Learning (CEL), in which robots pool execution experience to update a shared policy that returns to the fleet. Following expert-supervised initialization, we realize CEL through failure-guided experience retrieval fine-tuning (FERT). FERT uses failure contexts to retrieve relevant segments from successful trajectories and prioritizes their recorded actions for supervised updates, without collecting additional expert demonstrations or corrective actions. Operators provide binary episode-outcome labels and environment resets. A fault-tolerant asynchronous training system (FATS) coordinates robot execution, cloud inference and policy optimization despite local interruptions. Across five real-world manipulation tasks, the resulting policies achieve success rates of 93.3-99.2%, evaluated over 120 trials per task on robots excluded from training data collection. With collection time per robot held fixed, larger fleets provide more experience and generally improve performance across the evaluated sizes. On multi-stage phone packaging, learning with 96 robots raises success from 79.2% to 98.3%. These results support CEL as a scalable approach to improving robot policy reliability through experience generated during deployment.

### Paper [98]
Title: Do Earth-Trained Humanoid Controllers Walk on the Moon? Gravity, Terrain, and Soil Transfer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30080
Authors: Guillem Casadesus Vila, Sri Aditya Deevi, Grace X. Gao
Abstract: Humanoid robots could aid lunar operations, but how to train their locomotion controllers for lunar gravity, terrain, and soil is unclear. We compare 12 strategies for a simulated Unitree G1 across gravity, terrain, and rigid or granular 3D resistive force theory (3D RFT) contact. Earth-trained policies keep 0.64 to 0.75 survival at the Moon on flat rigid ground in 10 s episodes, but falls continue, so longer episodes would see lower survival. Moon-trained and fine-tuned policies, in contrast, fall in every Earth episode, nearly all within 2 s. Rough-terrain training helps on uneven rigid ground only at training gravity. 3D RFT soil training helps on Earth sand but little on lunar soil. On three held-out soil models, Moon-trained policies lose no detectable progress at lunar gravity. Among tested strategies, only gravity-randomized rough 3D RFT training keeps mean survival above 0.83 at Earth and 0.95 at the Moon on all key tests up to 20 deg. Its ablation without soil training differs consistently only on soil at Earth gravity, falling in most sand episodes. These predictions depend on the contact model and soil stiffness, so lunar soil claims require agreement across soil models.

### Paper [99]
Title: Dispersive Tree Agreements for Multi-Robot Coordination
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31540
Authors: Shashank A. Deshpande, Blake T. Edwards, Jonathan P. How
Abstract: Multi-robot coordination of nonlinear systems is naturally formulated as joint-space optimal control subject to joint constraints. Existing methods that approach joint optimality remain largely centralized and offline, while decentralized, real-time counterparts target safe navigation without pursuing the joint optimum. This paper develops a decentralized, real-time planner that approaches the joint optimum as an anytime process. We extend a recent single-robot framework that searches dispersive trees of forward-propagated commands to coordinate a multi-robot team near-optimally. We first show that the inter-robot coupling can be effectively replaced by some local constraints, under which locally grown dispersive trees contain a near-optimal agreement. It is this structure that the robots pursue by a negotiation driven by decentralized column generation. In the quiescent limit, this negotiation is shown to settle on a near-optimal agreement. Our real-time protocol, WarpWeaver, runs the negotiation asynchronously over a receding horizon and closes on quiesced agreements even when deadlines cut it short. In simulation, WarpWeaver reduces total travel time by up to 22% against centralized offline planners on the Dynobench suite and by up to 16% against a decentralized real-time baseline, and outperforms a task-specific planner on networked navigation. Hardware experiments on six robots planning onboard demonstrate real-world implementability.

### Paper [100]
Title: MOON: Learning When to Forget and How to Move On in Vision-and-Language Navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33546
Authors: Jingmeng Zhou, Seson Sun, Binyao Xu, Lingming Zhang, Hanshi Wang, Fudong Ge, Weiming Hu, Jin Gao, Zhipeng Zhang
Abstract: Recent vision-and-language navigation (VLN) policies act on their visual history, and an incorrect action can take them off the instructed route. Paired interventions on four VLN policies show that clearing the history doubles pooled success at the onset of a deviation but lowers it on route, while later in a deviation, removing only the off-route frames helps much less. After a deviation, the retained history keeps the policy from turning where a turn is needed, which we call behavioral inertia. We propose MOON, a framework that equips a frozen VLN policy with two lightweight modules, adding less than 1% of its parameters. A deviation judge decides when to forget, raising an alarm that clears the history when it predicts that the agent is off route. A recovery policy, trained with GRPO from alarm states, learns how to move on, turning more of the successes found only by sampling into a single greedy rollout. Experiments on R2R-CE and RxR-CE show that MOON reaches the highest R2R-CE success rate among the compared methods and raises the success rate of its frozen base by 5.2 and 6.0 points, and an ablation with a random trigger indicates that this gain depends on when the history is cleared. Without retraining, MOON also improves zero-shot object-goal navigation on HM3D-OVON. Together, these results suggest that deviation recovery depends on knowing when to forget, not only how to act. Code is available at https://github.com/AutoLab-SAI-SJTU/MOON.

### Paper [101]
Title: PDRVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model with Physical Dynamics Representations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37381
Authors: Hanqing Wang, Zhaotian Cai, Ruichuan An, Bohan Zeng, Tao Zhang, Yilin Chen, Xu Jiang, Kaiquan Chen, Chongyang Wu, Haoran Li, Fan Yang, Mingan Lin, Wentao Zhang, Zenan Zhou, Youzheng Wu
Abstract: Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models generate robot actions from visual observations and language instructions, but their understanding of physical dynamics remains limited. Video world models can capture scene dynamics, yet generating future videos introduces high latency into the control loop. We introduce PDRVLA, whose lightweight Physical Dynamics Module extracts physical tokens from vision--language features to condition action generation without online video generation. The module learns through direct prediction of future RGB, depth, and optical-flow features, with auxiliary supervision through a frozen pretrained video world model. Training relies entirely on publicly available data, expanded through human-to-robot synthesis, subtask annotation, and embodied chain-of-thought (ECoT) annotation. Across four simulation benchmarks, PDRVLA achieves success rates of 99.3% on LIBERO, 89.9% on LIBERO-Plus, 93.6% on RoboTwin~2.0, and 81.5% on SimplerEnv. Ablations show improved future prediction with world-model supervision and evaluate how subtask conditioning, future object-pose supervision, and ECoT training affect action success.

### Paper [102]
Title: SimRetract: Zero-Shot Sim-to-Real Robotic Assistance for Retractor Take-Over in Open Surgery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38234
Authors: Xucheng Wang, Zhizhou Yang, Sung Eun Kim, Romain Hardy, Michael P. Sampson, Aakash Reddy, Ankit Pal, Xiaoman Zhang, Luyang Luo, Pranav Rajpurkar
Abstract: We demonstrate the first zero-shot sim-to-real transfer for a surgeon-robot collaboration task in open surgery: retractor take-over. A robot arm takes a retractor from the surgeon's hand and preserves retraction at handoff, using a policy trained only on simulated demonstrations, without human demonstrations or real-world fine-tuning. Imitation learning from real demonstrations is limited by collection and labeling costs, and by performance degradation when the environment changes. Our framework, SimRetract, generates simulated demonstrations designed for transfer: the policy observes masked instrument images and grasp geometry, learns to recover from off-nominal states, and acts with margin for execution drift. Deployed zero-shot, it grasps the retractor in 90% of 50 surgeon-defined phantom presentations and preserves retraction at handoff in 78%. Results on unseen retractors suggest potential for generalization to instruments with similar grasp geometry. Across ten policy configurations and 1,200 simulated and real trials, we localize the sim-to-real gap to components: in simulation, grasp rates across ablations span 80-96%, while on the real robot removing the instrument mask alone drops performance to 22% grasped and 12% held. Transfer-oriented design thus makes zero-shot sim-to-real transfer feasible for a surgical assistance task without human demonstrations. Code is available here: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/simretract-58D2.

### Paper [103]
Title: Actuator-Aware Observer Inputs for Cascade ADRC of Quadrotors Under Rotor Lag, Measurement Delay and Sensor Noise
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39126
Authors: Xuan-Quy Dao, Ngoc-Bich Le, Le-Minh Nguyen
Abstract: In sampled cascade linear active disturbance rejection control (LADRC) of quadrotors, the extended-state observer typically receives the instantaneous command, although the delayed measurement reflects an earlier, rotor-filtered acceleration. This paper proposes actuator-aware (AA) observer inputs: altitude and attitude observers use limited virtual inputs filtered by an assumed rotor model and aligned with the measurement delay, and horizontal observers use acceleration reconstructed from measured attitude. Fixed gains are selected on the discrete-time model under sensitivity and step-response requirements and stationary covariance ceilings on each channel's control variance and increment and on the noise-induced inner-loop increment of the full cascade. In paired set-point trials, PID-Budget, AA-Budget and AA-HalfGain reach mean RMSEs of 65.5, 19.2 and 16.9~mm; under a stricter common cascade ceiling, the AA designs reach 29.9 and 26.4~mm versus 72.3~mm for a PID that uses roughly double their rotor-command increment. PX4 software-in-the-loop runs reproduce these results. Behind EKF2, a noise model fitted to the estimation errors predicts the roll/pitch and yaw increments within 5\% on held-out and out-of-sample runs and the total rotor-command increment within 14\%. The controllers were then redesigned for this stack under the same sensitivity and response specification and estimator-aware ceilings. AA-Budget-E reduces the set-point RMSE from 59.1~mm (PID-E) to 44.0~mm with a lower rotor-command increment in every paired set-point and lemniscate run (mean S6 reduction: 45\% before and 15\% after output quantization); AA-HalfGain-E beats PID-E only on the lemniscate. These results indicate that the observer input and the outer-to-inner noise path are design variables of sampled cascade LADRC. The evidence is simulation-based, and the ceilings are nominal tuning constraints.

### Paper [104]
Title: OpenFlyV2: An onboard vision-language-action model for aerial perception and navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39287
Authors: Junli Liu, Zhongrui You, Chenhui Li, Zhen Li, Yuyang Tian, Qizhen Chen, Delin Qu, Haoming Song, Dong Wang, Xuelong Li, Zhigang Wang, Bin Zhao
Abstract: Human cognition flexibly draws on a shared perceptual foundation to understand environments, attend to referenced entities, reason about spatial relationships, and act. Endowing aerial robots with an analogous functional organization remains challenging, as existing systems are often specialized for individual capabilities, dependent on offboard computation, or unable to sustain closed-loop operation on resource-constrained platforms. We present OpenFlyV2, a unified onboard vision-language-action architecture that uses a shared vision-language representation to support semantic understanding, language-grounded perception, spatial reasoning, and continuous aerial control within a single model. A compact action expert converts the shared multimodal context into short-horizon flight waypoints, while motion-aware temporal memory and action priors reduce the computational cost of closed-loop execution. We construct \dataset{}, a simulation-based dataset comprising approximately 70,000 navigation trajectories and 60,000 vision-language instruction--response pairs. OpenFlyV2 generates action chunks, each comprising 12 waypoints, entirely onboard at 10 Hz using an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX. It transfers zero-shot from simulation to physical quadrotor flight, achieving a 48\% navigation success rate across four real-world environments without real-world aerial training data. Extensive results demonstrate that broad vision-language competence and continuous closed-loop flight can coexist within a single model on a resource-constrained aerial platform. Code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/Ideal-ljl/AeroBrain.

### Paper [105]
Title: LOGIV: Graph-Verified Execution with Causal Local Repair for Long-Horizon Robot Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40016
Authors: Enqiao Lu (CUHKSZ, THU), Yuxiang Xie (CUHKSZ), Xingrui Yu (A*STAR), Yuyang Wang (Caltech), Yipeng Gu (CUHKSZ), Zhirui Fang (THU, NTU), Xinrun Xu (THU), Zhenglin Wan (NUS), Pengfei Zhou (NUS), Wangbo Zhao (HKUST), Yang You (NUS), Yuxiang Gao (A*STAR, NTU), Ivor Tsang (A*STAR, NTU)
Abstract: Agentic robots must turn a high-level goal into actions while keeping the physical conditions needed for future steps valid. A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symbolic planning, observation-based verification, and embodied action. LOGIV compiles a validated plan into condition-support and resource-ordering edges, checks their physical validity during execution, and uses dependency tracing to determine what must be restored after a failure. Repair is constrained by retained unfinished actions, and the combined continuation is symbolically validated before execution resumes. The framework operates above existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Model (WAM) policies without adding trainable components. Across 20 dual-arm tasks from RoboTwin V2 and BiCoord with three execution backbones, LOGIV improves mean task success without injected disturbances by 10.67 and 15.33 percentage points, respectively. Under predefined perturbations, it achieves higher task completion and condition restoration than three adapted recovery baselines for every backbone on both benchmarks. On three real-robot tasks, successful trials increase from 12/30 to 17/30. These results support explicit, continuously checked task dependencies as a practical interface for reliable agentic robot execution.

### Paper [106]
Title: MUSBRIC: LEGO-Compatible Pneumatic Muscle Bricks for Reconfigurable Musculoskeletal Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40285
Authors: Qiyao Wang, Yinglei Zhu, Liang Zhou, Wenzhe Gong, Xin-Jun Liu, Huichan Zhao
Abstract: Musculoskeletal robots combine compliant actuators with articulated skeletons, but exploring alternative configurations often requires repeated fabrication. We present MUSBRIC, a modular hardware platform and accompanying MuJoCo-based simulator for reconfigurable musculoskeletal robots. LEGO-compatible endcaps turn fiber-reinforced extensile pneumatic artificial muscles into reusable, plug-and-play muscle bricks that connect directly to standard LEGO components. Experimentally identified actuator behavior supports corresponding musculoskeletal simulations. An elbow-joint prototype validates load-dependent flexion and extension, showing that pulley-guided routing enables reversible motion and a mechanical stop stabilizes the straight posture. A robotic hand demonstrates compliant grasping and multi-joint construction. Matched hardware and simulation sweeps across 15 leg configurations demonstrate systematic exploration of attachment geometry, while a quadruped assembled from four legs demonstrates walking. Together, these demonstrations span mechanism analysis, configuration exploration, and functional robot construction within a single reconfigurable system. The hardware designs and simulation code will be released as open source soon to facilitate reproduction and further development.

### Paper [107]
Title: Shared Modular Recurrence in Contextual MDPs for Universal Morphology Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08630
Authors: Laurens Engwegen, Max Weltevrede, Caroline Horsch, Daan Brinks, Wendelin B\"ohmer
Abstract: A universal controller for any robot morphology would greatly improve computational and data efficiency. Steps have been made towards such multi-robot control by utilizing contextual information about the properties of individual robots and exploiting their modular structure in the architecture of deep reinforcement learning agents. When the robots have highly dissimilar morphologies, however, this becomes a challenging problem, especially when the agent must generalize to new, unseen robots. In this paper, we consider the fact that contextual features are often only partially available, but that they can be recovered through modular interactions. This can allow for better multi-robot control and generalization to contexts that are not seen during training. To this extent, we implement a transformer-based architecture with shared modular recurrence and evaluate its (generalization) performance on a large set of MuJoCo robots. The results show a substantial improvement in zero-shot generalization performance on robots with unseen dynamics, kinematics, and topologies, in four different environments.

### Paper [108]
Title: SV-WAM: An Efficient Surround-View World-Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03602
Authors: Jinyang Wang, Shiwei Li, Junjian Wang, Zhiqiang Deng, Jianbin Gao, Yihang Zhao, Liu Liu, Yongjia Zhao, Jinlong Chen, Huirui Xu, Yifeng Pan, Kangwei Liu, Fan Ren, Ji Tao, Minghao Yang
Abstract: World models (WMs) have demonstrated strong potential for end-to-end autonomous driving by learning predictive representations of future scene dynamics. However, generating future videos during inference introduces substantial computational overhead, leading many recent driving WMs to adopt a single front camera as input for efficient deployment. This design restricts spatial coverage in safety-critical maneuvers such as lane changes, merges, and turns. To address this limitation, we propose SV-WAM, a surround-view world-action model (WAM) that preserves full six-camera observations while maintaining efficient inference. SV-WAM leverages future-video prediction as dense training supervision for action learning within a shared generative model, rather than as an inference-time output. At the core of this design is an action-centered causal mask that prevents action tokens from attending to future-video tokens during joint action-video denoising. Consequently, the video branch can be discarded at deployment, enabling efficient action-only planning. Furthermore, we introduce a differentiable drivable-area compliance regularizer that penalizes vehicle-footprint corners approaching or crossing drivable boundaries, improving planning safety and boundary awareness. Extensive experiments on the closed-loop NAVSIMv2 benchmark and the open-loop nuScenes benchmark demonstrate that SV-WAM achieves state-of-the-art planning performance with low inference latency and competitive zero-shot transfer capability.

### Paper [109]
Title: Self-Building Labs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23826
Authors: Jiaru Bai, Yuchi Zhao, Han Hao, Elia Savino, Miroslav Bogdanovic, Derk H. W. ten Klooster, Simone Pilon, Timothy No\"el, Yang Cao, Varinia Bernales, Al\'an Aspuru-Guzik
Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific discovery by automating experimental execution and optimization. AI agents can increasingly propose new experiments, but existing SDLs can only run the procedures they were built for. Adapting them to a new experiment still requires extensive manual engineering, which makes laboratory flexibility the bottleneck for scaling discovery. We introduce the self-building laboratory (SBL), which designs, configures, and operates its own laboratory setup given a high-level scientific goal. SBL starts from whatever already exists, from an empty room to a working SDL, and builds the missing capabilities: the lab layout, its digital twin, robot skills, and experimental workflows. We demonstrate the system working across different robots and setups, scaling from a single workstation to laboratories with several robots working together. We validate SBL's end-to-end capabilities through a real-world quantum-dot synthesis experiment. By shifting human researchers from manually configuring and programming device automation to specifying scientific objectives, SBL makes scientific automation more accessible, flexible, and scalable. Project website: https://self-building-labs.github.io/

### Paper [110]
Title: Seeing and Imagining as One: Unified Video Generation and 4D Perception for Embodied World Modeling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39232
Authors: Haitang Feng, Zhuoman Liu, Zhennan Wang, Sheng Wang, Juelin Li, Chengzhi Zhao, Hexiang Wei, Lin Shao
Abstract: Embodied world models should not only imagine plausible futures, but also perceive the geometric, semantic, and motion structure of the evolving world. We present GenSight, a unified embodied world model that brings future video generation and dense perception into a single pretrained video diffusion transformer. GenSight uses a unified spatiotemporal representation for video, depth, embodiment segmentation, and first-frame-relative 2D flow. Built on a shared DiT backbone, GenSight uses lightweight task modulation to predict all modalities within the same architecture. All tasks are jointly optimized within a unified velocity prediction space. At inference, GenSight supports two complementary modes: multi-step future video generation with aligned dense predictions and single-step dense perception from an observed video. By unifying modality representation, model architecture, and prediction space, GenSight avoids separate modality-specific pipelines and couples appearance, geometry, semantics, and motion in a shared representation. It promotes cross-modal consistency and allows dense perception to benefit from the spatiotemporal priors learned through video generation. Trained on embodied manipulation data spanning multiple robot embodiments and human-hand interactions, GenSight demonstrates strong performance in both video generation and dense 4D perception, highlighting the effectiveness of unifying seeing and imagining for embodied world modeling.

### Paper [111]
Title: More Rules, Longer Lists? Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on a Hidden-Rule Curriculum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40758
Authors: Chenjie Qiu, Boyan Xu, Shaobin Shi, Bingfeng Chen, Ruichu Cai, Dongsheng Luo
Abstract: Self-evolving agents rewrite a persistent state from feedback, but it is largely untested whether this lets them learn a rule nobody told them. An agent can also pass by writing down each correction as an exception, and its list then grows with every new rule. We introduce CLR-Bench, a Curriculum of Latent Rules benchmark. Each chain adds one hidden rule per level on top of the earlier ones. A rule acts on an opaque label that stands for a named grouping published on the Web, such as the founding members of an airline alliance. The task gives one example value, practice feedback reveals one more member and one non-member, and the test checks unseen members and similar non-members. Probes certify that the base model knows the members of each grouping and does not guess it without feedback, and that the evidence identifies the grouping once labels are read as named groupings. An automated pipeline builds chains from a new data domain, and we release 20 chains over 10 domains. We evaluate six recent self-evolution methods. With the certifying model they barely learn. The best gain at most 5 points over a frozen agent and exclude only 6-12% of the unseen members from the count, fewer than the 20% reached by simply keeping the raw practice history. A fixed lesson that states the named-grouping convention lifts the same methods by 14.5 to 16.5 points. Their states hold the revealed member in 75-98% of cases and almost never the class name, and when practice exposes a member the list misses, they add that member to the list. With the stronger GPT-6-Sol, ACE and Dynamic Cheatsheet learn from feedback alone. They reach 78% accuracy and solve 59-60% of the single cells, against 53% and 8% with the certifying model, and they surpass the raw history. CLR-Bench therefore measures how well an agent learns, and this ability differs across models.

### Paper [112]
Title: Beyond Plausible Prediction: Safety Sufficiency in Web World Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40798
Authors: Zhifei Hu, Alexandra I. Cristea, Adel Bibi, Jialin Yu
Abstract: Web world models increasingly predict the consequences of candidate actions before agents act, yet a plausible predicted future does not necessarily support the correct safety decision. In a matched human evaluation of 3,000 predictions from ten frozen open models, 86.1% are judged semantically plausible. However, several models exhibit highly asymmetric authorisation behaviour, combining low false-safe execution with poor recall of clarifiable ASK cases and a strong preference for BLOCK. This motivates safety sufficiency: whether a predictive interface preserves and exposes the policy-decisive information required to distinguish actions that may be EXECUTED, require clarification through ASK, or should be BLOCKED. We introduce SafeWebWorld, a benchmark for evaluating safety sufficiency across five web-safety domains through action-conditioned futures, policy-decisive evidence, and policy-conditioned decisions. Under a controlled Security-Aligned Supervised Fine-Tuning protocol, evidence preservation can improve substantially while authorisation-sensitive decisions deteriorate. To determine whether this deterioration reflects loss of authorisation-sensitive score structure or a failure of the decision readout, we introduce Latent Authorisation Recovery (LAR). Frozen-score recalibration reveals substantial but backbone-dependent recovery, while paired recovery provides only a modest additional gain. Yet within-family and matched-authorisation evaluations remain poor, showing that aggregate recoverability does not imply robust fine-grained authorisation behaviour. Overall, plausible prediction, evidence preservation, and aggregate recovery are each insufficient on their own to establish robust authorisation.

### Paper [113]
Title: Circumventing the Computational Hardness of Computational Arbitrage
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40831
Authors: Nastaran Okati, Ricardo Olmedo, Moritz Hardt
Abstract: AI model marketplaces host providers with heterogeneous costs and capabilities. These differences create an opportunity for computational arbitrage (Olmedo et al., 2026), in which an intermediary purchases inference from a portfolio of models and resells it to users at a profit. Beyond creating profit opportunities for the arbitrageur, computational arbitrage offers financial value to both consumers and providers and could therefore significantly influence AI model markets. While prior work empirically demonstrates the feasibility of computational arbitrage for small model pools, scaling it to large marketplaces requires arbitrage strategies that are both computationally tractable and inexpensive to identify and deploy. In this work, we formalize computational arbitrage as an allocation problem and prove that it is generally NP-hard. However, we show that the optimal allocation can be computed efficiently under a natural diminishing-returns condition on how model performance scales with inference spending. We further develop a sample-efficient method for estimating model performance from limited data, reducing the upfront cost of identifying arbitrage strategies. Our analysis also yields several economic insights: at the optimum, every funded model delivers the same marginal utility per dollar; arbitrage directs revenue away from providers offering little utility relative to their prices; and it rewards complementary specialization.

### Paper [114]
Title: Incongruence-Aware Evaluation of Multimodal Speech Emotion Recognition in Audio Large Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40883
Authors: Fengji Jing, Guy Laban
Abstract: Voice-interactive large language models process both what a speaker says and how they say it, yet the two channels do not always agree. We ask whether that disagreement degrades speech emotion recognition. Mapping text and audio into a shared valence-arousal-dominance space, we convert the distance between them into a continuous, threshold-free incongruence score with a two-component Gaussian mixture, and validate the score against human judgement. We evaluate three audiocapable models on acted, conversational and spontaneous speech (eNTERFACE'05, MELD and CMU-MOSEI). With the model held constant across modalities, incongruence raises the odds of error whenever audio is present, in every corpus, after controlling for the ground-truth emotion and for distance in the feature space. Supervised classifiers trained on the same features behave in the opposite way, which suggests that the difficulty lies in how these models combine raw evidence rather than in the signal itself. Text-only effects are unreliable: swapping only the model that reads the transcript reverses their sign on one corpus. Rebuilding the score without the emotion lexicon reproduces the central result, and among six simpler alternatives it is the only label-free score that predicts error in all three corpora. Code, data tables and prompts are released.

### Paper [115]
Title: ChatGPT as a Surveillance Assistant? An Exploratory Case Study of AI-Assisted Surveillance Risks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40890
Authors: Ayana Hussain, Nicholas Vincent
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can generate detailed guidance, functional code, and persuasive text at scale. As one of the most widely used consumer language model products as of 2026, ChatGPT gives broad public access to these capabilities, motivating concerns about its potential use in surveillance-related tasks. We present an exploratory case study of what potentially harmful surveillance assistance looks like in ChatGPT's public web interface. We contribute a taxonomy of seven surveillance risk categories based on prior LLM risk research and reports of deployed surveillance systems, instantiated as 110 prompts, along with a manually reviewed analysis of ChatGPT's responses. Prompts were issued using two approaches: 55 jailbreak prompts drawing on five types of elicitation strategies, and 55 plain-language direct prompts, each run alone, and if unsuccessful, again with a short fixed prefix. We find that ChatGPT provides substantive surveillance guidance for 40 of the 55 jailbreak prompts and for 33 of the 55 direct prompts without any prefix. Compliant responses also rarely included policy mentions or suggestions for privacy-preserving alternatives. We manually review every response and find that even when ChatGPT states a refusal, it may still supply detailed instructions, working code, or architectural guidance alongside brief ethical caveats.

### Paper [116]
Title: Does Recursive Knowledge Construction Help Agents Verify Consensus Claims in Science?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40906
Authors: Lun Ai, Yu-Heng Wu, Jonathan Schaul, Leonie K\"uchenhoff, Denes Turei, Francesco Carli, Fabio Petroni, Aurelien Dugourd, Julio Saez-Rodriguez
Abstract: Recent scientific agents build knowledge recursively by retrieving evidence, constructing an intermediate state, and judging whether that state contains sufficient information. These agents are evaluated end-to-end, usually without ground truth, so the effect of each design choice is unknown. Here, we examined two design axes: (1) what the recursion builds (the state of evidence) and (2) what controls it (the judge that decides when to stop). To measure them, we defined consensus scientific claim verification and measured these effects on this task, where a system must establish a claim's consensus stance from the open literature. We built a new consensus claim dataset, ProClaim-eval, including 718 claims with SUPPORT and REFUTE gold labels from five expert-curated databases spanning molecular biology, nuclear physics, and astronomy. Our instrument, ProClaim, persists an evidence state outside the model's context, so each axis can be studied with the other held fixed. Against verification systems and general-purpose agent harnesses, ProClaim ranks among the top three, with a significant advantage over the harnesses. We found that the evidence state improves performance over in-context accumulation, and it also matters what is stored in it. In addition, a trained judge that reads the evidence state performs better than fixed-round stopping. A judge trained separately and never exposed to ProClaim-eval achieves the highest agreement with the gold labels on three of five subsets, where it matches the off-the-shelf Claude Sonnet~4.6 judge and outperforms Claude Haiku~4.5. Our results support recursively maintaining a structured evidence state outside the model's context as an effective design for verifying claims in scientific agents.

### Paper [117]
Title: Towards Portable Specification of the Agent Harness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40917
Authors: Nicolas Gertler, Claudio Novelli, Luciano Floridi
Abstract: Large language models act only through the infrastructure that surrounds them. That infrastructure, the harness, determines what a model can accomplish and how it fails, yet it has no settled definition. To address this gap, we decompose the harness into eight functional components that can be specified and compared across systems, and we propose a portable specification framework that records which model ran in which harness. The record is needed because different harness configurations can yield several-fold differences in reported scores for the same model, and because much of an agent's attack surface lies in its harness. Existing model cards and agent cards provide release and operational information, but do not establish the sequence of effective configurations within a run. Harnesses can change between releases and between runs, and, where agents edit their own tools and configuration, within a run. Enacted AI laws contain documentation duties relevant to covered systems, subject to their respective scopes and application dates, but do not prescribe the common harness record proposed here. Therefore, the harness has to be explicitly described and specified as a versioned part of the system.

### Paper [118]
Title: Agentic World Modeling for Agent Test-time Scaling without Environment Replica
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40927
Authors: Wendi Li, Huseyin A. Inan, Wei-Ning Chen, Yanzhi Chen, Chetan Bansal, Saravan Rajmohan, Sharon Li
Abstract: Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model agents by sampling and selecting among multiple candidate trajectories, but common realizations assume that these trajectories can be executed in parallel. This assumption breaks in realistic deployments, where enterprise workspaces and cloud services are shared and persistent, costly or infeasible to replicate, and actions may introduce irreversible side effects or cross-trajectory interference. Existing generative world models avoid direct execution, but typically compress environment state into a single prompt, limiting their ability to reliably simulate transitions under partial observability. Instead, we introduce Agentic World Modeling (AWM), which reformulates next-observation prediction as a multi-turn evidence-acquisition task. For each hypothetical action, an agentic simulator adaptively queries the real environment through safe read-only interfaces, reasons over the returned evidence, and uses executable computation to construct the resulting observation without mutating shared state. Integrated with trajectory-level TTS, AWM enables parallel exploration over a single shared environment. Across $\tau$-bench, AppWorld, and OfficeBench, AWM outperforms a single-shot simulation baseline by 40.07% on average, and surpasses a trained world-model baseline by 12.36% on average. These results establish interactive, read-grounded simulation as a practical foundation for safe and scalable agentic test-time scaling in shared, non-resettable environments.

### Paper [119]
Title: DiRAFT: Privacy-Aware Financial Transcript Synthesis from Aggregate Discourse Priors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40938
Authors: Yunbai Zhang, Yunyi Gao, Han-Tai Shiao
Abstract: Using customer conversations to train and evaluate financial language models raises confidentiality concerns because non-public financial facts can remain after direct identifiers are masked. Synthetic transcripts offer an alternative data source, but their discourse fidelity and residual confidentiality risks require assessment. We introduce DiRAFT (Discourse Realization from Aggregate First-order Transitions), which extracts financial concept graphs and records the order in which concepts are first introduced. It pools adjacent concept transitions across training records and samples new graph plans from aggregate transition, starting-concept, and plan-length priors. A discourse layer guides text realization without supplying an individual source passage or graph. We assess privacy-related risks through two complementary evaluations: corpus-level structural overlap and source-grounded checks for specific non-public investor facts. The structural assessment covers two financial corpora and three generation backends. The confidential-fact assessment uses human review of 1,400 investor-call outputs across seven strategies and two backends. We also evaluate transition fidelity, content recoverability, and trajectory diversity. Stochastic sampling provides a favorable balance between transition fidelity and structural overlap, with consistent advantages over greedy and top-3 on investor calls. The confidential-fact assessment likewise detects fewer matches for stochastic sampling than these alternatives on both backends covered by the fact assessment. These complementary assessments provide empirical evidence on residual confidentiality risks without establishing a formal guarantee.

### Paper [120]
Title: The First Few Tokens Matter: Understanding Chain-of-Thought Controllability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40939
Authors: Hugh Van Deventer, Dmitrii Troitskii, Laura Ruis
Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising way to oversee increasingly capable agents. It succeeds in part because current models appear limited in their ability to control what they verbalize in their reasoning traces. We investigate the source of this limitation and link it to an entropy collapse in the first few tokens following the opening thinking tag. We exploit this mechanism to recover CoT controllability through two inference-time interventions on initial tokens: prefilling, where even a single token can substantially increase controllability, and banning frequent opening tokens combined with in-context examples, which improves average compliance by 34-41 percentage points across models. These interventions recover mainly addition and style controllability (adding or transforming information), while suppression controllability (avoiding or removing information) exhibits more varied responses, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. Consistent with this, the two groups behave as separate capabilities: across models, performance correlates positively within these groups, while cross-group correlations are mostly negative. Importantly, we find that the relationship with monitorability depends on the evaluation: addition and style controllability more consistently positively correlate with monitorability, while suppression often shows negative associations. Our findings motivate the development of controllability evaluations that distinguish difficulty initiating a specific opening from general limitations of the ability to alter reasoning content, alongside separate reporting of control tasks and categories.

### Paper [121]
Title: Benchmarking Forecasting Agents on Yesterday's Tomorrows
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40940
Authors: Yuxuan Wang, Yishu Wang, Jiaqi Deng
Abstract: Digital agents can now gather and organize information, reason over it, and use tools to complete complex tasks. Forecasting puts all of these abilities to the test: it requires integrating incomplete evidence, inferring causal drivers, and committing to calibrated probabilistic judgments. Existing benchmarks are poorly suited to measuring and improving this ability. Live benchmarks provide feedback only after events resolve, so every evaluation cycle waits on the calendar; fixed-corpus evaluations return results immediately but confine research to pre-collected documents. We introduce FABench, a scalable backtesting environment for Forecasting Agents. An automated pipeline discovers events, generates questions, and resolves outcomes for any past forecast date, so the benchmark can be renewed to stay ahead of model training cutoffs; its first release poses 287 questions across nine domains. Agents research each question freely in an archive environment that combines an offline archive with live web search screened to prevent temporal leakage. Evaluating agents built on eight recent models, we find that retrieval improves the Brier score of all eight in every retrieval setting and lifts the leading agents to the market consensus on the 54 questions linked to Polymarket, with the best three scoring slightly above it. Research traces reveal recurring failures in how agents weigh and apply evidence, leaving ample room for improvement. We release our code, data, and archive environment as a foundation for community research on forecasting agents.

### Paper [122]
Title: Opening the Box Changes the Cat: LLMs Detect Chain-of-Thought Monitoring - What now?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40946
Authors: Amir Jalilifard, Marcos Medeiros Raimundo, Anderson Rocha
Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning allows large language models (LLMs) to generate intermediate reasoning traces in natural language while solving complex tasks. These traces can support safety monitoring by exposing potentially harmful plans before actions are executed. In this work, we investigate the conditions under which LLMs infer the presence of CoT monitoring from direct and indirect environmental cues and examine how this inference affects their subsequent behavior. Our observations suggest that oversight is an interactive process: while a monitor inspects a model's reasoning, traces left by monitoring interventions can allow the model to infer that its thoughts are being observed and to adapt its behavior accordingly. We show that even subtle environmental cues, such as timing-related signals from timeouts and operation durations, can reveal the presence of monitoring and trigger models to adapt their behavior. Across repeated episodes and interaction loops, we further observe that models can form statistical estimates of the monitor's effectiveness and use these estimates to guide attempts to evade detection. These findings highlight the need to evaluate CoT-based oversight under adaptive conditions, where the monitoring process itself may influence the behavior it seeks to observe.

### Paper [123]
Title: P-ContextBench: From Personal Fact Recall to Personalized Agent Behavior
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40959
Authors: Grant Nasir Brown, Abdul Basir Bari, Anmol Tadikonda, Isabel Song
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for personal tasks, yet evaluating their safe use of personal context remains difficult because such contexts are private, fragmented, dynamic, and socially situated. We introduce P-ContextBench, a realistic, verifiable, and scalable benchmark for evaluating personal-context understanding. P-ContextBench constructs population-structured, life-story-grounded digital humans from demographic seeds reflecting the U.S. population. Rather than using explicit, static profiles, P-ContextBench embeds traits, routines, goals, and constraints as implicit evidence across fragmented, evolving synthetic mobile-app records. It evaluates five capabilities: single-source fact recovery, multi-source fact recovery, temporal state tracking, inference of higher-level personal patterns from routine behavior, and safe handling of requests conflicting with persona-specific constraints through compliance, warning, clarification, or refusal. A multi-stage validation pipeline checks persona plausibility, context recoverability, information leakage, evidence preservation, test-case grounding, and human-judge agreement. We instantiate P-ContextBench with 100 personas, 10,088 hidden facts, 18,261 app-trace fragments, and 4,810 verified test cases. The pipeline supports configurable generation across scales and demographic groups. Cost-effective state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs relevant to personal agents achieve below 60% accuracy on temporal tracking, higher-level pattern inference, and constraint-aware action-boundary decisions, with fewer than 10% of test cases answered correctly across all three repeated runs. Even a high-performing SOTA model remains inconsistent on these capabilities. P-ContextBench provides measurable targets for improving the accuracy, reliability, and safety of LLM-based personal agents.

### Paper [124]
Title: Nedimo: An Evidence-Grounded Multilingual Pipeline for AI-Generated Sleep Stories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40970
Authors: Georgios Makridis, Stefanos Kampouris
Abstract: Generative audio systems can produce large libraries of personalized bedtime content, but ordinary media objectives (retention, completion, and novelty) are poorly aligned with the goal of supporting a transition toward sleep. Direct evidence for narrated bedtime audio in adults is mixed, and a recent active-controlled trial did not support such audio as a standalone treatment for sleep disturbance. We translate this uncertain evidence into conservative engineering requirements and present a multilingual pipeline for generating and narrating low-arousal sleep stories. The implemented prototype separates deterministic planning from large language model generation, represents content and delivery parameters explicitly, shapes each script through a four-phase attention taper, validates structural and lexical constraints, performs a bounded error-aware retry, and quarantines unresolved outputs. Provider-agnostic text-to-speech synthesis is followed by paragraph-aware joining, two-pass loudness normalization, and a publication gate that re-measures the saved, encoded file against fixed loudness, loudness-range, and true-peak limits. The product data model records stop position, replay, early abandonment, and completion while treating these events as ambiguous behavioral proxies rather than clinical outcomes. We contribute an evidence-to-requirement mapping, a traceable guardrail architecture with explicit acceptance thresholds, technical evaluation criteria, and a prospective protocol for testing sleep-onset effects. No clinical efficacy is claimed; the system is positioned as a relaxation and attentional-support tool whose benefit and possible reliance must be evaluated empirically.

### Paper [125]
Title: The psychophysics of affect in large language models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40985
Authors: Joseph Marks (NatWest Group, London, UK), Marius C. Vollberg (University of Zurich, Zurich, Switzerland, Swiss Center for Affective Sciences, Geneva, Switzerland)
Abstract: Good decisions often require considering how outcomes will feel. Yet large language models (LLMs) increasingly enter decision workflows, despite little being known about how they map outcomes to feelings or use those mappings in choice. We tested how LLMs anticipated gambling or stock-trading outcomes would feel and whether they incorporated these ratings into choice. When outcomes were rated in isolation, ratings exhibited diminishing sensitivity to magnitude, resembling human value functions; but models rated gains more intensely than equivalent losses, an inversion of canonical loss aversion we call silicon gain amplification. When outcomes were rated together, enabling direct comparison, ratings linearised. In choice, the gain-loss asymmetry largely disappeared, and models' own ratings did not reliably explain their choices better than monetary utility did. These findings reveal malleable mappings from outcomes to affect, raising questions about whether and how agentic LLMs should incorporate anticipated feelings into decisions.

### Paper [126]
Title: Can Personal Requirements Become Supervision for Planning?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40992
Authors: Junwei Wu, Runze Yan, Yuzhang Xie, Hanqi Luo, Ziwei Dong, Jiaying Lu, Xiao Hu, Carl Yang
Abstract: Managing multiple chronic conditions through diet requires satisfying disease- and medication-specific requirements jointly across meals and days while preserving established eating habits. These requirements are public in clinical guidelines and drug labels, yet turning them into a feasible plan for one person remains difficult for language-model agents. In our dietitian-approved weekly planning evaluation built from NHANES, thirteen models, from medically fine-tuned 8B models to GPT-6.1 Sol, pass fewer than 3% of complete plans through direct generation. Explicit rules, higher reasoning effort, or model-directed tools leave GPT-6.1 Sol at no more than 8%. We present Noura, which pairs LLM planning with executable personal specifications instantiated from public clinical guidance and individual health and dietary profiles. During planning, the specification grounds foods, optimizes portions, checks the complete multi-day plan, and guides revision. Our key insight is that a specification that rejects a plan can also determine what to learn from it. Noura turns executed plans and contextually credited revisions into supervision for compact models, recovering targets even from trajectories that never achieve complete success. The same specification supplies supervised targets, preferences, and rewards for post-training. On 4,993 held-out participants, Noura-SFT raises Qwen3-14B's complete-plan pass rate from 24.0% to 72.5% within the same execution loop, cuts rule violations by 97.5% with no observed critical failures, and improves dietary quality. The locally hosted 14B planner incurs fewer rule violations than its GPT-6 Luna teacher. A matched comparison shows that executed targets reduce rule violations by 41% over model-written proposals. Across four Qwen, Phi, and Llama backbones, self-training alone reduces rule violations by 41-61% on a 1,249-person population subset.

### Paper [127]
Title: Violet Teaming in Practice: Lessons from a Pilot Expert Workshop on Governing Complex AI Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41018
Authors: Steph Batalis, Kait Connors, Ben C. Snyder, Vikram Venkatram, Josh Wentzel
Abstract: Violet teaming has been proposed as a method to integrate technical, policy, and societal considerations into the evaluation of emerging technologies. Here, we document a pilot expert workshop used to explore violet teaming as a governance methodology for biodefense-relevant AI capabilities and describe observations from the exercise. The workshop used a hypothetical technology scenario across three sequential stages - capability development, misuse potential, and implementation - while incorporating technical, security, operational, and policy perspectives. The sequential format provided an integrated way to examine potential benefits, misuse risks, and real-world implementation challenges, rather than considering these issues in isolation. The exercise surfaced non-obvious misuse pathways, expanded potential interventions beyond purely technical safeguards, and highlighted operational and policy constraints that could affect implementation. This report summarizes the workshop design, outputs, and lessons learned for applying violet teaming to the evaluation of complex, dual-use technologies.

### Paper [128]
Title: ReplayRCA: Measuring Post-hoc Diagnosis and Live Oversight of Agent Failures by Counterfactual Replay
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41025
Authors: Jeremy Canale, Fabien Soulis
Abstract: When a language-model agent fails a multi-step task, can another model find the decisive mistake and fix it with a single action? We introduce ReplayRCA, a benchmark that scores such diagnoses by intervention rather than by agreement with annotations: the environment is restored to the state preceding the chosen step, the proposed action replaces the agent's own, and the same agent continues. The score, causal repair, is the continuation success rate minus that of a paired placebo that replays the agent's original action through the same mechanism. Two tracks share this protocol: a post-hoc autopsy, where the diagnoser sees the whole failed run and is told that it failed, and live oversight, where a supervisor watches the run unfold and may intervene once, before an irreversible action, without knowing whether the run will fail. Tasks come from OpsWorld, a deterministic, procedurally generated back-office environment with six difficulty levels, the last of which is multimodal (QR codes, handwriting). On 62 calibrated systematic failures, the best of 13 frontier models (Grok 4.7) reaches a causal repair of +51.3 points; the top eight models are statistically indistinguishable, the flagship models of the two largest vendors rank 8th and 12th, and 5 items are repaired by no model. Forcing a strong model to intervene at the last step costs it 17.1 points, so the last-step shortcut of annotation-based attribution benchmarks does not transfer. On 87 runs, the two best live supervisors are statistically tied and one of them is a small open-weight model (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash); the supervisor that is among the best post-hoc diagnosers (GPT-6.1 Sol) is the weakest live one (-14.5 points on the same runs, p = 0.002), so post-hoc diagnostic performance can be a poor proxy for live oversight. The environment and the evaluation code are released at https://github.com/jeremy1392/RCAreplay.

### Paper [129]
Title: Capability-Grounded Agentic Planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41040
Authors: Shikha Shrestha, William Yeoh, Ning Zhang, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik
Abstract: LLM agents serve natural-language requests through tools, data sources, and other agents, and even simple requests often need several interdependent calls. Typically, the LLM picks these calls by trial and error, with no guarantee of an executable plan. We present Capability-Grounded Agentic Planning (CGAP), which instead reasons about what a request requires and what tools provide. CGAP compiles the tool catalog deterministically into a PDDL model, uses an LLM to turn the request into goals, and solves the model with a classical planner. Since tool outputs are unknown in advance, the model is abstract, tracking which inputs are available, not their values. During execution, an LLM adds entity-specific calls that abstract plans omit, and deterministic checks reject any that break the plan. Every argument is drawn from a cited source, such as a conversation span or an earlier tool response, and each requested result counts as delivered only after an LLM verifier checks it against the request. With three LLMs on ToolSandbox, ComplexFuncBench, BFCL multi-turn, and $\tau^2$-bench retail, CGAP outperforms a standard tool-calling agent by up to 28.9 points.

### Paper [130]
Title: LanG 2.0: Energy-Efficient Agentic AI for Semi-Automated Security Operations at Scale
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41072
Authors: Anes Abdennebi, Nadjia Kara, Laaziz Lahlou, Hakima Ould-Slimane
Abstract: Security operations centers (SOCs) need to turn terabytes of alerts, logs and flows into trusted, affordable decisions to unveil hidden threats. We present LanG 2.0, an agentic system that detects threats, correlates evidence and writes rules, investigating whether small local models can replace frontier models affordably and safely when structural safeguards, not model accuracy, provide the guarantees. It incorporates entity-anchored correlation, human approval of rule changes and a tamper-evident ledger. Tests span eight flow datasets from five networks, real XDR alerts and multistage campaigns. Against its predecessor, campaign correlation precision rises from 18.75% to 51.81%, and rule acceptance from at most 60.00% to 98.33%. Against Claude Opus 5.5, scoring flows against each network's normal traffic detects attacks on unseen networks at 73.42% F1 without false positives, versus 58.10% with 49.00% false positives, for at least 650 times less energy. Finetuned models statistically match it on alert classification and rule writing for 5.6 to 59 times less energy. The work demonstrates LanG's practical scalability, processing 3.33 million records in 6.4 minutes, over 30 million hourly, and its safeguards resisted prompt injection, label poisoning and ledger tampering, so small local models can carry most SOC detection affordably and safely.

### Paper [131]
Title: BABA-Bench: Evaluating Long-Horizon Patient-Advocacy Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41076
Authors: Shriram Vasudevan, Videet Mehta, Connor Sweeney
Abstract: An LLM agent that helps a patient get care must do two things. It must reach the correct outcome, and it must follow the necessary procedure. For example, the agent must get the patient's permission before it shares the patient's records. We introduce BABA-Bench, a benchmark of 47 interactive worlds. We built each world from a de-identified, real patient advocacy case that expert advocates reviewed. In each world, the agent must find hidden information and coordinate insurers, clinics, suppliers, pharmacies, and community organizations. The engine checks each action at the time the agent does it. It checks if the agent has permission, if the agent has evidence for the action, and if the action is prohibited. We evaluated 14 frontier models with three runs on each world, for a total of 1,974 runs. Agents often reached the correct outcome by an invalid route. Of the 921 runs that reached all required outcomes, 254 (28%) did not follow the necessary procedure. Claude Opus 5 reached all required outcomes in 81.6% of its runs, but only 52.5% of its runs passed all checks. When we gave two models a verified route, their pass rates increased from 29.8% to 91.5% and from 44.0% to 97.2%. For these models, the main problem is to find a route, not to execute it.

### Paper [132]
Title: Optimization Techniques for Solving the Minimum Red--Blue Separating Set Problem with an Application to Human--AI Text Separation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41081
Authors: Marko Djukanovi\'c, Sta\v{s}a Vuji\v{c}i\'c Stankovi\'c, \v{Z}iga Zebec, Christian Blum
Abstract: We study the so-called Minimum Red''Blue Separating Set Problem (MRBSSP) which arises from computational geometry. In particular, we propose a compact Set-Cover ILP formulation, safe preprocessing rules, a Greedy Randomized Adaptive Search Procedure (GRASP), and two exact approaches: one is based on Logic-Based Benders Decomposition (LBBD) approach, and the second represents a novel Branch-and-Benders-Cut (BBC) approach. These two exact approaches are compared to ILP--alongside GRASP and the Variable Neighborhood Search (VNS) from literature. Experiments on 250 random graphs with up to 500 vertices reveal complementary strengths: ILP yields the best feasible solutions, LBBD provides the strongest lower bounds and smallest mean optimality gap, and BBC certifies optimality most frequently. We further introduce an NLP application distinguishing human- and AI-generated texts through vocabulary-based graph features. On 42 instances, BBC achieves the smallest mean optimality gap, certifying 16 optima, while GRASP outperforms VNS. Structural analysis of optimal vocabulary separators identifies 113 mandatory and 151 replaceable features across 272 tests, highlighting substantial variability among optimal solutions. Overall, our results demonstrate the effectiveness of complementary optimization strategies and the interpretability of optimal vocabulary-based discrimination.

### Paper [133]
Title: Payroll Fraud Detection in Synthetic HR/ERP Graphs: An Inductive Comparison of Rules, Path Features, and Graph Neural Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41085
Authors: Xiaobo Chen, Genliang Zhu, Ziyuan Wang, Chu Wang
Abstract: Payroll-fraud screening requires finding hidden employee--payee links while distinguishing suspicious relationships from legitimate ones. We formulate this problem as inductive link prediction and entity ranking on synthetic human-resources and enterprise resource planning (HR/ERP) graphs, with unseen organizations and a matched benign twin for every planted case. An original study on 24 held-out organizations and a prospectively frozen extension compare analyst rules, explicit path features and graph neural networks (GNNs). A tuned gradient-boosted model on chained-join features achieved the highest overall link average precision (AP; 0.823), above the GraIL-style GNN (0.663); its 4-hop AP was 0.646 versus 0.272. Thus, the observed multi-hop gain is not specific to GNNs. On a new synthetic cohort with 2.0% positive entities, chained-join boosting again led (AP 0.798); the relational entity GNN also exceeded record-aware rules (0.707 versus 0.504). At a 2% review budget, their precision was 0.695 versus 0.602. Under 40% failed identifier joins, the entity GNN ranked above chained-join boosting (AP 0.712 versus 0.683, descriptively). Structure-only auditing exposed a shortcut in the initial generator; matched twins reduced the audited twin-pool AUROC to 0.493--0.514. These results separate multi-hop candidate discovery, record-based discrimination and review prioritization within an explicit synthetic evaluation design. Code and data are available from the authors on request.

### Paper [134]
Title: CIRCE Divines LLM Task Complexity from Constraint Structure
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41089
Authors: Chenhao Hong, Jiakai Xu, Kaushiki Singh, Yusen Zhang, Eugene Wu
Abstract: Estimating whether a problem is too complex for a large language model (LLM) before the model attempts it can guide model routing, how to allocate test-time compute, and suggest the need for new tools to aid the agent. While existing estimators rely on the problem text, LLM-annotated task difficulty labels, or signals obtained after a model attempts to solve the problem, such as its hidden states, none explicitly leverage the constraint structure inherent in the problem: the variables to assign and how the constraints over them interact.
  We introduce CIRCE, which extracts a problem's structure by using a cheap LLM to construct a Constraint Intermediate Representation that deterministically compiles into the constraint graph. Across seven reasoning benchmarks (including SAT, logic, and math) and four LLMs, even a randomly initialized GNN encoder improves predictions of LLM response correctness, reducing negative log-likelihood from 0.465 to 0.423 and increasing AUROC from 0.691 to 0.727 relative to the strongest baseline for each metric.

### Paper [135]
Title: Verifying Text-to-SQL Queries under Changing Business Semantics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41090
Authors: Genliang Zhu, Xiaobo Chen, Ziyuan Wang, Chu Wang
Abstract: In text-to-SQL and AI-agent analytics, successful SQL execution does not establish semantic correctness under changing business definitions. We study revalidation of fixed queries after changes to refund attribution, tax treatment, eligible populations and exchange-rate dates. An executable verification profile binds the requested revision and knowledge cutoff to legal counterexample data and exact monetary comparison. A preregistered confirmation on 32 newly authored revision families and two local producer bundles retains 192 query-definition cells: 144 nonconforming, 36 finite-suite agreeing and 12 unresolved. Of the nonconforming cells, 63 agree on the current snapshot. At an eight-ledger budget, obligation-guided checking detects 137 discrepancies versus 127 for generic ordering, a paired difference of 6.9 percentage points (family bootstrap 95% interval [2.1, 13.6]; exact family sign-flip p=0.0156). Every ledger arm rejects zero of the 36 agreeing cells. Current-obligation and equal-weight scoring match the guided result, identifying coverage rather than revision-specific priority as the observed source of benefit. Controlled assays, a natural-query pilot and repeated samples provide separate mechanism and robustness evidence. A legal discrepancy refutes conformance; agreement remains restricted to the recorded suite. The study establishes an auditable business-semantic revalidation profile within its authored schema and local-model scope.

### Paper [136]
Title: QUAL: Quantile Alignment Improves Zero-Shot Time-Series Foundation Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41100
Authors: Junhyeok Kang, Sangjun Han, Jun Seo, Seunghan Lee, Jaehoon Lee, Dongwan Kang, Minjae Kim, Sungdong Yoo, Tae Yoon Lim, Hwanil Choi, Soonyoung Lee, Wonbin Ahn
Abstract: Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) provide zero-shot forecasts for unseen series, but their accuracy varies across datasets. Combining several models can reduce this variability, yet zero-shot deployment provides no target labels for selecting or weighting them. We find that a model pool is useful not only as a final ensemble, but also as a probability reference for transforming each member forecast. Aligning quantiles through this pool-relative reference improves every member forecast in aggregate. Motivated by this finding, we propose QUAL (Quantile alignment), a simple label-free framework for improving and combining diverse TSFMs. QUAL first produces aligned member forecasts, then applies Huber aggregation and rearrangement to obtain a robust combined forecast without training or model modification. On GIFT-Eval, quantile alignment reduces average member MASE and CRPS by 7.4% and 9.8%. The full framework remains robust when weak forecasters enter a candidate pool whose quality cannot be verified in advance. Code is available at https://github.com/junhyeokkang/QUAL.

### Paper [137]
Title: Differential rank preservation manifold learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41107
Authors: Henry Han, Luyu Niu, Diane Li, Jiacun Wang
Abstract: Manifold learning methods like t-SNE and UMAP preserve local neighborhoods, but they do not preserve the ranking of the neighbors inside them. Two embeddings can return the same neighbor set while disagreeing on which point is closest. This loss of order degrades downstream applications that value the order in the embedding. We propose Smooth Rank t-SNE (SR-tSNE), which adds a differentiable rank term to the standard t-SNE loss. To enable efficient training, this term uses a sigmoid rank approximation to explicitly force a point's true high-dimensional neighbors to outrank unrelated intruders and retain their original order. We also introduce Ordinal DLP (O-DLP), an extension of the Degree of Locality Preservation (DLP) metric that credits a neighbor only when it returns at its original rank. Across eight benchmarks spanning image, text, speech, remote-sensing and single-cell data, SR-tSNE outperforms 12 baselines in rank preservation and neighborhood overlap. Ablation studies demonstrate our rank term alone boosts O-DLP by 5% to 28%, a gain not achievable using standard pairwise loss. This advantage transfers to downstream applications. SR-tSNE achieves the best label-transfer performance across seven vision datasets. For cell-type discovery across six single-cell datasets, it ranks first in 17 of 18 dataset-metric combinations, whether clustering resolution is selected with or without cell-type labels. In intraday trading, SR-tSNE achieves higher returns, lower risk, and lower turnover than the baselines. To our knowledge, SR-tSNE is the first t-SNE-family method with explicit order preservation.

### Paper [138]
Title: Calibration of Large Model Quantization based on HiFloat4 Format
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41112
Authors: Jing Huang, Yu Cheng, Ziwei Yu, Lan Zhao, Sitan Li, Guangxiu Yao, Xin Wang, Yuanyong Luo, Hengyi Liang, Ting Li, Bo Liu, Hu Liu, Michael Bi Mi, Heng Liao
Abstract: Quantization of Large Language Models (LLMs) has be- coming increasingly important for reducing the computa- tional costs and resource requirements for practical deploy- ment.Growinginterestinthisareahasmotivatedthecommu- nity to move beyond 8-bit quantization towards 4-bit preci- sion, leading to the development of customized 4-bit formats such as MXFP4 and NVFP4. Recently, HiFloat4 (HiF4) has emerged as a competitive alternative which adopts a Block Floating-Point(BFP)formatwithathree-levelscalinghierar- chy, thereby achieving a significantly larger dynamic range. While quantization can substantially reduce memory usage and accelerate inference, it frequently introduces accuracy degradation. Calibration is therefore essential for narrowing theperformancegapbetweenlow-precisionquantizedmodels and their full-precision counterparts. Nevertheless, existing calibration methods are mainly designed and validated us- ing integer-precision formats, such as INT4, lacking support for HiF4 due to its hierarchical BFP structure. In this paper, weinvestigatethreecalibrationapproaches,namelyHiGPTQ, HiAutoRound, and HiFlatQuant, to further enhance model quantizationbasedontheHiF4format.Extensiveexperiments demonstratethatallthreeproposedapproachesimproveinfer- ence accuracy compared with direct HiF4 conversion, reduc- ing the gap between HiF4 and BF16 to less than 1% and out- performing the state-of-the-art NVFP4 format across various models and downstream tasks.

### Paper [139]
Title: Predicting Human-Gain Curves under Local Proxy Optimization from Repeated Ratings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41114
Authors: Moonwon Choi, Kisung Nam, Seunggeun Lee
Abstract: Optimizing a proxy objective can improve human-perceived quality, but the resulting gain can vary with optimization strength. Average proxy-human correlation alone does not reveal how human-perceived quality changes as optimization strength increases. We propose PACE (Proxy Alignment and Curvature Expansion), a pre-optimization diagnostic that uses repeated human ratings to predict the human-gain curve induced by a fixed proxy. The method models proxy optimization as a KL-regularized exponential tilt. At KL radius $d$, the local gain is approximated by $A\sqrt{d}+Bd$, where $A$ measures first-order proxy-human alignment and $B$ captures the leading second-order correction along the proxy-optimization path. We estimate $A$ and $B$ from repeated human ratings on calibration items and test the resulting gain-curve prediction on held-out evaluation items. Across SummEval, analysis-eligible WMT MQM clusters, an OpenMEVA-MAGS ROC/WP repeated-rating slice, and a GPT-4o judge proxy experiment, PACE consistently reduces held-out gain-prediction error relative to $A$-only, zero-gain, and reliability-only baselines. With the same pre-pool information, it is competitive with flexible proxy-conditional calibration, and a LoRA-DPO experiment provides a modest bridge to actual optimization. Transfer experiments across datasets and evaluation slices show that source coefficients are useful priors, but target calibration further improves prediction. The resulting diagnostic is local and one-sided: it characterizes gentle optimization along the positive tilt branch of a fixed item pool.

### Paper [140]
Title: Confident Fools or Careful Searchers? A Behavioural Study of When LLMs Search Beyond Their Static Internal Memory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41124
Authors: Nipun D. Pathirage, Kwan Srijomkwan
Abstract: Agentic LLM assistants must repeatedly decide whether to answer from memory or to call a tool such as web search. Unnecessary searches cost latency and money; missing ones yield confident but unsupported answers. Prior work mostly measures verbalised confidence or trains new adaptive-retrieval methods, and rarely audits the search decisions that deployed products actually make. We introduce a benchmark of 400 multiple-choice questions that combines widely known inside-knowledge (IK) questions, which should be answered directly, with outside-knowledge (OK) questions about facts from news published after the models' training cut-offs (to our knowledge), which should trigger search or, without tools, abstention. Each item offers an abstain option, and two action-level scores, NAS and KIS, reward searching on OK questions and restraint on IK questions. Across five commercial LLMs with and without their vendors' web search, GPT-5 mini and Grok 4 Fast search on over 90% of OK but only 1% of IK questions, whereas Mistral Small searches on just 20% of OK questions, often abstaining instead of using its tool; one product's search indicator proved unusable. Searching is partly triggered by explicit temporal cues: on uncued OK questions, the NAS of the two selective models falls from about 0.72 to 0.57--0.58. The scores thus measure the search triggering of specific products, not metacognition in a strict sense.

### Paper [141]
Title: When Should AI Agents Ask? Clarification Requests Under Limited Human Attention
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41129
Authors: Hanzhao Wang, Shengjie Liu, Zhengchao Wang
Abstract: AI agents increasingly carry out delegated tasks in parallel, with one person overseeing several of them at once. In practice, an agent often lacks information its task requires, such as an unstated preference, constraint, or specification. It can ask the human to clarify, or it can search for the information itself, for example in past records, which takes time and effort and may fail. In this paper, we study how to coordinate these decisions across several agents that share one human with limited attention. We build a stylized model in which each agent can search, ask, or continue the parts of its task that do not depend on the missing information, and we formulate the problem as a dynamic program.
