Moreover, our defended model shows no noticeable reduction in benign utility in nine benchmarks covering agentic tool calling, instruction following, science, and math. We release our $\href{//github.com/Sizhe-Chen/SecPO}{\text{code}}$, $\href{//huggingface.co/datasets/Sizhe-Chen/Qwen3.6-27B-Instruct-SecPO-trainset}{\text{training data}}$ (with optimized injections from AT), and $\href{//huggingface.co/Sizhe-Chen/Qwen3.6-27B-Instruct-SecPO}{\text{model}}$.

### Paper [281]
Title: AutoAnalysis: An automated, self-updating meta-analysis of human-AI collaboration
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42001
Authors: Michelle Vaccaro, Abdullah Almaatouq, Thomas W. Malone
Abstract: As generative artificial intelligence (AI) becomes more capable and widely used, understanding when and how humans should work with these systems is becoming increasingly important. However, the empirical evidence in this space offers conflicting recommendations, which leaves individuals and organizations without clear guidance on when to combine human and AI capabilities. Existing meta-analyses can help reconcile these findings, but they require substantial manual effort and cannot readily incorporate new evidence as it emerges. To address this challenge, we introduce AutoAnalysis: an automated, self-updating meta-analysis of when humans and AI work better together, and when they work better alone. AutoAnalysis' most recent run synthesizes 1,955 effect sizes from 420 papers. Across this body of work, we found that, on average, human-AI combinations performed significantly better than humans alone, but they performed significantly worse than the better of humans or AI alone. These findings highlight the important distinction between improving human performance and achieving human-AI synergy and underscore the need to evaluate collaboration against both human and AI performance alone. As new studies emerge, AutoAnalysis will integrate their findings into the meta-analysis, providing cumulative evidence about when humans and AI perform better together and how human-AI performance varies across AI agents, tasks, and processes.

### Paper [282]
Title: Know When the Equation Is Wrong: Structural Uncertainty for LLM-Guided Symbolic Regression under Extrapolation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42024
Authors: Mostafa Cham, Bayu Adhi Tama, Jianwu Wang
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can help discover mathematical equations from data, but most LLM-guided symbolic regression methods focus on a single best equation without quantifying uncertainty over alternative functional forms. This is challenging because distinct equations can fit the observed data similarly while producing substantially different extrapolations. Our experiments on the LLM-SRBench dataset show that standard conformal intervals achieve nominal in-distribution coverage but frequently fail under extrapolation. To tackle this challenge, we introduce LLM-SR-UQ, a framework that retains competing equation structures and uses their disagreement to characterize uncertainty about the underlying functional form. We extend the standard fixed-width conformal approach to quantify structural intervals, which measure disagreement among candidate equations and identify where prediction intervals should widen. Extensive experiments show our framework substantially improves coverage attainment by incorporating structural disagreement while preserving in-distribution coverage. Our approach also shows good generalizability when it is applied to other symbolic-regression methods.

### Paper [283]
Title: There Is No Free Loop: When Does Looping a Trained Model Pay for Its Inference Compute?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42030
Authors: Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker, Pashmina Cameron
Abstract: Looping re-applies a block of layers so that each token passes through the block more than once. Adding a loop to a trained model is attractive because it leaves the parameter count, and hence the disk size, the memory footprint and the training cost unchanged. Unfortunately, every generated token pays for the loop. Re-applying m of N layers makes each token about (N+m)/N times as expensive. Loops that a model learns during large-scale pretraining justify this price, but whether loops added after pretraining justify this cost remains unclear. We compare a looped model with two simple baselines. We first compare it with the same model trained identically without the loop (equal parameters). Secondly, we compare it with the best unlooped use of the same inference floating-point operations (equal FLOPs). Across Qwen3 and Qwen3.5 models from 0.8B to 9B parameters, only the looped 0.8B model beats both baselines. At 4B and 9B, the looped model does not beat the first baseline, i.e. the same model trained without the loop. This is not because the benchmarks are too easy, since on the same benchmarks the unlooped 9B model still scores up to 13 points higher than the 4B model, showing that there is ample room for improvement. Introducing loops in an already pretrained model therefore only pays for small models with a fixed parameter budget.

### Paper [284]
Title: Learning Clean Generative Processes from Noisy Measurements
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42036
Authors: Fabian Kruse, Lorenzo Baldassari, Ivan Dokmani\'c
Abstract: We study how to learn a generative model of clean images using only noisy observations. Diffusion processes and their generalizations to non-Gaussian noise require posterior predictions at every noise level. We first show that it is possible to learn these predictions from independent noisy views using Noise2Noise regression, but only at or above the noise level of the training data. To learn the predictions below that level, needed to generate clean images, we impose consistency: a prediction should equal its expectation after continuing the sampling process. We derive conditions under which consistency uniquely determines the continuation to the clean image distribution and verify them for Poisson, binomial, Gamma, and negative-binomial measurements. Experiments on CIFAR-10, photon-counting microscopy, and synthetic aperture radar show that the required posterior predictions can be learned from noisy views alone and that the consistency relation repairs the continuation beyond the noise level of the data, which severely deteriorates without consistency.

### Paper [285]
Title: Near-Optimal Second-Order Convex Finite-Sum Optimization under Individual Smoothness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42040
Authors: Xinliang Zhang, Lesi Chen, Zonghan Li, Jingzhao Zhang
Abstract: We study the stochastic second-order oracle complexity of solving smooth convex finite-sum optimization with $n$ components. Very recently, Tsedenov \& Kovalev (2026) showed that the minimax optimal complexity under averaged smoothness and convexity is $\tilde{\Theta}(n + n^{6/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ by establishing nearly matching upper and lower bounds. In this paper, we show that individual smoothness and convexity provably enable further acceleration, where the lower complexity bound is $\Omega(n+ n^{5/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ under this stronger assumption. Then, by establishing a novel second-order self-bounding inequality, we design a new Taylor-corrected lazy Newton method, which can lead to a better complexity of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n + n^{5/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ by applying the optimal Monteiro-Svaiter acceleration, matching our lower bound up to only logarithmic factors.

### Paper [286]
Title: CRoS: Causality Reward on Scrambled CoTs Improves Accuracy and Reasoning Faithfulness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42054
Authors: Xiao Yu, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Zaid Khan, Hyunji Lee, Mohit Bansal
Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become the standard method for training language models to reason. However, RLVR typically relies solely on outcome correctness, which does not guarantee that the CoT causally contributes to the final answer. Without this connection, outcome rewards may fail to reinforce high-quality reasoning. Existing causality metrics assess CoT causality by perturbing the CoT, such as deleting reasoning steps or injecting errors, and measuring changes in the final answer. However, these perturbations inevitably alter information in each step. Consequently, high causality scores may reflect sensitivity to information changes introduced by perturbation rather than true causality. Such confounding effects further induce undesirable reasoning behaviors when these metrics are used as training rewards, including generating less informative CoTs and reducing exploration and self-correction. To address this problem, we propose CRoS (Causality Reward on Scrambled CoTs), a novel CoT causality metric that perturbs the order of reasoning steps without altering content, thereby preserving information within each step. On Reasoning Gym with Gemma-3-4B, CRoS outperforms existing causality and outcome rewards in out-of-domain accuracy, CoT quality, and CoT faithfulness. Specifically, CRoS achieves 46.8% accuracy on five unseen logic and games datasets, outperforming the strongest baseline (43.8%). It also achieves better CoT quality, consistently outperforming baselines in CoT-Pass@K from K=1 to 128, averaging 43.4% vs. 41.4%. For CoT faithfulness, CRoS achieves a hint acknowledgment score of 34.0% vs. 20.2%.

### Paper [287]
Title: Subquadratic Attention with a Hierarchical Tree-Structured Indexer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42112
Authors: Leon Lufkin, Cutter Dawes, Alexander Speicher, Kamesh Krishnamurthy, Tom\'as Figliolia, Beren Millidge
Abstract: The quadratic scaling of attention is a fundamental bottleneck limiting the long-context capabilities of large language models (LLMs). A variety of approaches have attempted to circumvent this, but they either fail to match attention's expressiveness (e.g., sliding-window or linear attention variants with fixed-size state) or only achieve constant-factor speedups (e.g., sparse attention methods whose indexers themselves scale quadratically). In this paper, we derive an asymptotically subquadratic sparse attention method based on a hierarchical, tree-structured indexer, which we call Zyphra Hierarchical Attention (ZHA). ZHA constructs a tree of recursively compressed key summaries over the KV cache, which future queries traverse from the top down to retrieve a sparse subset of keys. Crucially, by iteratively growing the tree's depth, the total cost including routing and final attention grows sublinearly in sequence length, resulting in overall subquadratic (but superlinear) scaling for attention. We demonstrate that ZHA can be cheaply applied to existing pretrained models with only a short KL distillation training stage over a few billion tokens, and maintains performance on long-context retrieval and challenging reasoning, mathematical, and agentic coding evaluation tasks. Moreover, we show that our method is highly robust to tree geometry including very deep trees of up to 6 levels. Finally, we develop a detailed performance model and show that our approach scales subquadratically, dramatically outperforms competing methods beyond 1 million context, and ultimately has the potential to extend effective context windows to the hundreds of millions or billions of tokens.

### Paper [288]
Title: A Hybrid SMT-NRA Solver: Integrating 2D Cell-Jump-Based Local Search, MCSAT and OpenCAD
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00557
Authors: Tianyi Ding, Haokun Li, Xinpeng Ni, Bican Xia, Tianqi Zhao
Abstract: In this paper, we propose a hybrid framework for Satisfiability Modulo the Theory of Nonlinear Real Arithmetic (SMT-NRA for short). First, we introduce a two-dimensional cell-jump move, called \emph{2D-cell-jump}, generalizing the key operation, cell-jump, of the local search method for SMT-NRA. In contrast to the existing cell-jump, which updates assignments exclusively along a given line, the 2D-cell-jump enables search over an arbitrarily selected plane. Then, we propose an extended local search framework, named \emph{2D-LS} (following the existing local search framework, LS, for SMT-NRA). Finally, we present a hybrid framework for SMT-NRA integrating 2D-LS, MCSAT and OpenCAD, to improve search efficiency through information exchange. The experimental results demonstrate improvements in local search performance, highlighting the effectiveness of the proposed methods.

### Paper [289]
Title: Ax-Prover: A Deep Reasoning Agentic Framework for Theorem Proving in Mathematics and Quantum Physics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12787
Authors: Benjamin Breen, Marco Del Tredici, Jacob McCarran, Javier Aspuru Mijares, Weichen Winston Yin, Kfir Sulimany, Jacob M. Taylor, Frank H. L. Koppens, Dirk Englund
Abstract: We present Ax-Prover, a multi-agent system for automated theorem proving in Lean that can solve problems across diverse scientific domains and operate either autonomously or collaboratively with human experts. To achieve this, Ax-Prover approaches scientific problem solving through formal proof generation, a process that demands both creative reasoning and strict syntactic rigor. Ax-Prover meets this challenge by equipping Large Language Models (LLMs), which provide knowledge and reasoning, with Lean tools via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which ensure formal correctness. To evaluate its performance as an autonomous prover, we benchmark our approach against frontier LLMs and specialized prover models on two public math benchmarks and on two Lean benchmarks we introduce in the fields of abstract algebra and quantum theory. On public datasets, Ax-Prover is competitive with state-of-the-art provers, while it largely outperforms them on the new benchmarks. This shows that, unlike specialized systems that struggle to generalize, our tool-based agentic theorem prover approach offers a generalizable methodology for formal verification across diverse scientific domains. Furthermore, we demonstrate Ax-Prover's assistant capabilities in a practical use case, showing how it enabled an expert mathematician to formalize the proof of a complex cryptography theorem.

### Paper [290]
Title: ThinkSafe: Self-Generated Safety Alignment for Reasoning Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23143
Authors: Seanie Lee, Sangwoo Park, Yumin Choi, Gyeongman Kim, Minki Kang, Jihun Yun, Sung Ju Hwang, Dongmin Park, Jongho Park
Abstract: Large reasoning models achieve strong performance through reinforcement learning but can suffer safety degradation. External teacher distillation can restore safety yet may compromise reasoning performance. We formalize safety realignment as a KL projection: the student's native filter-accepted distribution is uniquely KL-optimal, while teacher targets incur excess KL when they differ. Guided by this analysis, we propose ThinkSafe, which generates safety-alignment data from the frozen student without external teachers. Refusal steering elicits safety reasoning, and a safety guard filters the responses before offline fine-tuning. Our analysis bounds steering-induced target distortion and gives a sufficient condition for lower average target KL than teacher distillation. Experiments on DeepSeek-R1-Distill and Qwen3 show substantial safety improvements with competitive reasoning performance. On Qwen3-0.6B, ThinkSafe achieves better safety and comparable reasoning to GRPO in about one-seventh of the wall-clock time, including data generation. Code, models, and datasets are available at https://github.com/seanie12/ThinkSafe and https://huggingface.co/Seanie-lee/collections.

### Paper [291]
Title: SoK: Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): Taxonomy, Architectures, Evaluation, and Research Directions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07379
Authors: Saroj Mishra, Suman Niroula, Umesh Yadav, Dilip Thakur, Srijan Gyawali, Shiva Gaire
Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are increasingly evolving into agentic architectures where large language models autonomously coordinate multi-step reasoning, dynamic memory management, and iterative retrieval strategies. Despite rapid industrial adoption, current research lacks a systematic understanding of Agentic RAG as a sequential decision-making system, leading to highly fragmented architectures, inconsistent evaluation methodologies, and unresolved reliability risks. This Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) paper provides the first unified framework for understanding these autonomous systems. We formalize agentic retrieval-generation loops as finite-horizon partially observable Markov decision processes, explicitly modeling their control policies and state transitions. Building upon this formalization, we develop a comprehensive taxonomy and modular architectural decomposition that categorizes systems by their planning mechanisms, retrieval orchestration, memory paradigms, and tool-invocation behaviors. We further analyze the critical limitations of traditional static evaluation practices and identify severe systemic risks inherent to autonomous loops, including compounding hallucination propagation, memory poisoning, retrieval misalignment, and cascading tool-execution vulnerabilities. Finally, we outline key doctoral-scale research directions spanning stable adaptive retrieval, cost-aware orchestration, formal trajectory evaluation, and oversight mechanisms, providing a definitive roadmap for building reliable, controllable, and scalable agentic retrieval systems.

### Paper [292]
Title: Controllable User Simulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11519
Authors: Guy Tennenholtz, Ofer Meshi, Amir Globerson, Uri Shalit, Jihwan Jeong, Craig Boutilier
Abstract: Using offline datasets to evaluate conversational agents often fails to cover rare scenarios or to support testing new policies. This has motivated the use of controllable user simulators for targeted, counterfactual evaluation, typically implemented by prompting or fine-tuning large language models. In this work, we formalize controllable simulation as a causal inference problem. By bridging natural language evaluation with off-policy evaluation methodology, we show that the standard practice of training simulators via supervised fine-tuning on post-hoc trajectory labels yields a structurally biased model. Specifically, these labels are inextricably coupled to the data-generating behavior policy, injecting a look-ahead bias that breaks causal consistency. Furthermore, we prove that under policy shift this failure causes the variance of evaluation metrics to explode geometrically, a phenomenon we term controllability collapse. To restore causal consistency, we establish theoretical conditions for accurate simulation and propose practical training mitigations: a priori controls, step-wise dynamic controls, and direct policy-conditioned learning. Empirical evaluation confirms that while standard global controls distort conversational distributions and collapse behavioral diversity, our causally grounded simulators eliminate look-ahead bias, preserve natural variance, and exhibit robust zero-shot generalization to unseen agent behaviors.

### Paper [293]
Title: Teaching Large Language Models When Not to Know: Learning Temporal Critique for Ex-Ante Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14636
Authors: Chenlu Ding, Jiancan Wu, Yanchen Luo, Zheyuan Liu, Yancheng Yuan, Xiang Wang
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often fail to reason under temporal cutoffs: when prompted to answer from the standpoint of an earlier time, they exploit knowledge that became available only later. We study this failure through the lens of ex-ante reasoning, where a model must rely exclusively on information knowable before a cutoff. Through a systematic analysis of prompt-level interventions, we find that temporal leakage is highly sensitive to cutoff formulation and instruction placement: explicit cutoff statements outperform implicit historical framings, and prefix constraints reduce leakage more effectively than suffix constraints. These findings indicate that prompting can steer models into a temporal frame, but does not endow them with the ability to verify whether a response is temporally admissible. We further argue that supervised fine-tuning is insufficient, since ex-ante correctness is not an intrinsic property of an answer, but a relation between the answer and the cutoff. To address this gap, we propose TCFT, a Temporal Critique Fine-Tuning framework that trains models to acquire cutoff-aware temporal verification. Given a query, a cutoff, and a candidate response, TCFT teaches the model to identify post-cutoff leakage, explain temporal boundary violations, and judge temporal admissibility. Experiments with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct show that TCFT consistently outperforms prompting and SFT baselines, reducing average leakage by 41.89 and 37.79 percentage points, respectively.

### Paper [294]
Title: Generative Recursive Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19376
Authors: Junyeob Baek, Mingyu Jo, Minsu Kim, Mengye Ren, Yoshua Bengio, Sungjin Ahn
Abstract: How should future neural reasoning systems implement extended computation? Recursive Reasoning Models (RRMs) offer a promising alternative to autoregressive sequence extension by performing iterative latent-state refinement with shared transition functions. Yet existing RRMs are largely deterministic, following a single latent trajectory and converging to a single prediction. We introduce Generative Recursive reAsoning Models (GRAM), a framework that turns recursive latent reasoning into probabilistic multi-trajectory computation. GRAM models reasoning as a stochastic latent trajectory, enabling multiple hypotheses, alternative solution strategies, and inference-time scaling through both recursive depth and parallel trajectory sampling. This yields a latent-variable generative model supporting conditional reasoning via $p_\theta(y \mid x)$ and, with fixed or absent inputs, unconditional generation via $p_\theta(x)$. Trained with amortized variational inference, GRAM improves over deterministic recurrent and recursive baselines on structured reasoning and multi-solution constraint satisfaction tasks, while demonstrating an unconditional generation capability. https://ahn-ml.github.io/gram-website

### Paper [295]
Title: GENSTRAT: Toward a Science of Strategic Reasoning in Large Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23238
Authors: Vartan Shadarevian, Kia Ghods, Alex Kenich, Anany Kotawala
Abstract: Fixed game suites for evaluating strategic reasoning in large language models (LLMs) saturate and are hard to keep free of contamination. We introduce GENSTRAT, a system for evaluating the strategic capabilities of LLMs driven by procedural generation of two-player zero-sum imperfect-information betting games, allowing an evaluator to draw novel, fresh games on demand. Each game is characterized on a scale of five complexity axes (state space, temporal contingency, opponent dependence, private information, precision) and measured by Monte Carlo simulation. Using this generator, we construct a 50-game benchmark and hold a tournament of twelve frontier and open-weight models, gathering data from over 40,000 LLM vs. LLM matches. We further construct a composite complexity axis, and find that, on the eight hardest games, the gap between the best and worst model is 1.7x its value across the whole set of games. Ablations show that a higher thinking budget, disclosing the opponent's strategy, and higher reasoning matched to a game's specific demands (for private information and planning depth) were associated with higher margins for the LLM player.

### Paper [296]
Title: Physics-Audited Agentic Discovery in Scientific Machine Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07379
Authors: Diab W. Abueidda, Bilal Ahmed, Panos Pantidis, Mostafa E. Mobasher
Abstract: Agentic discovery can yield mechanics surrogates with low validation error that nonetheless violate physical requirements. We present Physics-Audited Agentic SciML (PA-SciML), a framework combining large language model agents with physics auditing. Physical requirements, encoded as contracts such as superposition, boundary conditions, and causality, are fixed before candidate search and tested on predicted fields without reference solutions, including through an adversarial search. Audit results guide later proposals and model selection but are reported separately from validation error. Single-edit tests document reusable design changes. Four solid-mechanics problems of increasing complexity, from parametric elasticity to heterogeneous stiffness fields and cyclic plasticity with combined hardening, reveal causality, zero-input, and stress-bound violations in surrogates selected by error alone. In elastodynamics, two surrogates reach nearly equal error, but only the audit-enabled one is causal by construction. Audit-enabled surrogates pass the required checks at sampled inputs while matching or exceeding audit-disabled accuracy in most comparisons.

### Paper [297]
Title: Neuro-Symbolic Meta-Policies for Temporal Knowledge-Graph Memory under Partial Observability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18368
Authors: Taewoon Kim, Vincent Fran\c{c}ois-Lavet, Michael Cochez
Abstract: Partially observable reinforcement learning requires deciding what to retain, retrieve, and forget over time. We introduce a neuro-symbolic meta-policy that learns which symbolic memory heuristic to apply at each decision point while keeping execution symbolic. Our setting uses temporal knowledge-graph memory in RoomKG, where hidden state and observations are represented as Resource Description Framework (RDF) graphs and memory is augmented with temporal RDF triple annotations. The model combines knowledge-graph encoding of memory contents with value heads for question answering, exploration, and forgetting, yielding a controller that is both adaptive and inspectable. This gives the work a direct Semantic Web grounding through RDF-based representation, annotation-compatible graph semantics, and graph-based symbolic operations over explicit memory state. On train/test room splits at long-term memory capacity of 512, the qualifier-aware StarE-GNN configuration achieves the best held-out performance among the compared symbolic, neural, and neuro-symbolic systems while preserving step-level traceability of memory-management decisions.

### Paper [298]
Title: FedPA-LoRA: Product-Aligned Framework for Mitigating Aggregation and Initialization Errors in Heterogeneous Federated LoRA
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15381
Authors: Juseok Jeon, Ramy E. Ali, Doyun Kwon, Myungbeom Her, Jinhwi Kim, Jinhyun So
Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient federated fine-tuning of large language models, but its factorization creates a tension between aggregation fidelity and local optimization continuity. Factor-wise aggregation incurs aggregation mismatch but better preserves factor continuity, whereas product-space reconstruction reduces this mismatch at the cost of greater factor-level initialization mismatch. We propose FedPA-LoRA, a product-aligned framework that addresses both mismatches and establishes convergence bounds for homogeneous and heterogeneous ranks. Clients preserve local factors across rounds to eliminate initialization mismatch and align their products with rank-specific global references to reduce client drift. The server aggregates heterogeneous-rank updates in product space to mitigate aggregation mismatch and efficiently reconstructs a rank-constrained global adapter without forming the dense aggregate. By decoupling local and reference ranks, FedPA-LoRA supports client-specific computation and communication budgets. Experiments on natural language understanding and generation tasks show consistent gains under data and resource heterogeneity, including a 6.82 percentage-point improvement in average GLUE accuracy under heterogeneous ranks. The code is available at https://github.com/Juseok-Jeon/FedPA-LoRA.

### Paper [299]
Title: From Association to Causation: Improving Retrieval Precision of Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Causal Relations and an Attention Mechanism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21702
Authors: Jing Liu, Yongxing Qi, Muchen Jiang, Chengnan Hu, Qingqing Peng, Haoming Wang, Yuqing Wang, Yang Yu, Xu Zhang, Ting Wu
Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds LLM generation on retrieved documents, but the standard terminal retrieval stage--dense-vector similarity, optionally followed by reranking--often returns documents that share keywords with the query without containing the needed information, a failure mode that grows with the knowledge base. We trace it to a conceptual gap: similarity captures only associational relations, whereas the documents that matter are linked to the query causally. We model the terminal retrieval stage with a causal graph grounded in Reichenbach's common cause principle: the keywords shared by the query and a retrieved document form a latent common cause A, and the document's residual keywords form a latent set B linking the document to the ideal output. Since a retrieved document is a collider (A -> d <- B), retrieval itself opens an associational path between the query and B, which licenses a training-free, attention-style re-scoring rule: the cosine similarity between the query embedding and the weighted centroid embedding of B. Unlike causality-enhanced RAG variants that model causal relations inside the knowledge content, our graph models the causal structure of the retrieval process itself. On a real 471-document enterprise knowledge base, the method promotes a relevant guideline from rank 6 to the top 3; on a controlled diagnostic corpus reproducing the keyword-stuffing regime, it improves the mean target rank from 2.88 to 1.25, while a trained cross-encoder reranker barely helps (2.63). Conversely, on three BEIR benchmarks the score underperforms the similarity baseline, delineating the applicability boundary: the method guards the keyword-stuffing regime of growing proprietary knowledge bases and complements neural rerankers; a corpus-level calibration gate selects the correct regime with >= 95% reliability. A fully local testbed demonstrates deployability.

### Paper [300]
Title: SIRF: A Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model for Industrial Content Risk Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752
Authors: Suwan Wu, Yumeng Lin, Pengcheng Yuan, Xiaolong Jiang
Abstract: For industrial content risk control, the real deployment constraint is not average accuracy but how much risk can be auto-handled under high precision and second-level latency. We present SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model), which internalizes a platform's complex policies, synthesized without additional human annotation via EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT), into the weights via continued pretraining (CPT), so rules are applied at high precision under an ultra-low-latency, verdict-only deployment. A controlled same-source comparison (Qwen3-8B-SFT vs. SIRF-8B-SFT, identical policy injection and verdict-only output form, differing only in policy-grounded CPT) attributes the gain to internalization: SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over the baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens without harming general ability, and among included, logprob-available models under this interface it matches or exceeds far larger systems. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer (20% more mis-penalized samples recovered) and transfers to a freezing scenario at low cost (~70% relative mis-penalization reduction).

### Paper [301]
Title: A Unified Per-Token Gating Family for On-Policy Distillation: FKL/RKL Mixing with Multi-Channel and Bias Coefficients
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11768
Authors: Suwan Wu, Yumeng Lin, Pengcheng Yuan, Xiaolong Jiang
Abstract: Per-token gating of forward/reverse KL losses has become a standard technique for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD), but existing methods such as EOPD (Jin et al., 2026) and ToDi (Jung et al., 2025) each fix a single gating signal and a single gating direction, and the two have never been compared directly. We introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) in which direction-aligned proxies of EOPD and ToDi appear as one-dimensional (1D) restrictions, and which adds multi-channel composition and an explicit bias as further degrees of freedom. On TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020) emotion and hate, with a Qwen3-32B teacher and a Qwen3-4B student, configurations in the full family reach higher accuracy than the matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells, and a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment places dynamic gating ahead of effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Because cells share training data, models, and parameter substructure, we report both counts as exploratory aggregate directional evidence rather than as independent hypothesis tests. Targeted three-seed paired replications of the nine headline comparisons singled out by that sweep -- including a third task, offensive -- are directionally consistent, but individually smaller than the single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3. We therefore present the parameterization primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD.

### Paper [302]
Title: Unified Agentic Video Editing Across Levels of Complexity and Creativity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12769
Authors: Surabhi S. Nath, Kim Ferres, Milan Petrovi\'c, Lion Schulz
Abstract: Editing is a core component of video production, requiring creative planning and decisions under multiple constraints. Here, we report methods for agentic tooling for automated video editing across three tasks varying in editorial goal, complexity and creativity, namely scene previews, video summaries and cinematic trailers. We evaluate the outputs and discuss implications for automation and agency.

### Paper [303]
Title: LazyAgent: Demand-Driven Materialization and Physical Optimization of Agentic Programs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058
Authors: Xin Heng
Abstract: Agent runtimes that plan before acting run every step as soon as it is ready. Neither these runtimes nor their benchmarks distinguish a step that is ready from a step the request needs, and the difference is large: across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, an eager runtime that stops once the goal is met spends 62.2% of its CPU on work the request never needed. LazyAgent is, to our knowledge, the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes. Its rule: run a ready step only if the current request needs it, and keep that answer exact as the plan changes. A demanded set derived from the program decides, in one traversal with no model calls, so LazyAgent spends nothing outside the request. Because demand is defined exactly, its behavior is proved: builders know before any run where demand must save and where it must tie. The bill is known before a plan runs, so LazyAgent can act as a query optimizer to choose among candidate workflows. Measured live against that eager runtime, LazyAgent saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates where a frontier LLM writes the patches, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, and 24.0% of dollars inside a live LangGraph application, with no losses. When the LLM re-plans after real code failures, it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal. On the production workflows it saves 42.0% of CPU, ties exactly where a request needs the whole graph, and adds at most 0.43% overhead. The same set steers every physical operation: projection saves 80-89% of a shared CI step, where the identical eager control saves 0.0%. Public agent benchmarks are designed the same way as eager runtimes, so they cannot show this difference. We compose our test programs from their tasks and call on industry to build benchmarks that measure how efficiently agent workflows run.

### Paper [304]
Title: Fine-Tuning VLM for Enhancing AI's Spatial Intelligence: Understanding 3D and 2D Rotations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04206
Authors: Uttamasha Monjoree, Wei Yan
Abstract: Spatial intelligence is a fundamental skill in multiple domains, such as Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM), Medicine, Architecture, and Construction. Recent studies indicate that Vision-Language Models (VLMs) still face limitations in spatial reasoning, which inhibits artificial intelligence (AI) from performing practical spatial tasks. Using multiple object-rotation datasets developed for training and evaluation, our experiments demonstrated promising improvements in both 2D and 3D rotation detection. Fine-tuned Google DeepMind-built Gemma-4 mixture-of-experts (MoE) models significantly outperformed fine-tuned Gemma-4 generalist models in predicting rotations defined by both their axes and angles. Fine-tuning also substantially improved angle estimation for 2D representation without requiring an explicit coordinate system. Furthermore, identifiable objects did not improve angle-detection accuracy; instead, objects with prominent linear features showed improved performance.

### Paper [305]
Title: Verification and Self-Improvement in Agentic AI: Foundations and Limits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10611
Authors: Chien-Ping Lu
Abstract: A self-improving agent can find accepted solutions more often without expanding the tasks its verification interface can accept. We extend task-relative verification with explicit support and transcript interfaces to distinguish search gains from changes in accepted-task coverage. A stage's native reach contains tasks accepted with default support; its closure frontier contains tasks accepted with any support the declared interface permits. A semantic containment criterion identifies the authority or exposure condition that every verified escape must violate, while guard-transfer invariance prevents a change of representation from being counted as a capability expansion. An executable Boolean-program repair agent makes the distinction concrete: over 36,864 paired runs, counterexample feedback reduces verifier calls without changing coverage, whereas an enforced permission change admits a new target with a proof excluding every old-interface program. Exact and uniformly gapped randomized verification share this account; independent majority amplification improves reliability while preserving the accepted language. Separate adaptive-selection contracts state what is additionally required to interpret a proposed successor's score as evidence of deployment improvement. Classical hierarchy results and finite audits provide supporting boundaries. The framework specifies the evidence needed to establish improved search, wider accepted capability, and reliable successor selection.

### Paper [306]
Title: From Stages to States: Unified Iterative Reasoning for MLLMs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15620
Authors: Chuang Yu, Xuefei Ma, Yuanhao Feng, Wuxiong Zeng, Bo Wang, Minghong Cai, Dongming Wu, Fei Zhou, Chen Tang, Wenwen Tong, Lewei Lu, Xiangyu Yue
Abstract: Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) substantially enhances the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) by explicitly modeling intermediate reasoning processes. However, most learning-based MCoT methods follow a stage-centric paradigm, where different reasoning behaviors are assigned to predefined stages and optimized separately. This results in fragmented reasoning and a training-inference reasoning-state gap. To address these limitations, we propose ReState, a unified iterative reasoning framework that reformulates multimodal reasoning from a stage-driven pipeline into a state-driven dynamic process. It comprises two complementary components: Unified Reasoning State Modeling (URSM) and Closed-Loop Reasoning-State Learning (CRSL). URSM unifies reasoning construction, correct reasoning preservation, and erroneous reasoning correction as reasoning-state transitions within a shared state space, enabling these heterogeneous reasoning behaviors to be learned jointly. CRSL further incorporates self-generated historical reasoning states during training and recursively updates the current state during inference, thereby mitigating the reasoning-state gap and enabling iterative self-refinement without requiring an additional reflection module, critic, or independent correction stage. Furthermore, we construct VersaCoT-10K, a high-quality CoT dataset spanning eight visual reasoning domains, which provides diverse supervision for multi-domain reasoning and reasoning-state refinement. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReState exhibits strong adaptability across different MLLM backbones and achieves strong performance on multiple public reasoning benchmarks, supporting the shift from stage-centric reasoning toward state-centric reasoning. Our data and code are available at https://restate-project.github.io

### Paper [307]
Title: WeFair: Fidelity-Aware Alignment from Human Feedback for Personalized Query Rewriting in Short-Video Search
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16927
Authors: Yingtao Ren, Aolin Li, Cheng Cheng, Huiyun Hu, Huasheng Liang, Baoxing Xie, Jun Yuan, Haijun Wu, Yiwei Fu, Chenxing Wang
Abstract: Short-video search needs compact query rewriters that recover users' personalized intent from ambiguous queries under strict latency constraints. Existing LLM-based methods have improved rewriting by using synthetic reasoning and subsequent consumption-behavioral feedback. However, overemphasizing subsequent consumption can favor engaging content over the user's immediate need, causing intent drift even when rewritten queries are grounded in behavioral history. We propose WeFair, Fidelity-Aware Alignment from Human Feedback for Personalized Query Rewriting in WeChat short-video search. WeFair uses human feedback according to how directly it reflects personalized intent, assigning feedback fidelity to a progression from demonstration to preference to verification. We first mine actual queries and user reformulations from search sessions, and then screen them for contextual support. In the demonstration stage, a supervised fine-tuning curriculum progresses from large-scale context-to-query learning to reasoning and direct prediction on human reformulation and preservation. In the preference stage, direct preference optimization learns relative preferences from subsequent consumption feedback. In the verification stage, group relative policy optimization rewards exact agreement with reformulation or preservation targets to constrain intent drift. Further analysis shows that broad supervision benefits the full curriculum supervised training, while strict verification achieves better performance than auxiliary rewards. In offline experiments, WeFair-2B outperforms strong rewriting baselines in overall accuracy and intent preservation. An online A/B test shows relative increases of 2.593% in effective video-view rate and a 2.350% relative reduction in query reformulation rate. WeFair-2B has been deployed in the WeChat short-video search system, which serves over 100 million queries per day.

### Paper [308]
Title: Does a Governed Confidence Score Know When a Text-to-SQL Answer Is Right? An Exploratory Study on BIRD and a Pre-registered Confirmation on Spider
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17256
Authors: Raghu Venkat, Tricha Anjali
Abstract: Conversational analytics lets people ask questions of records spread across many systems and get answers in plain language; it is useful only if each answer can be trusted and explained. A production system of this kind attaches a confidence score to every answer it writes over a database and uses it to show the answer, show it with a warning, or withhold it. The score's weights, caps and thresholds were set by design. This paper audits that design. On the 1,534 questions of BIRD dev, answered by GPT-5.1 with five samples per question, the designed score separates right from wrong answers weakly (AUROC 0.624) and withholds 35.7% of correct answers. Nearly all of its ranking signal comes from agreement among the samples, and most of it is lost where agreement is combined by a minimum with a checklist that drops to zero on any unsupported claim, including commentary. Before any Spider answer existed we froze and hashed a rule in which agreement with the shown answer is the score, with deterministic vetoes and deterministic and judge-based caps. On Spider dev AUROC rose from 0.566 to 0.725 (+0.159, 95% CI +0.097 to +0.221; database-level CI +0.098 to +0.234), with PASS accuracy 95.7% against a 92.6% base rate. The frozen rule was not the best agreement-based rule on Spider, and the harness and prompts were rebuilt after registration. Added after review: a GPT-5.1 judge asked for P(correct), averaged with agreement, ranked higher in every evaluation (0.837 on Spider), but it grades its own model's answers and its gain shrinks under execution-match labels; one answer per question from a second model family raised agreement's AUROC from 0.749 to 0.800 on Spider, a gain that held under both label definitions. Labels are model-judged; a ten-answer human pilot found no disagreement, and a full audit has not been done.

### Paper [309]
Title: Terminal-RSI: Recursive Self-Improving Terminal Agents via Recursive Self-Rewrite and Policy-Refine
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22192
Authors: Junyao Yang, Zongxia Li, Yucheng Shi, Zhongzhi Li, Ruhan Wang, Ruiwen Zhou, Haitao Mi, Shuicheng Yan, Leowei Liang
Abstract: Converting verifier outcomes into useful policy updates and partial progress into complete task resolution is crucial for terminal agents. We present Terminal-RSI, which alternates Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR) of verified trajectories with critic-inheriting Recursive Policy-Refine (RPR). We formulate Turn-Boundary Skip-Observation GAE (TB-GAE) to retain token-specific baselines while applying decay across interaction turns. Family Dense Reward (FDR) calibrates semantic progress across task families; Success-Prioritized Reward (SPR) separates strict success from bounded partial credit. A Warm-up Critic Model, recursive critic handoff, and stage-specific learning rates coordinate the two refinement phases. The five-stage sequence from Qwen3.8-27B base model through RSR-1, RPR-1, RSR-2, and RPR-2 yields Terminal-RSI-27B: 19.84% on Terminal-Bench 4.0, 86.52% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, 16.95% on Terminal-Bench 3.0, and 79.00% on Terminal-Bench Hard. On LHTB, process reward rises from 0.21 to 0.37 and strict completion from 0/46 to 5/46; SWR100 success doubles from 3.00% to 6.00%. By recursively coupling verified trajectory rewriting with critic-inheriting policy refinement, each retained stage consolidates discovered behaviors and progressively advances the agent from partial local repairs to strict terminal verification.

### Paper [310]
Title: From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24662
Authors: Xike Xie, Yu Li, Weizhong Yang, Amalie Dyda, Shaohua Kevin Zhou
Abstract: Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics.
  We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. Building a PHWM endows three unmet structural gaps in current digital medicine: dynamic health-state modeling, population-level intervention intelligence, and validation, equity, and governance for action-oriented use. We argue that addressing these gaps requires three minimal capabilities: representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries, while highlighting the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine.

### Paper [311]
Title: Model Organisms of Collusion: Corrective Transfer in Untrusted Monitors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30582
Authors: Juan-Pablo Rivera, Ari Spiesberger, Juan J Vazquez, Arun Jose, Axel Ahlqvist, Adeline Kassler, Meru Gopalan, Sandy Tanwisuth, John Hughes
Abstract: AI control protocols aim to get useful work from models that may be misaligned by monitoring their behaviour. One approach uses a second copy of the same model as the monitor. This avoids relying on a weaker reviewer the model could outsmart, but risks collusion: the model, now monitoring itself, may cover up its own misbehaviour. One remedy is to fine-tune the monitor on whatever examples of misbehaviour the control team has caught. We ask whether this training also removes collusion that the control team has not yet found. We fine-tune Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B to install five policy-monitor behaviour pairs, correct the monitor on 300 honest reviews of one caught collusion, and measure collusion on the other four in unseen prompt formats. Averaged over the three seeds of each family, correction reduces held-out collusion from 91% to 14-20% under three of the four methods we compare (individual seeds 10-32%), while a control fine-tuned on the same number of unrelated examples leaves 56-93%. Family-average accuracy on control items changes by at most five points and capability is preserved, apart from a drop in mathematical accuracy on one Llama seed. Under the tested training configuration, methods with similar caught-task accuracy differ in held-out transfer, from 58% to 83% average reduction, so the caught behaviour alone does not reveal how far a correction reaches. Training on a known failure removes it almost entirely (1-2% remaining), yet 14-20% of unknown failures persist, so near-perfect correction of the caught behaviour overstates how much collusion has been removed.

### Paper [312]
Title: Reject, Remember, Retry: Why Multi-Turn Attackers Should Remember Their Mistakes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31937
Authors: Jekaterina Novikova, Yuqing Huang, Jack Ji, Sagarika Suresh, Jithin Pradeep, Lovedeep Gondara
Abstract: Existing multi-turn jailbreak attackers condition on the same conversation history as the target. We introduce $R^3$ (Reject, Remember, Retry), a black-box multi-LLM framework in which the attacker conditions on a compact state rather than the raw transcript. Turns that fail to raise the attack risk score are removed from the target's context but remembered by the attacker, together with the reason for failure. We call this asymmetric memory. Across two benchmarks and six target LLMs, $R^3$ outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 53% in ASR@5 under matched query budgets, and a low-capacity attacker with $R^3$'s state is able to outperform high-capacity attackers without it by up to 34% in ASR@5. Ablations attribute most gains to remembering rejected turns, and the risk score acts as a coarse threshold rather than a progress measure.

### Paper [313]
Title: AgentOrch: How Close Can Global Orchestration Come to Step-by-Step Interaction?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33375
Authors: Zijie Liu, Qi Yang Huang, Jinhao Duan, Yueh Che Lee, Thomas A. Joseph, Zayne Turner, Adam Seligman, Tianlong Chen, Zhaozhuo Xu
Abstract: Enterprise workflow agents typically rely on step-by-step interaction (Step-by-Step): the agent executes an action, observes its result, and invokes a large language model (LLM) again to decide the next step. Global orchestration (Global) instead generates a complete executable workflow in advance and executes it without further LLM calls, substantially reducing inference overhead, but it remains less accurate than Step-by-Step. We study how close Global can come to Step-by-Step and what narrows the remaining gap. We introduce AgentOrch, a benchmark that directly compares global orchestration with step-by-step interaction on 650 enterprise SOPs and 3,220 runtime variants under matched tools, environments, and evaluation. Step-by-Step remains more accurate for every evaluated model, but the gap has shrunk by about 79% from early models to the strongest recent model. We identify three routes to further narrowing this gap. Incremental workflow construction improves Global accuracy by an average of 9.98 percentage points (pp) without runtime execution feedback. Revising complete workflows with execution feedback improves all evaluated models, including 6.13 pp on held-out runtime variants. Targeted training on complete workflows improves accuracy by an average of 12.18 pp across all five trained models. Although a gap remains, global orchestration offers a promising path toward enterprise agents that deliver higher accuracy with fewer LLM calls.

### Paper [314]
Title: LegoSR: A Unified Framework for Self-improving Agent-Guided Scientific Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33465
Authors: Hao Mao, Xu T. Liu, Peng Zhao, Shuai Lu, Yuntian Chen
Abstract: Equation discovery solvers often use different data interfaces, equation representations and evaluation criteria, making their results hard to compare. We present LegoSR, a unified framework for algebraic symbolic regression and differential equation discovery. LegoSR integrates seven solvers through a common equation representation and evaluation process, and supports searches constrained by partially known equations. Researchers can run solvers directly or use LegoSR Agent, which selects solvers and adapts searches using solver knowledge, intermediate results and lessons from past tasks. On noisy ordinary and partial differential equation benchmarks, LegoSR Agent recovers nearly as many equations as the union of seven expert-tuned solvers and outperforms a generic agent with the same model, tools and budget by up to 29 percentage points. Three examples of increasing difficulty show the agent's role growing from running a solver to choosing one and then to building a partially known equation from failed searches.

### Paper [315]
Title: Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829
Authors: Hangyi Zhao (Faculty of Data Science, City University of Macau, Macau, China), Bonan Zhang (Faculty of Data Science, City University of Macau, Macau, China), Fusen Guo (School of Systems and Computing, UNSW Sydney, Canberra, Australia), Jiaheng Wei (RMIT University, Melbourne, Australia), Chao Chen (RMIT University, Melbourne, Australia), Tianqing Zhu (Faculty of Data Science, City University of Macau, Macau, China)
Abstract: Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.

### Paper [316]
Title: MuSID-KD: Multigranularity Knowledge Distillation for Generative Recommendation with Semantic IDs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35218
Authors: Fengshi Zhang, Xiyan Ding, Shuxiao Xie, Wei Zhang
Abstract: Generative recommendation with semantic IDs (SIDs) represents each item as a sequence of discrete tokens and autoregressively predicts the next item's SID. Despite strong recommendation performance, repeated decoding over multiple SID positions incurs substantial inference cost. Knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a powerful generative teacher to a lightweight student, yet conventional objectives do not fully account for multi-token SID generation. We identify two limitations: item-level distribution alignment may not adequately preserve the teacher's ranking of candidate groups sharing SID prefixes, while uniform alignment across SID positions may affect final item ranking differently. We propose MuSID-KD, which combines Hierarchical Candidate Distribution Alignment (HCDA) and Ranking Impact Guided Levelwise Distillation (RIGD). HCDA aggregates item probabilities according to shared SID prefixes and aligns candidate distributions at successive levels, from candidate groups to individual items. RIGD evaluates the reduction in pairwise ranking surrogate loss when one student score contribution is replaced with the teacher's contribution, and allocates supervision accordingly. Experiments on real-world recommendation datasets using three lightweight student architectures demonstrate improved recommendation accuracy and better alignment of prefix marginal distributions and pairwise item rankings, without modifying the student's online inference architecture. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/m7k2-review-83f9/.

### Paper [317]
Title: SpectRAG: Weighted Chunk Graphs for Token-Budgeted Context Selection in Multi-Hop Retrieval-Augmented Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36991
Authors: Zafer Sahinoglu, Abdulazeez Mohammed, Buse Yaren Kazangirler, Cahit Berkay Kazangirler
Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems ground large language models (LLMs) by retrieving external evidence and conditioning generation on the retrieved context. Although dense retrieval is effective for local semantic matching, independently ranked retrieval can return redundant passages, miss cross-chunk dependencies, and provide incomplete evidence for multi-hop reasoning. We propose SpectRAG, a spectral graph-augmented retrieval framework that combines hybrid candidate seeding, weighted chunk graph construction, graph-based expansion, spectral relevance propagation, and token-budgeted context selection. Document chunks are represented by dense semantic embeddings and graph-spectral coordinates derived from a weighted chunk graph whose edges encode semantic similarity, document adjacency, section hierarchy, shared entities, citation links, and similarity between LLM-generated utility questions. At query time, SpectRAG builds a high-recall seed set using exact inner-product search and hybrid relevance signals, expands it over the chunk graph, re-scores candidates using graph proximity and spectral information, and assembles the final context under a fixed token budget. The goal is not merely to retrieve individually similar chunks, but to construct a context that is relevant, diverse, structurally connected, and sufficient for grounded reasoning. Experiments on MultiHopRAG, UltraDomain, and long-document question-answering settings show that SpectRAG improves broad evidence recovery and achieves competitive generation quality against dense, hybrid, HyDE, LightRAG, and NaiveRAG baselines. The results further indicate that graph-spectral structure is particularly useful for inference-oriented and cross-document retrieval, while highlighting trade-offs in early precision, computational cost, and domain-dependent performance.

### Paper [318]
Title: Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461
Authors: Milad Sikaroudi
Abstract: Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.

### Paper [319]
Title: Learning to Reason from Reference Samples Alone: Reinforced Adversarial Learning for Modern LLM Post-Training
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38851
Authors: Albert Bou, Javier Valdes-Aleman, Jon M. Laurent, Samuel G. Rodriques, Andrew D. White, Michaela Hinks, Siddharth Narayanan
Abstract: In many settings where LLM post-training is desirable, high-quality answers are recognizable but difficult to score with explicit rewards. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is limited to tasks where an explicit correctness signal is available. Prior adversarial approaches to training reasoning LLMs either complement verifiable rewards with co-trained critics or require reference reasoning traces. We ask whether a co-trained LLM judge can turn reference answers alone into the sole reward for on-policy post-training of reasoning LLMs. We call this method Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL). We first validate RAL on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation), using the verifiers only for evaluation. With sufficiently capable judges and enough training, RAL matches direct RLVR. We then apply RAL to scientific abstract generation in two settings. In the first setting, RAL guides the generator to produce abstracts in the scientific domain of the reference abstracts, even though the prompt leaves that domain unspecified. In the second setting, we generate abstracts for U.S. National Institutes of Health grant proposals conditioned on the grant title. Human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges find our generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than those of frontier-LLM baselines. These results suggest a path toward post-training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains, where increasingly capable judges infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers.

### Paper [320]
Title: What Breaks When Facts Become Stories? The Distortions Source-Agreement Metrics Miss When Models Retell Real Events
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39500
Authors: Kwangsuk Park, JiWoong Yang, Vaniushkina Kseniia
Abstract: Language models are now widely used to turn recorded facts into media for public consumption, yet evaluation of such output still assumes agreement with the source as its measure of success. In reconstruction, where departing from the source can itself be the goal, this criterion measures the wrong construct: applied as is, it counts intended departures as errors; withheld, it lets real distortion through. We had three model families rewrite 2,000 news articles as summaries and as stories, measured what changed on every axis of the five Ws and how, and asked of each detected distortion whether the assigned perspective warranted it, which set a third of them aside. Time and manner were not lost but replaced, while place and causation lost the expressions that carry them. Accountability distortion behaved otherwise. Barely present in summaries, it rose up to sevenfold in stories, and of those whose direction could be determined, two in three lightened the perpetrator's responsibility. Such distortions survive claim-level fact checking and leave the retelling grammatically cleaner than its source. Where our judges disagreed, three authors relabeling the same cases converged on whether a distortion had occurred but not on whether it was warranted, a boundary contested rather than merely hard to detect. To support work on detecting and reducing them, we release ReTold-2k, a reconstructable corpus with the scoring protocol and per-judge baselines kept unaggregated.(https://github.com/Retold2K/retold2k).

### Paper [321]
Title: MoLRSI: A Recursive Self-Improving AI agent for Molecular Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39714
Authors: Yikun Zhang, Xiwei Cheng, Mingze Yuan, Yu Zhang, Brian Shim, Aarti Krishnan, Daniel Rosen, Kunyang Sun, Yingze Wang, Zhenfeng Deng, Botao Yu, Bingxu Liu, David Liu, Tianyu Liu, Yuanqi Du, Nir Hacohen, James J. Collins, Wengong Jin
Abstract: Molecular discovery requires navigating vast combinatorial search spaces while balancing competing biological objectives. AI agents can automate expert-defined molecular design protocols, but automation alone does not address how to optimize workflows for new tasks or improve their development through experience. Here we introduce MoLRSI, an AI agent that combines systematic optimization of workflows and model architectures with persistent cross-task memory to recursively improve its search policy for molecular discovery. Workflow performance, refinement outcomes, and execution feedback guide subsequent searches, allowing the agent to improve both its workflows and its strategy for developing them. We evaluate MoLRSI in two challenging molecular discovery settings that require balancing competing properties. In antibiotic discovery, MoLRSI developed a screening workflow balancing antibacterial activity, cytotoxicity and structural novelty, achieving an experimental hit rate of 15.9%, compared with 8.7% for a previous expert-led workflow using the same training data and screening library. In de novo peptide-MHC binder design, MoLRSI developed workflows that jointly consider target binding and specificity, producing eight experimentally validated hits against MART-1 and SARS-CoV-1 pMHCs with preferential binding over tested off-targets while Claude Science completely failed. These findings demonstrate how experience-guided workflow optimization can address competing discovery objectives while improving the search strategies used in subsequent campaigns.

### Paper [322]
Title: EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813
Authors: Qinuo Cao, Xin He, Dianxuan Fu, Lizhi Zhang, Zhaoqing Xing, Jiapu Wang, Xin Wang, Na Zou, Yi Chang, Qinggang Zhang
Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions. Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.

### Paper [323]
Title: Algebraic World Model: From Exploration to Proofs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40503
Authors: Guancheng Pan, Qixin Tan, Yang Yuan, Andrew Chi-Chih Yao
Abstract: AI has made remarkable progress in mathematics, solving challenging problems with machine-checkable proofs in Lean, yet typically relying on predefined mathematical definitions and formal specifications. This raises a natural question: can it reason rigorously about an unknown world? We introduce the Algebraic World Model (AWM), a framework that turns interaction with unfamiliar environments into explicit mathematical models supporting formal reasoning. AWM explores the environment and develops executable Python hypotheses, continually testing and revising them against observations. It then structures these hypotheses into typed objects and shared rules, simplifies their structure through category-theory-guided transformations, and formalizes the resulting model in Lean 4 to prove reachability and impossibility. AWM can formally prove that a goal is unreachable, even beyond explored trajectories. On two impossible game levels, it constructs closed, kernel-checked Lean proofs excluding every legal winning sequence in all six runs, while Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, equipped with coding tools, produce none in twelve baseline runs. Four of AWM's six initial bounds are correct, compared with one for Opus 5.5 and none for GPT-6 Astra; given only feedback that a cheaper win exists, AWM revises its rules and constructs new proofs, reaching six of six correct bounds. Moreover, AWM produces interpretable mathematical models whose rules can be inspected and tested step by step. Across all 25 public ARC-AGI-3 games, these models enable Lean to replay the winning trajectories of all 183 levels and generalize beyond observed play, predicting unseen transitions and detecting changes in game mechanics. With Claude Opus 5, AWM clears all levels with a score of 99.9, while its certified winning trajectories use only 43% of the human baseline's actions.

### Paper [324]
Title: Time Will Tell: Harness Time to Denoise Collaborative Filtering
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09181
Authors: Xinyu He, Jose Sepulveda, Mostafa Rahmani, Alyssa Woo, Fei Wang, Hanghang Tong
Abstract: Neural graph collaborative filtering is a powerful tool for recommendation due to its superb capability of capturing the user-item interaction. Despite the substantial progress, the noisy nature of interaction data remains a significant challenge in collaborative filtering, especially for data collected from implicit feedback. Both noisy interactions and interactions crafted by malicious users could greatly degenerate model performance. To purify the input data and enhance model robustness, it is vital to understand how noisy interactions can be identified and rectified. Existing denoising methods focus on mining the correlation between user/item embeddings and noisy interactions, neglecting the temporal dimension for denoising. However, time information bears its natural and unique advantage in revealing noisy edges as we observe in this paper. In this work, we introduce a simple yet effective mechanism for generating time-aware user/item embeddings that capture both global preferences and temporal patterns. Two strategies, reweighting adjacency matrix (Tint-A) and reweighting loss function (Tint-L), are proposed to leverage the embeddings for the task of using \underline{t}ime \underline{in}forma\underline{t}ion to denoise \underline{c}ollaborative \underline{f}iltering (TintCF). Extensive experiments have been conducted on four real-world datasets to demonstrate the efficacy of our methods and illustrate how time information indeed helps identify and rectify noisy edges. Our codes are available at \href{https://github.com/xinyuu-he/TintCF}{https://github.com/xinyuu-he/TintCF}.

### Paper [325]
Title: Integrating AI for Human-Centric Breast Cancer Diagnostics: A Multi-Scale and Multi-View Swin Transformer Framework
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13309
Authors: Farnoush Bayatmakou, Reza Taleei, Milad Amir Toutounchian, Arash Mohammadi
Abstract: Despite advancements in Computer-Aided Diagnosis (CAD) systems, breast cancer remains one of the leading causes of cancer-related deaths among women worldwide. Recent breakthroughs in Artificial Intelligence (AI) have shown significant promise in development of advanced Deep Learning (DL) architectures for breast cancer diagnosis through mammography. In this context, the paper focuses on the integration of AI within a Human-Centric workflow to enhance breast cancer diagnostics. Key challenges are, however, largely overlooked such as reliance on detailed tumor annotations and susceptibility to missing views, particularly during test time. To address these issues, we propose a hybrid, multi-scale and multi-view Swin Transformer-based framework (MSMV-Swin) that enhances diagnostic robustness and accuracy. The proposed MSMV-Swin framework is designed to work as a decision-support tool, helping radiologists analyze multi-view mammograms more effectively. More specifically, the MSMV-Swin framework leverages the Segment Anything Model (SAM) to isolate the breast lobe, reducing background noise and enabling comprehensive feature extraction. The multi-scale nature of the proposed MSMV-Swin framework accounts for tumor-specific regions as well as the spatial characteristics of tissues surrounding the tumor, capturing both localized and contextual information. The integration of contextual and localized data ensures that MSMV-Swin's outputs align with the way radiologists interpret mammograms, fostering better human-AI interaction and trust. A hybrid fusion structure is then designed to ensure robustness against missing views, a common occurrence in clinical practice when only a single mammogram view is available.

### Paper [326]
Title: Beyond Public Access in LLM Pre-Training Data
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00020
Authors: Sruly Rosenblat, Tim O'Reilly, Ilan Strauss
Abstract: Using a legally obtained dataset of 34 copyrighted O'Reilly Media books (13,962 paragraphs), we apply the DE-COP membership inference attack to test whether OpenAI models show recognition of non-public (pay-walled) content they were not licensed to train on. Our main contribution is examining non-public books, which allows us to consider potential access violations and not just training inclusion. Our results suggest that GPT-4o, OpenAI's more recent and capable model in our sample, exhibits patterns consistent with recognition of non-public books, with an AUROC score of 0.82 (95% bootstrapped CI: 0.60-0.96) -- where above 50% is better than random chance. GPT-3.5 Turbo (trained two years prior) and the smaller GPT-4o Mini both score near chance recognition on non-public data (0.54 and 0.56, respectively). Results are robust to independent paraphrasers (Gemma and Qwen) and leave-one-book-out sensitivity analyses. We also test both GPT-4o and GPT-4o mini, with the same October 2023 training cutoff date, to ensure the model is not simply identifying language shifts over time. These findings support disclosure requirements for pre-training data provenance and formal licensing markets for training content. Results remain preliminary given the small book sample, possible contamination of post-cutoff controls, and differences across models in size, architecture, and training composition.

### Paper [327]
Title: Optimizing Anytime Reasoning via Budget Relative Policy Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13438
Authors: Penghui Qi, Zichen Liu, Tianyu Pang, Chao Du, Wee Sun Lee, Min Lin
Abstract: Scaling test-time compute is crucial for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Existing approaches typically employ reinforcement learning (RL) to maximize a verifiable reward obtained at the end of reasoning traces. However, such methods optimize only the final performance under a large and fixed token budget, which hinders efficiency in both training and deployment. In this work, we present a novel framework, AnytimeReasoner, to optimize anytime reasoning performance, which aims to improve token efficiency and the flexibility of reasoning under varying token budget constraints. To achieve this, we truncate the complete thinking process to fit within sampled token budgets from a prior distribution, compelling the model to summarize the optimal answer for each truncated thinking for verification. This introduces verifiable dense rewards into the reasoning process, facilitating more effective credit assignment in RL optimization. We then optimize the thinking and summary policies in a decoupled manner to maximize the cumulative reward. Additionally, we introduce a novel variance reduction technique, Budget Relative Policy Optimization (BRPO), to enhance the robustness and efficiency of the learning process when reinforcing the thinking policy. Empirical results in mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms GRPO across all thinking budgets under various prior distributions, enhancing both training and token efficiency.

### Paper [328]
Title: LLINBO: Trustworthy LLM-in-the-Loop Bayesian Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14756
Authors: Chih-Yu Chang, Milad Azvar, Chinedum Okwudire, Raed Al Kontar
Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sequential decision-making tool widely used for optimizing expensive black-box functions. Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable adaptability in low-data regimes, making them promising tools for black-box optimization by leveraging contextual knowledge to propose high-quality query points. However, LLMs lack explicit surrogate modeling and calibrated uncertainty, and their internal mechanisms remain opaque. This structural opacity can result in unnecessary experiments, which are costly in BO and may prevent the search from approaching the optimum.
