This paper presents an MSDF accelerator for quantized U-Net segmentation with a two-stage grouped processing element supporting signed INT8 operands and in-stream bias accumulation. Four runtime mechanisms operate directly on the output digit stream: exact early negative detection (END) in ReLU layers, exact sign-only decision making in the segmentation head, calibrated low-order-digit skipping, and calibrated pruning. The two approximate mechanisms are selected offline under an accuracy constraint, while execution requires only lightweight control and does not modify the stored weights. On a residual U-Net trained with nnU-Net for BraTS, the proposed mechanisms reduce digit cycles by 38.38\% while achieving a mean Dice score of 80.58\% on 73 held-out cases, compared with 81.20\% for the floating-point model; the exact mechanisms alone reduce cycles by 18.79\% without altering the quantized output. Synthesized in 45~nm, the processing element operates at 500~MHz, occupies 0.858~mm$^2$, and consumes 0.726~mJ per $192\times192$ patch under switching-activity-annotated power analysis. A projected eight-output accelerator with shared activation delivery achieves 16.6~ms latency and 1.67~mJ per patch.

### Paper [377]
Title: Self-improving data synthesis can scale embedding-model adaptation across domains and tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14730
Authors: Zucan Lyu, Rui Tang, Jianlyu Chen, Peisheng Jin, Defu Lian
Abstract: AI agents require specialised retrieval across domains and tasks, but adapting embedding models is constrained by costly manual training-data design. Here we introduce SYNTRA, a self-improving agentic data synthesis framework that turns a few retrieval examples into reusable synthesis instructions. Given an unlabelled corpus and a few relevant query-document pairs, SYNTRA infers implicit information needs and relevance criteria, then uses feedback on trial outputs to refine instructions for query generation and relevance assessment. Experiments across 24 datasets spanning 15 domains demonstrate that the same synthesis workflow produces effective training data, benefiting even strong embedding models with and without existing labelled training sets. Self-improvement strengthens this supervision on all six evaluated tasks, yielding better retrieval than human calibration on four. The training value persists at larger scales: on MS MARCO, retrieval gains continue across a 32-fold range of synthetic training-data sizes. SYNTRA thus enables scalable embedding-model adaptation with minimal human intervention.

### Paper [378]
Title: PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390
Authors: Han Shen, Qifu Tyler Sun, Fanyang Meng, Ye Wang, Yongsheng Liang
Abstract: Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. However, when task contexts are passed across agents, shared context that has already been processed by upstream agents often needs to be prefilled again, resulting in substantial redundant computation. Reusing an already prefilled key--value (KV) cache can avoid this recomputation, but requires the reusable KV state to be transferred across the network before the receiving agent can begin generation. Under strict time-to-first-token (TTFT) deadlines and unreliable edge links, delivering the complete KV cache may be infeasible, making it necessary to prioritize and protect the most valuable KV information within a limited transmission budget. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that jointly exploits model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. These results demonstrate the importance of jointly optimizing KV-cache utility and deadline-aware transport reliability when reusable LLM state must be handed off across unreliable edge networks.

### Paper [379]
Title: DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561
Authors: Sergio Burdisso, Esa\'u Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia S\'anchez-Cort\'es, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, Petr Motlicek
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.

### Paper [380]
Title: PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005
Authors: Lei Tian, Chunzheng Zhu, Kai Wu, Yifan Zhang, Haihua Yang
Abstract: Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.

### Paper [381]
Title: A Unified Attack Framework against Statistical Generative Watermarks for Large Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27653
Authors: Romina Omidi, Nima Naderloui, Yuan Hong, Binghui Wang
Abstract: Watermarking has emerged as a key tool for identifying text generated by large language models (LLMs), with current schemes embedding statistical signals into the generation process to enable later detection. In this work, we identify a shared weakness across a broad class of popular LLM watermarking methods, including KGW (and its variants), GumbelSoft, DipMark, ITS, and SynthID. We show that these schemes exhibit a fundamental link between their detection scores and the token probability shifts they introduce during generation. This link, which we call score-ratio alignment, allows an attacker with only black-box query access to the model--and no knowledge of secret keys or model weights--to produce text that evades detection while remaining fluent and natural. We prove that the resulting attack is optimal across all covered watermark families, and show that the weakness is intrinsic: any watermark designed to maximize statistical detection power necessarily induces the very structure that attackers can exploit. We evaluate our attack on seven popular generative LLM watermarking schemes, three LLMs, and tasks, which reduce detection rates to at most 0.05 across all settings while preserving text quality. Our findings show that current watermarking methods can be reliably defeated under realistic attack conditions, highlighting the need for fundamentally different watermarking designs that decouple detection scores from token probability shifts.

### Paper [382]
Title: Superintelligent Software Engineering: Governance That Scales with Capability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31306
Authors: Mohammad Mehdi Gholinejad
Abstract: Software agents are being trained to exceed human engineers; we argue that capability alone does not produce better software engineering. We define superintelligent software engineering as the discipline of building and evolving software with agents whose capability exceeds that of human engineers, in which governance (gates, contracts, decisions and decay control) scales with capability. The argument rests on three claims. First, correctness in a project rests on decisions and contracts that the code does not carry. Second, knowledge decays. Third, once an agent outpaces its reviewers, reading catches a shrinking share of the changes built on stale knowledge. A simple model shows that, past a fixed review capacity, escaping changes grow linearly with throughput while the share that reading catches falls toward zero; governance can lower the slope, capability alone cannot. An exploratory snapshot of 452 of the most-starred public GitHub repositories finds a root-level or .github agent context file in 48.67% (95% confidence interval 44.1 to 53.3); in the median file that names repository paths, 9.5% to 22% of those paths do not resolve, depending on how a reference is read. We then present OSTEON, a discipline for the regime, archived on Zenodo under DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23436020 as a plugin for Claude Code. In OSTEON, each project gets a governed knowledge repository of decisions and contracts, every change is checked mechanically against it, and the knowledge itself is checked for decay at every session start; planning is gated, and sessions record what they learn without writing rules. We report no measured effect of OSTEON, mark claims about its mechanisms as opinion and the author's practice heuristics as design rationale, and state the questions that would falsify the thesis.

### Paper [383]
Title: SOPO: Socratic Guided Policy Optimization for Span-Level Hallucination Detection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32212
Authors: Jiawei Dong, Zilong Bai, Pengtian zhu, Peng Zhou
Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained widespread adoption in various natural language processing tasks, but suffer from hallucination issues where they generate unfaithful or inconsistent content. While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown promising applications in reasoning tasks, its application to span-level hallucination detection is challenged by localization ambiguity, reward sparsity on hard examples, and uncontrolled length inflation. To address these issues, we introduce Socratic Guided Policy Optimization (SOPO), a multi-turn RL framework for robust hallucination detection. The key innovation of SOPO is a Socratic feedback mechanism, where a teacher model guides the reasoning trajectories during rollout by providing indirect hints inferred from the ground truth. This teacher-guided approach enables the model to generate more effective reasoning trajectories for hard examples, thus alleviating reward sparsity. Furthermore, we incorporate Turn-based Advantage Scaling to penalize verbosity bias, encouraging the model to develop efficient and autonomous reasoning capabilities. Empirical results on RAGTruth demonstrate that SOPO (4B) achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing leading proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5) and standard RL baselines, while obtaining higher reported mean scores than GRPO on four out-of-domain benchmarks, with mixed evidence against CAPO. We release our training and evaluation code alongside the 1.7B and 4B SOPO models at https://github.com/patsnap/SOPO.

### Paper [384]
Title: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509
Authors: Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao, Anh Nguyen Hoang, Ziqi Zhang, Weidong Wang, Zhiheng Lyu, Lingming Zhang
Abstract: LLM agents have been increasingly adopted for security-focused code review, but existing benchmarks mostly derive the reviewed changes from their later fixes instead of the real-world bug-introducing commits. In this paper, we present SecPRBench, the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating agentic security review on validated real-world code changes. More specifically, we have built an automated agentic pipeline to autonomously harvest, build, and validate massive real-world bug-introducing commits for popular software systems. To ensure precise evaluation, we also adopt LLM-as-a-judge to credit the findings matching ground-truth vulnerabilities. To date, we have collected 265 tasks from nine critical systems, including Firefox, V8, and OpenSSL with millions of lines of code. Across eight frontier models, recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%, with Grok 4.6 and GPT-6 sol ranking at the top. Our study also shows that most misses are not simply oversights: in 68% of them, the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism. Security-focused harnesses also behave differently: OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost.

### Paper [385]
Title: Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33968
Authors: Pedro Dall'Antonia, Juan Belieni, Ana Carolina Erthal, Saullo H. G. de Oliveira, Eliezer de Souza da Silva, Diego Mesquita
Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are trained to sample objects from compositional spaces with probability proportional to a reward function, but ineffective exploration can cause learned samplers to concentrate on a few high-reward regions. An intuitive remedy is to train multiple GFlowNets independently in parallel and combine their samples. However, this does not prevent multiple models from collapsing onto the same high-reward areas, leaving large regions of the state space unexplored. With this in mind, we propose Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets (HCG), an approach that dynamically partitions the search space to coordinate discovery without relying on hand-crafted boundaries. HCG imposes an ordering in which the highest-ranked model may explore the entire space, while lower-ranked models are progressively pushed toward regions not covered by higher-ranked ones. We provide a theoretical characterization of how specific learning objectives can shift probability mass away from designated areas, instantiating this mechanism within our architecture. A shared classifier coordinates the hierarchy by partitioning the search space, keeping the GFlowNets independently parameterized and enabling asynchronous training. Furthermore, we derive a normalizer-weighted aggregation rule for the ensemble and prove that it recovers the target reward-proportional distribution under the stated assumptions. Finally, experiments across multimodal distributions, sequence generation, combinatorial optimization, and biological sequence design show that HCG improves mode coverage and target approximation, translating increases in the number of coordinated samplers into broader, complementary exploration.

### Paper [386]
Title: Adapting LLM-SC to Living Sacred Heritage: Domain-Specific Extraction, Semantic Networks and Human Evaluation on Pashupatinath Temple Reviews
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35685
Authors: Pujan Pant, Sagar Newpane, Hari Sitaula, Manish Sakhakarmy
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) pipelines can turn visitor reviews into structured data, but generic schemes may miss rituals, sacred spaces, cultural restrictions and emotionally mixed experiences at living sacred heritage sites. We adapted the LLM-Social Computing (LLM-SC) framework to Pashupatinath Temple, Nepal, by specifying a 12-type entity taxonomy (four original LLM-SC types plus eight sacred-heritage types), four relation types and nine aspect-sentiment dimensions in a GPT-4o extraction prompt, which was applied to a fixed random sample of 2,500 English-language Tripadvisor reviews from a processed corpus of 3,939. The exports contain 11,286 entity, 6,232 aspect-sentiment and 5,590 relation records, covering 2,485, 2,487 and 2,433 reviews, respectively; the eight added sacred-heritage types account for 64.8% of entity records. Against an adjudicated two-annotator reference for 200 reviews, exact normalized surface-and-type matching gave provisional entity scores of 41.0% precision, 21.8% recall and 28.4% micro-F1. Aspect-presence F1 was 53.6% and end-to-end aspect-plus-polarity F1 was 42.6%. Human adjudication supported 153 of 194 first-listed relation candidates (one per review; 78.9%), a candidate-level rate that does not estimate relation recall or whole-graph precision. The contribution is a culturally grounded, domain-specific adaptation with component-wise human evaluation and explicit reproducibility and generalization limitations.

### Paper [387]
Title: I-HITL: Inalienable Human-in-the-Loop
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35835
Authors: Samuel Breckenridge, Dani Vilardell, Wenhao Wang, Fan Zhang, Ari Juels
Abstract: Human oversight is a pillar of AI safety. It is often exercised by a human in the loop (HITL) who approves critical actions taken by an AI agent. AI agents' increasing ability to bypass guardrails, though, raises the question of how to enforce HITL robustly.
  We introduce the concept of inalienable human-in-the-loop (I-HITL). An I-HITL protocol ensures that an agent can initiate an action only with explicit human approval, even when the human interacts with a system (e.g., a local agentic harness) compromised by the agent. A strong I-HITL protocol additionally ensures such approval even when an adversarial human attempts to delegate approvals to an agent.
  To motivate I-HITL, we report on experiments with a popular agentic wallet platform in which raising an agent's spending cap requires HITL approval via emailed one-time passcodes (OTPs). Given access to the user's email and a task blocked by the cap, we find that ten different frontier models will often autonomously raise the cap, harvesting OTPs to bypass a HITL guardrail and surfacing other vulnerabilities.
  We formally define I-HITL using the concept of communication topologies and prove necessary and sufficient conditions for its realization. We show that an I-HITL protocol requires a trusted entity - a trusted computing device (or equivalent) - and characterize which such devices can and cannot realize an I-HITL protocol. We present a prototype I-HITL system called Countersign that can plug into and strengthen existing HITL systems using off-the-shelf consumer mobile devices.

### Paper [388]
Title: When Is a Relation Worth Trusting? Manipulation Costs, Exposure, and the Trust Trap in Multi-Relational Graph Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37239
Authors: Vincess Dongmo, Astride Melvin Fokam Ninyim
Abstract: Multi-relational graph detectors learn from clean data how much to trust each relation, but an adversary pays a different price to manipulate each relation and each action; taking such costs as given, we ask when using a relation is worse than ignoring it. Our first main result proves that degree gating strictly beats every selection or reweighting of a relation when part of the population is isolated on it and the attacker cannot afford to open the gate, and that this window is sharp; on real graphs, validated gating gains exactly on the relations that meet these conditions and nowhere else. Our second shows that a per-node exposure bound, which extends to message passing, tells a platform which relation to harden: exposure per unit cost, computed without any attack, picks the relation whose hardening raises the exact trap threshold most in 15 of 16 cases, against at most 7 for informativeness, cheapness, or an analysis with equal edit costs. Supporting tools give exact worst cases for linear relational models, certified brackets for R-GCN and tree ensembles, and a finite-sample guarantee for adversarial validation, under which clean-trained graph detectors fall below their graph-free counterparts in 58 of 90 settings on six graphs. A selection protocol built on these results is never significantly worse than ignoring the graph; with at most four relations it mostly abstains, as validation alone would, and its cost-aware proposals help when relations are many, so far shown on synthetic graphs. Without cost information, the safe choice is to abstain.

### Paper [389]
Title: DeFT-RAG: Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826
Authors: Dingyi Kang, Bingzhe Li
Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model (LLM) answer questions by retrieving relevant passages from a large corpus. However, RAG struggles with multi-hop questions, which require chaining facts spread across several documents through intermediate entities that the question never mentions. Graph-based RAG methods retrieve once from the entities a question names and so rarely recover the full evidence chain. Iterative RAG methods recover it step by step, but their accuracy is limited by the many tasks they ask the LLM to perform, such as planning the sub-questions, deciding their dependencies, rewriting them as answers arrive, and keeping track of what is known. We propose DeFT-RAG, a RAG framework built on three components. First, DeFT-RAG generates one self-contained sub-question at a time from the original question and the sub-questions answered so far with their answers (QA pairs). Each sub-question names every entity it asks about, which removes the need to plan, track dependencies or rewrite, and lets the LLM focus on a single fine-grained task at a time. Second, DeFT-RAG moves the remaining LLM work outside the LLM: rule-based updates to a QA table that keeps track of what is known, and yes/no decisions made by a fine-tuned lightweight decision model. Third, DeFT-RAG caches the QA pairs and reuses an answer when a later question asks a sub-question with the same meaning. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, DeFT-RAG achieves the best accuracy among thirteen baselines with both gpt-4o-mini and Qwen3.6 as the backbone, while giving the LLM fewer input tokens than any other iterative method. With a QA cache shared across 1,000 MuSiQue questions, 23.1% of sub-questions are answered from the cache, which reduces LLM calls by 12.9% without lowering accuracy.

### Paper [390]
Title: Making Sense of Audience Change: Causal Attribution and Boundary Management on Xiaohongshu
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38026
Authors: Zhiyi (Erin),  Zhou, Kyrie Zhou
Abstract: Recommendation-driven platforms can reshape not only what users see, but also who becomes visible and how community boundaries are negotiated. We examine Xiaohongshu discussions surrounding a perceived World Cup-related audience influx to understand how users attributed community change and how those explanations related to boundary-management strategies. We analyzed 262 comments from eight high-engagement threads using researcher-led thematic analysis, structured coding, and permutation-based association analysis. Users described personalization as both social separation and a boundary that could fail, interpreted platform growth through audience acquisition and commercialization, and contested whether gender should define community membership. Among 70 comments with explicit attributions and strategies, attribution and strategy were strongly associated (Cramer's V = .583): algorithmic attributions concentrated in technical boundary management, while social-actor attributions more often aligned with social enforcement or normative socialization. These findings connect causal sensemaking with boundary management and show how audience restructuring can create governance consequences on recommendation-driven platforms.

### Paper [391]
Title: Turing LLM Post-Training: Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39876
Authors: Yue He, Jiahui Hu, Shaman Tang, Hua Zhou, Qiman Wu, Menglin Li, Yizhao Wang, Yuheng Zhang, Da Zhu, Zhou Wang, Anhua Liu, Hanlin Chen, Mingyuan Wang, Rui Xin, Yuhua Wei, Shuang Su, Siqi Liu, Kai Yang, Weichao Huang, Hang Zhang, Honggou Yang, Xianming Liu
Abstract: We study post-training for dynamically routed mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, focusing on integrating independently optimized specialists while keeping consolidation efficient. Our approach combines calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD), which matches each selected teacher on a local Top-$K$ support with teacher-wise loss normalization and clipping, with an execution path that uses post-capacity bounded-width dispatch, final-route replay, and asynchronous rollout--training overlap. On Turing-20B-A2B, the selected model achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16\% of the measured specialist gain in every domain. At matched $K=32$, the full calibration objective scores 0.9962, compared with 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping. At a matched 8K response cap, the model exceeds both shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on all four reasoning-enabled mathematics and code benchmarks (unweighted mean: 85.92, versus 80.33 for Qwen3-8B and 74.21 for SFT), and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader evaluation metrics. In a PPU-based profile, the combined execution configuration reduces consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds, an observed 2.86$\times$ speedup over serial execution. These results demonstrate strong specialist integration alongside more efficient consolidation, while reflecting the complete training and execution framework rather than any single component.

### Paper [392]
Title: NECA: Neighbor Event Curve Augmentation for Multi-Horizon ICU Deterioration Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40420
Authors: Chenyang Wang, Theresa Jiawei Tang, Xinran Liu, Xinyu Lu, Xulei Yang
Abstract: Early prediction of ICU organ dysfunction events supports timely clinical monitoring and intervention, but estimating short-term organ-specific risk from early ICU records remains challenging due to irregular measurements, heterogeneous clinical variables, and the need to predict risk across multiple future horizons. Existing supervised models usually treat each patient as an isolated sample and do not explicitly use the observed event timing of similar historical patients. We propose Neighbor Event-Curve Augmentation (NECA), a model-agnostic method that retrieves training-set patients with similar early-window profiles and converts their subsequent organ dysfunction event onset times into cumulative event-curve features over 6h, 12h, 24h, and 42h. These features are concatenated with the original patient representation and used by standard downstream predictors. On PhysioNet 2012 and a 4,000-stay MIMIC-IV subset, NECA improves average PR-AUC and ROC-AUC across four organ dysfunction event tasks and seven supervised learners. The largest gain appears for neurological dysfunction events, where task-level average PR-AUC increases from 0.461 to 0.557 on PhysioNet 2012. Ablations show that clinically meaningful retrieval and horizon-specific neighbor risk drive most of the improvement, while the full cumulative curve adds task-dependent temporal context. These results support similar-patient event timing as a structured temporal risk signal for ICU organ dysfunction event prediction. The source code will be publicly released upon acceptance of this paper.
